## A.20 Executing Entity information
The Executing Entity is the same as the Accredited Entity, namely the Development Bank of South Africa (DBSA). DBSA will manage the GCF project.

The DBSA will work closely with the National Water Partnerships Programme (NWPP) custodian, the South African national Department of Water and Fishery (DWF), which acts as national water sector lead, will ensure ownership of the NWPP. The National Designated Authority (NDA), namely the Department of Forestry, Fisheries and the Environment (DFFE). The NWPP is a report to the NDA that supports it in executing its reporting obligations and commitments as National Designated Authority.

## A.21 Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
### Climate change problem
South Africa is a water-scarce country, and the sustainable provision of water is amongst its most significant challenges. The country is located within southern Africa's 'drought belt' and, according to the World Bank, is the fifth most water-scarce country in sub-Saharan Africa. Since the 1960s, South Africa has already experienced a rise in average annual temperatures country-wide by 1.5°C. Temperature


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6 OF 158

| A |
| --- |

has increased more markedly across and inland areas of the country, with records showing that both maximum and minimum daily temperatures have risen, in all seasons. Rainfall trends display less clarity than temperature, with significant inter-annual variability. There are also considerable geographic variations in rainfall patterns. The number of days with rain has decreased significantly during the early rainfall season. Observations point to significant decreases in the number of rain days across the country and a tendency towards a decrease in the duration of wet spells. There is a tendency towards an increase in the intensity of rainfall events, coupled with prolonged dry spells.

While mean annual precipitation being only 60% of the global average and with this being spatially and temporally variable, there are significant disparities between urban and rural areas as well as inter-provincial disparities. This is exacerbated by major urban centres and growth nodes not being in alignment with water resource availability. These nodes, such as Cape Town, Port Elizabeth, and Johannesburg (including the surrounding metropolitan areas), have experienced severe water stress due to water transfers. These transfers have enabled the continued socio-economic development of the country, however, they have also led to increased competition for water resources between regions dependent on water resources within other catchments in other parts of the country and the associated climate risks. In 2017-19 due to the "Day Zero" drought induced by climate change, the City of Cape Town experienced a severe water crisis. The city's population has increased from 3 million to over 4 million requiring over a period of two years creating a dual shock on public health and on socio-economic growth. This is an example of how climate risks can be exacerbated by other factors such as urbanisation, a series of sub-programmes, including the proposed national Water Resource Programme (WRP) presented here for consideration.

Access to World Resources Institute's (WRI) water risk index, at the country level South Africa faces "extremely high" baseline physical water risk. Many parts of South Africa have reached or are fast approaching the point at which all viable freshwater resources are fully utilised. According to the National Water Risk Index, South Africa is in the top 10 most vulnerable countries in terms of climate change vulnerability and is in the top five most vulnerable countries in terms of water stress. It is also amongst the top half of countries that have suffered the most climate-related heritage losses in the last two decades." In terms of baseline water stress, South Africa falls within the top quarter (25%) of all countries globally. The country's vulnerability to climate change is further exacerbated by parts of South Africa display some of the highest baseline water risk levels in the African continent and amongst the highest in the world.

The likelihood of annual severe drought is projected to increase by 39% in the 2050s compared to 1986-2005, under a high emissions future scenario (RCP8.5). Temperatures have been rising since the mid-1970s and are expected to continue to rise at an increasing rate. The projected increased evaporation will cause an overall decrease in water availability in large parts of an already water-stressed country. South Africa is currently experiencing a severe drought, which has been rated by CSIRO in Australia and CSIR in South Africa as a preferred model due to its finer resolution at a local scale. This model indicates that the current drought conditions are likely to continue under current conditions of drought, due to the drastic increase in maximum temperatures and very hot days." In the 2044–2064 period, projected change in the drought tendencies (i.e., number of cases exceeding near-term historical droughts) is expected to be more severe than for the 1985-2005 period. Under RCP scenarios (RCP 8.5) will increase in frequency and duration, at a 95% statistical significance level." But setting end 2044 or even 2030 is not an effective decision window and adaptive capacity needs to be built now and strengthened progressively as the impacts of climate change further worsen.

# South Africa has submitted its first adaptation communication as a component of its Nationally Determined Contribution (A-NDC) in line with the Paris Agreement.

The adaptation communication provides detailed information on South Africa's planned contribution to the global adaptation goal during the period from 2015-2030. It outlines the country’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP), details of planned adaptation actions, and the national vulnerability assessment. The plan notes that water stress is one of the key vulnerabilities in South Africa.

## Proposed Intervention

Through the NWRS, South Africa will meet its 2018 National Water and Sanitation Masterplan (NWMSMP). The NWMSMP has identified a range of interventions to address current and future water challenges which are projected as being a 17% deficit by the year 2030. These interventions include decreasing water demand and water losses through non-revenue water, as well as other investments to supplement the water supply infrastructure. The WRP is one such intervention that will be funded under this proposal.

The commitment of US$253 million of catalytic capital to support the establishment of a national WRP and to provide technical assistance for its implementation is proposed in this funding request. The WRP will address climate change and security at local and regional scales, where climate vulnerability is the driver. Broadly speaking, the WRP will be implemented through three main components: (i) establishing a programmatic approach to the development and implementation of water reuse infrastructure through support to municipalities to identify, prepare and structure water reuse projects; (ii) establishing a blended finance structure that will provide access to concessional funding to reduce the cost of borrowing for individual water reuse projects. The investment scale of the WRP and the design of the Blended Finance structure results in a New Asset Class (this is described in detail below). This asset class will allow for greater private sector participation from the private sector in this newly created asset class would, over time, allow for private sector support to be sustainable without continued concessional funding. The WRP will also provide a programmatic approach to address climate impacts upon socio-economic aspects and local livelihoods. The programmatic support to ensure that water reuse projects are implemented at scale will result in the creation of resilient communities at these local levels, but also the use of catalytic GCF funds will support in establishing a paradigm shift in the structuring of blended finance options and the creation of a new asset class.

## Climate Adaptation Benefits

By providing a programmatic response that recognises the importance of water reuse in improving South Africa’s water security and climate resilience, the WRP will provide a Government-wide response that involves institutions across the water value chain and will support in providing financial resources as well as technical assistance to municipalities. The WRP will also provide a framework for addressing climate impacts upon socio-economic aspects and local livelihoods. The programmatic support to ensure that water reuse projects are implemented at scale will result in the creation of resilient communities at these local levels, but also the use of catalytic GCF funds will support in establishing a paradigm shift in the structuring of blended finance options and the creation of a new asset class.

class. The indicative pipeline of 10 Advanced Treatment Plants would result in an additional 440M/day of water.

Water Reuse as a new asset class is in line with the GCF result area Adaptation Results Area 2 (ARA2): Health, well-being, food and water security in that it reduces pressure on water supply; and supports efforts to improve water service standards. Water reuse also contributes to the development of infrastructure within urban areas. It also supports paradigm shift development pathway: "Strengthening integrated water resources management" by providing a new segment for renewable water sources of the upcoming GCF Water Security Sector Guide.

Additionally, due to the nature of water reuse projects this programme will 8602 sector ARA1: Most vulnerable people and communities (MVP) in infrastructure and built environment.

The proposed Programme expects to directly increase the resilience to climate change of 3,424,737 beneficiaries (based upon an initial pipeline of 10 projects) and also a additional 3,877,805 indirect beneficiaries (including a potential addition 17 projects).

The following co-benefits could have been identified:

- Co-benefit 1: Environmental. Through the implementation of the WRP there will be improvement in the quality of rivers and groundwater with there being a reduction in the discharge from wastewater treatment works, with these waters being redirected to water reuse plants. Also, with the reduction in the volume of effluent discharged into rivers and groundwaters, there is likely to be an increase in fish stocks.

- Co-benefit 2: Economic. With municipalities having the mandate to drive local economic development, there will be a positive impact on the creation and expansion of economic activities including local agriculture and industry, as well as other businesses.

- Co-benefit 3: Gender. In many communities women and girl children are often responsible for the collection of water from distant sources. Water reuse can have profound impacts on the availability of supply and the improvement of water service standards.

# PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

South Africa is ranked amongst the top half of most vulnerable countries in terms of climate change vulnerability and in recent years has demonstrated change vulnerability. Under the 2021 Notre Dame – Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) Index (reflecting 2019 data), South Africa’s overall ranking is 54 out of 183 countries, with a score of 67.2. It is ranked in the top half of countries that have lost the most climate change-related historic losses in the last two decades, by the Germanwatch index. In terms of becoming more vulnerable to water stress and scarcity, South Africa ranks amongst the top 10 water-stressed nations. According to one global multi-variate index of water risk, parts of South Africa display some of the highest levels of water risk globally. The country is also ranked in the top half of countries that have lost the most climate change-related historic losses in the last two decades, by the Germanwatch Index.

While there are currently no significant spatial and temporal variations of water availability in South Africa (or indeed anywhere), all the available climate science information creates a consistent narrative that water scarcity for South Africa will increase due to climate change. Demographic changes and increased demand for water will also contribute to this trend, while economic development will compound the problem, but there can be little doubt that climate change is likely to be a significant driver.

This analysis brings together a number of different climate information sources (from the IPCC, recent peer reviewed literature, and other sources) to provide an overview of projected future climate conditions. While the different sources are in broad agreement, any differences in projected climate conditions are highlighted and explained where possible. The following sections present the key findings from these sources.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 126 OF 158

## B

The model ensemble has been shown to outperform individual models and captured spatial trends across climatic zones, for Africa and the Arabian Peninsula**. In a study aimed specifically at understanding future rainfall patterns in southern Africa, Sian et al. (2021)** compared the historical data of 23 CMIP GCMs with observed precipitation records from South Africa. The model ensemble was found to capture interannual precipitation variation, seasonal intra-annual variability and trends. On the basis of the best performing models, the ensemble is expected to perform well for future projections under all SPSs (SSP4.5 and they note that it is imperative to properly manage water resources through sound planning).

The IPCC report concludes with high confidence (AR6 WGI Technical Summary TS 4.3.2) that with 2°C of global warming above pre-industrial levels, the frequency and intensity of extreme precipitation events will increase in many regions by mid-century. The report also places high confidence in a projected decrease in total precipitation for southwestern Africa regions (TS 4.3.2.1). The impacts of these physical changes are summarized in the Working Group II report on Impacts, Adaptation and Vulnerability.

Since GCMs cannot simulate precipitation well over areas of fast-changing topography or high elevation, when examining future rainfall projections at a sub-national scale, finer resolution Regional Climate Models (RCPMs) are required. The RCPM outputs from the CORDEX-Africa initiative (CORDEX Africa Working Group and WMO gives access to future projections from the Coordinated Regional Climate Downscaling Experiment (CREX) project. These models have been evaluated against observed precipitation characteristics of the CORDEX-Africa models when compared with rain gauges and satellite observations was carried out by Karypidou et al. (2022)** who concluded that the CORDEX-Africa RCMs provide a more realistic representation of the spatial distribution of rainfall than the GCMs. The best agreement with observed indices was achieved using the multi-model ensemble average, which is considered to be the best consensus information for climate impact assessments over southern Africa. The best agreement with observations was found in the 2050s and 2100s time periods.

Consensus projections are now available from the sixth Coupled Model Intercomparison Project (CMIP6), with the Shared Socio-economic Pathways (SSP) scenarios consistent with the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change’s (IPCC) six assessment report. In all future time periods, under all scenarios except the SSP1-2.6 and SSP5-8.5, there is a projected increase in mean annual temperature due to climate change. It is expected that under a business-as-usual future, by mid-century the South African coast will be 3°C warmer than the interior as much as +4 °C. Future projections of precipitation for the period 2020-2039, under all SPSs (barring the most optimistic and unrealistic), indicate there is a decrease in total annual average rainfall volume.

When the WFP-toolkit is used for screening, it will consider a range of scenarios with the actual geographical focus (full catchment area) of the proposed sub-projects.

Over the past four decades, South Africa has experienced more than 60 hydro-meteorological hazards (droughts, floods, heatwaves, droughts and extreme temperatures) that have resulted in the death of an estimated 1,692 people and affected more than 21 million people. The intensity and frequency of hydro-meteorological hazards have increased in South Africa the past four decades and will likely continue to increase due to climate change and human activities contributing to climate change.

The Project Evaluation and Screening Process also draws on information from the online national “Green Book” (https://www.greenbook.org.za/about-the-green-book.html). The Green Book was co-developed by the South African CSIR and the International Development Research Centre (IDRC) and was developed between 2013-2015. It is a comprehensive, multi-disciplinary assessment of water resources in South Africa and co-developed this project with universities, government departments, NGOs and other peer groups.

The Green Book is understood nationally to be a reliable platform for understanding climate vulnerability at both the national and sub-national levels. The Green Book provides an overview of the country’s water resources based on a detailed technical report, including a specific analysis for surface water which gives a supply water climate risk map for South Africa. This map was developed using a hydrological model (HMS) that is driven by data based on a 50km resolution RCM, developed by the Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial Research Organisation (CSIRO) in collaboration with CSIR and WMO, using the regional climate models RCP4.5 and RCP8.5. The model suite’s capability to simulate present-day and socio-economic African climate has been demonstrated and well published (e.g., Engelbrecht et al., 2011; Engelbrecht et al., 2013).

The surface water analysis of the Green Book combines population data with climate model data. The vulnerability of individual towns was calculated based on the estimated current demand and supply as represented by the RCMs, and the future demand and supply is calculated for the year 2050 (Engelbrecht et al., 2013). The future vulnerability was calculated by adjusting the water demand for each town proportional to the increase in population growth for both high and medium growth scenarios. There is a complete technical report available on this topic (CUNA, 2014).

Annexes B2 to the FP provides a “Climate Change Risk & Vulnerability Analysis – Water Sector and Select Municipalities (WFP Priority Cities),” which demonstrates that the Green Book municipal vulnerability tool can be used for prioritizing water sector investments. The results of this analysis are presented in Table 7, augmented by the additional information about projections of water use at the local level. This annex presents Table 1, which shows the projected change in mean annual precipitation and evaporation for each municipality selected purely to demonstrate the utility of the software and are not intended to indicate the location of potential projects. The table shows that water stress is driven by significant climate change factors at eight or more of these locations, which is consistent with the information coming from both global and regional climate models.

## Summary of the multiple sources of information above, the IPCC syntheses, global and regional climate models, and the nationally funded portal for climate change vulnerability in South Africa all describe a scenario of increased frequency and intensity of droughts in the future with higher temperatures. Changes in temperature (both means and extremes) will be largely similar across the whole country, so there will be spatial differences but everywhere will be exposed to higher evaporation and evapotranspiration. Rainfall is expected to decrease over most parts of South Africa, particularly in the north-eastern part of the country, with an overall tendency towards dryness for many parts of South Africa.

Thus, the country's already stressed water resources will be put under greater pressure by climate change, which will also increase the risk of droughts. The region warms at nearly twice the rate of the rest of the world, becoming a climate change hotspot in terms of both hot extremes and drying.** Noting the pre-existing vulnerability to water stress, the country is already experiencing the effects of drought as experienced and the widespread water-efficiency measures put into action to combat the extreme water shortage, the increasing risk of depleted precipitation that these results imply would indicate that such efforts will be increasingly difficult.

For many South Africans, this will translate into severe water insecurity and an exacerbation of socio-economic fragility. The interconnectedness of the water supply systems means that this vulnerability will have a knock-on effect on other sectors as well. In 2016, 35% of South Africa's population did not have access to safely managed drinking water services (Indicator 6.1.1). Annual per capita freshwater availability is projected to decrease from the current average annual availability of 5,732 cubic meters a year and even to the Sub-Saharan Africa average of 3,700 cubic meters a year, placing South Africa amongst the 40 countries with the lowest per capita freshwater availability on the planet.

Slow-onset climate hazards like droughts are expected to become far more frequent, *with one in a 100-year drought events* likely to occur multiple times in a century. Model projections for future rainfall in southwestern South Africa show that there is an increasing probability of multi-year droughts occurring between the present day and 2100. These multi-year drought events were responsible for the worst droughts experienced by South Africa since the 1970s, with the most recent being the 2004–2005 drought, which had the same situation with severe water restrictions in place. The Green Book tool using climate model projections (multi-model ensemble) for future time periods consistently points to a higher future for most of South Africa.

## Figure 8: Water change in water supply risk under high growth and climate change scenarios, across all regions when water supply is dependent on local water resources alone (S1) and with interconnected and augmented supply systems (S2)

Based on current demand projections (driven in part by population growth) and without effective intervention, in addition to the water conservation sub-programmes included in the NWPP, the water deficit confronting the country will be exacerbated by climate change, which is expected to reduce the reliability of water sources. At the municipal level, climate change coupled with population growth and increased water demand will result in a significant reduction in water supply capacity for most municipalities, particularly in the key growth nodes of the country: Western Cape (Cape Town and linked secondary towns), Eastern Cape (Nelson Mandela Bay), Gauteng (Johannesburg, Pretoria and Ekurhuleni) and KwaZulu-Natal (Durban). Suboptimal water infrastructure management (including water losses) also contributes to the challenge. It is clear that climate change adaptation must be a key component of any solution to the problem underlying the expected water deficit. Climate change adaptation is thus central to any, and all solutions.

### Country Ownership of the Proposed Programme and Alignment with National Priorities

The Department of Water and Sanitation (DWS) has established the National Water Partnerships Programme and Water Partnerships Office (WPO) in the DBSA. The WPO is a ring-fenced entity housed in DBSA, reporting committee chaired by the DSO. DWS oversees the work of the WPO. Roles of the WPO include:

- Develop standardized national programmes for private sector participation in municipal water and sanitation services.
- Support municipalities to develop their own projects and/or co-finance projects with other partners, without having to "reinvent the wheel" for each partnership.
- Support municipalities to access funding from various sources including DFIs.

The WPO has received seed funding from NDT and DWS and has requested a budget for the WPO. The WPO structure includes a head of office and a programme head for each of the standard programmes, in addition to technical support staff. A project management system is being established and will be filled shortly. In the interim the WPO is being run by existing DBSA officials.

The WPO has identified the following standardized programmes (other programmes, such as Off-grid or other water sanitation programme may be added in future) presented in Table 2:

| Table 2: Programmes to be included under the NWPP |

|---|---|
| 1 | Comprehensive water conservation and water demand management and cost recovery programmes focusing on reducing losses, reducing over-consumption and improving cost recovery. Private sector obtains return on investment from water reuse. |
| 2 | Management Programme in which private sector provides overall management support to W&I function in municipality. The private sector is paid a fee for this service. This programme is funded by savings through efficiency improvements or from increased revenue generation. |

| 4 | Water-to-use Programme focusing on further treatment of municipal wastewater to release it for another use, potentially in agriculture or industry. The private sector obtains return on investment from this use of water: generation of energy and sewage biosolids. |

To prepare for a hotter and drier future with depleting freshwater resources and growing water demand, South Africa developed its National Water and Sanitation Master Plan in 2018. The Master Plan (which built on an earlier National Strategy for Water Re-use) identifies water re-use as one of the central strategies in the country's climate change adaptation strategy.

The National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) provides for a clustered approach that outlines a range of interventions to increase human resilience, economic resilience, environmental and ecological infrastructure resilience. The NCCAS recognises that there are significant social, political, institutional barriers, the institutional challenges and fragmentation in approach, the need for new technologies as well as the key financial constraints that inhibit adaptive response. Implementing water re-use nationwide does pose a number of challenges including: lack of awareness; lack of capacity to implement; lack of funding; responses (Action 1.128) and underscores the need for more water-wise water management practices in urban, industrial and agricultural sectors.

South Africa's updated draft Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) reinforces this commitment further through its Goal: Implementation of the NCCAS interventions for the period 2021-2030; including in line with South Africa’s commitments under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), and the broader global community, that water re-use is amongst his highest climate change adaptation priorities by showcasing the National Water Conservation and Waste Water Management Strategy (NWCWMS) as a key intervention. The NCCAS also aligns with South Africa's most recent Biennial Update Report (BUR). Water re-use is also consistent with South Africa’s National Development Plan (NDP), which aims to improve access to clean water, sanitation and water security targets (such as ensuring all South Africans have access to clean running water in their homes; ensuring that they can enjoy safe drinking water for domestic use and industry; and developing a national water conservation programme to increase the efficiency of water use).

The NCCAS was developed in 2019 and is aligned with the Paris Agreement's Article 7 and the associated aspects of the Paris Rules. The Strategy further outlines nine strategic objectives to which sectoral representatives have committed, including: increasing awareness; improving capacity for implementation; and has informed an updated adaptation communication as a component of its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC). The A-NDC has outlined a number of relevant goals and associated actions as tabulated in Table 3.

Table 3 South Africa's First Adaptation Communication

#### National priorities, strategies and actions

USD $4 – 5 billion required for the implementation of NCCAS adaptation action plan in South Africa. USD $8 million per year over a period 2021 to 2030 for South Africa.

#### Implementation and support action plan for NCCAS adaptation finance to South Africa

The 2018 UNCCC report recommends that developing countries such as South Africa should receive USD $27.63 billion by 2030 to adapt to climate change. The financial sector shall play a pivotal role in mobilizing resources and meeting requirements to meet the NCCAS objectives.

Direct access: Direct access adaptation finance by the private sector.

USD $16 – USD 50 billion by 2030.

Development of climate change adaptation investment projects.

From a climate change adaptation perspective, water re-use is already a proven resilience strategy in South Africa after being successfully deployed as a response measure[4] by the CCT in the fourth month of 2018. The City of Cape Town has been successful in its efforts to reduce the amount of potable (drinking) water used for irrigation and industrial purposes, and would be well-placed to increase reuse for potable and other non-potable uses.

For the cost-effectiveness and sustainability of water re-use as a climate change adaptation measure, it is important that cities in South Africa mainstream such approaches. As a recent study noted, "prior to the drought, seawater desalination and water reuse ranked lower in the CCT's water reconciliation strategies[5]."


---
**Page 22**

only be considered a priority during the drought period when the City experienced severe water shortages. These options should not be considered crisis options, but rather be considered as normal options at all times. At such instances, these projects are highly considered and implemented usually at a high premium. Such projects include the rehabilitation of existing infrastructure.

It is with this intent, i.e. to establish and scale-up water-re-use infrastructure as the norm where there is climate induced water insecurity in municipalities, that South Africa has designed the proposed national water security framework.

It is important to consider the impacts of climate change in the context of current vulnerability as well as future resilience to climate shocks and the potential for adaptation. The Green Book risk assessment (CSIR 2019) was commissioned by the Water Reuse Programme (WRP) and is the first comprehensive municipal water security assessment completed in South Africa to date, being completed in 2019 and being updated regularly. This assessment has shown by looking at climate change impact in conjunction with water supply context (local supply E1 and regional supply E2) and demand context (population growth and economic growth), that the current level of vulnerability is high for most municipalities. By taking a median climate scenario and looking at the adjustments in vulnerability from the current situation to 2050, it was found that the level of vulnerability will increase by between 10% and 40%, with some instances more than this. This particularly key in county economic nodes of Gauteng, KwaZulu-Natal and Western Cape. The most vulnerable municipalities are located around the cities of Johannesburg Tshwane, eThekwini, Nelson Mandela Bay and Cape Town are taking place.

In looking at possible pathfinder projects to be undertaken by the WRP, a longer-term pipeline was shortlisted to ten indicative projects across the country. While this assessment is not definitive it provides a useful indication of where there may be opportunities for water security improvements. A localised analysis of vulnerabilities was undertaken using the Green Book municipal vulnerability assessment tool as well as the Water Reuse Programme’s (WRP) project prioritisation tool.

The vulnerabilities at municipal level are summarised in Table 1. While all show increased risk and vulnerability due to the impacts of climate change a number of these do not demonstrate strong climate arguments for water security improvements. A more detailed analysis using the WRP project prioritisation assessments is imperative and will be undertaken as a first step prior to projects being provided project preparation support. Only projects with clear climate arguments will receive financial support from the WRP.

The Environmental and Social Management Frameworks for the WRP outline the importance of ensuring that all projects adequately address all related environmental and social risk. These detailed vulnerability studies must also be used.

## B.2 (a): Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words; approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

There is a growing realization that the South African water sector needs progressive and innovative approaches to enable adaptation to climate change impacts and ensure a resilient and water secure future. The Water Sector Reform Programme (WSRP) aims to support the relevant provincial government who are required to initiate and undertake technical projects such as water reuse projects. This programme will transform the provision of these water services while building resilience to climatic variability and ensuring long-term sustainability. The WSRP will also strengthen institutional and financial capacity of these institutions as well as the broader water sector.

This is aligned to the 2018 NISWP, the 2019 National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy, and the 2021 Fonds Africain de Développement (FAD) Water Sector Strategy. The WSRP will address regulatory issues surrounding a range of systemic issues, underpinning infrastructural interventions as well as being to ecosystem-based approaches in a structured manner to revolutionize urban water systems and ensure sustainable adaptation to climate change related water security challenges.

Therefore,

* If technical and financial support is provided to municipalities in the identification, preparation and implementation of water reuse projects for climate adaptation at scale through a pipeline of projects,
    * Then the local socio-economic resilience of communities to climate change will be strengthened through improved water security. Because transformational change in public and private sector funding mechanisms will also be enabled by the WSRP, this will underpin the upscaling of water reuse nationally.

The WIRF will therefore directly reduce the climate vulnerability of communities through the scaling up of urban water reuse projects. The implementation of these projects will be enabled through the introduction of programmatic support to municipalities to develop prepare and implement these projects as well as by developing and providing innovative public-private blended financing arrangements. This will not only


---
**Page 24**
---

The WRP aligns with these opportunities and trends and is designed to take a holistic view of water reuse opportunities, supporting optimal projects on a case-by-case basis without a pre-determined leaning towards specific sectors or technologies. The WRP will focus on catalyzing action in the water sector for climate resilience purposes.

In so doing, the WRP will realize a number of important co-benefits that are supportive of the overall impact that the programme will have. Through the upscaling of water reuse projects the programme will result in real environmental benefits and will provide an economic benefit to downstream aquatic ecosystem health through the maintenance of water quality and quantity. The WRP will also provide a range of co-benefits for local livelihoods, particularly in terms of economic activities, and have the co-benefit of supportive localized development, particularly where many local economic activities are dependent upon water for their processes and are operational within municipal settings.

The WRP will also have an impact on improving levels of capacity regarding water security and developing climate resilience which will enable municipalities to better manage future water stress. The WRP will also have a focus on improved levels of capacity regarding water security and developing climate resilience which will enable municipalities to better manage future water stress.

The WRP will create indirect impacts by catalysing climate resilient solutions within municipal settings with a focus on innovation and new technologies. These projects will create a demonstration effect that will further encourage replication in other municipalities. The limited understanding of climate focused responses and the fragmented nature of water security has often led to poor alignment, often with poor alignment. The provision of programmatic support to municipalities will assist in ensuring adaptive improvement and improved climate resilient outcomes and will align water reuse projects with national priorities such as disaster risk reduction, urban planning, water regulation and so forth.

Inability of municipalities to technically conceptualize and propose water reuse projects: Most municipalities have limited technical capacity regarding climate change and the adaptive responses that are required and as such struggle to introduce innovative solutions. The establishment of a centre of excellence will help to address this gap (e.g., through training, mentoring and support for project proposals).

## Limited municipal capacity to procure and structure contracts to support new business models
Many municipalities have weak supply chain management capacities that are not able to develop new institutional and contractual arrangements. The provision of support through the WPRO will provide technical assistance in developing these capacities, as well as providing oversight to oversee the implementation of projects.

## Limited access to funding for project preparation: Access to catalytic finance is limited and when combined with the weak capacity of staff to develop funding applications, often municipalities cannot unlock the climate adaptation projects they require. The structured provision of project preparation support through the WPRO will provide technical assistance in developing proposals that are ready to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) or other sources of finance.

## Lack of private sector involvement in the financing, development and ownership of climate resilience interventions: The urgent and substantial infrastructure gap in South Africa’s water sector calls for alternative financing solutions. Currently, private sector investment in climate resilient infrastructure is limited due to a lack of long-term financial instruments and prohibitive risk. In addition, there is often mistrust between public sector and private sector actors. An opportunity exists through the WPRO to provide technical assistance in developing innovative “blended finance” and to develop risk-sharing protocols that facilitate project bankability and enable private sector investment. The WPRO will also provide a structured advisory role that will facilitate the arrangements between public and private sector actors.

## Limited understanding of climate impact on water security and interventions required: There is a lack of awareness among municipalities about how climate change impacts water security at local and scales, and the importance of developing a range of alternative supply options such as water reuse. It is therefore important to highlight that there are many ways in which climate change can be addressed and understanding a structured approach to development and exchange knowledge will be essential to the WPRO’s success.

The introduction of programmematic technical, financial and procurement support will assist municipalities to develop adaptive capacity and have profound impact upon how municipalities strengthen their abilities to manage water security challenges, thereby changing the nature of the water supply discourse. This is important as many municipalities are currently developing a range of sub-projects that will address climate related vulnerabilities, are well structured and bankable. Through this support capacity can be built within municipalities, and between municipalities, with important support from partnerships between key national government departments such as the Department of Water and Sanitation (DWS), the Western Cape Municipal Infrastructure Support Agency (MISA), Infrastructure South Africa (ISA), and the South African Local Government Association (SALGA). The WPRO will also provide technical assistance in developing water reuse and will develop and provide a range of guidance documents, implementation protocols, standardised procurement documents and other tools that will assist municipalities to support sub-projects.

This programmematic approach is essential for supporting the ongoing developments in the engagement of the private sector as well as the establishment of a new asset class. It will also support the ongoing development of innovative structuring and financing of water reuse projects.

The WPRO will communicate developments and initiatives that will have targeted impact at institutional and policy levels, including the role of water reuse and the role and water reuse in this. Noting the often-negative perceptions of water reuse, improving the levels of understanding of water reuse and its benefits is essential to the success of the WPRO. The development of a clear narrative on why water reuse is equally important for the WPRO to work closely with key national government departments, as well as the municipalities, to improve the understanding of the technical, financial and regulatory dimensions of these

## B

### The Theory of Change for the WRP is provided in Figure.

The introduction of the WRP as a dedicated facility to invest in climate adaptation measures, integrating climate change considerations into water resource management and water security approaches, and providing key stakeholders technical assistance and capacity building will contribute to a paradigm shift in South Africa’s financial and water sector to spur the prioritisation climate adaptation measures at scale. This transformative shift will be achieved by supporting the development of institutional arrangements between provinces and municipalities through supportive effective coordination and integration between institutions, plans and programmes that are aligned with national priorities for climate change adaptation in terms of long-term water security and the advancement of the sustainable development goals.

The programme will shift the development pathway by support integrated and transformational planning (Component 1), by introducing innovative technologies and approaches (Component 2), by mobilising finance at all scales (Component 2), and by sharing knowledge of successful experiences at scale and developing climate expertise (Component 3).

| GOAL | DESCRIPTION |
| --- | --- |
| **Goal 1** | To support the development of institutional arrangements for water projects for climate change adaptation in South Africa. |

| OUTCOMES & CO BENEFITS | OUTCOMES: Climate sensitive water resources management at municipal level will be more effectively implemented. Environmental Co-benefits: Improved water demand and supply, improved water quality, reduced flood risk and enhanced ecosystem services. Economic Co-benefits: Increased economic growth and development. Social Co-benefits: Improved access to safe drinking water for all, improved sanitation facilities, improved health outcomes. Gender & Inclusive Co-benefits: Enhanced gender equality in the water sector, youth and women’s empowerment, increased participation of vulnerable groups in decision-making processes. |
| PROJECT OUTPUTS | A presentation platform of climate change adaptation projects are developed. Recommissioning of existing water infrastructure is completed. Water resource management projects are implemented. Gender and inclusive capacity building is supported. |

| PROJECT PARTNERS | South African National Department of Water and Sanitation, provincial departments of water resources, project implementers, municipalities, and other stakeholders. |
| BARRIERS | Lack of institutional arrangements for climate change adaptation in the water sector. Limited technical expertise on climate change adaptation at municipal level. Insufficient financial resources to support climate change adaptation projects. Inadequate capacity building and knowledge sharing among institutions and government departments. |

| ASSUMPTIONS | The development of a dedicated facility, WRP, will be supported by the national government. National and provincial coordination between sectors and levels of government will be supported in the implementation of WRP. Water sector stakeholders have sufficient technical expertise to implement climate change adaptation projects at scale. |


---
**Page 27**

Therefore, part of the response to South Africa’s vulnerability to climate change the WRP therefore will support a climate adaptive development pathway in five ways. Firstly, by reducing water demand through improved natural, technical, and institutional system efficiency incorporated by water reuse sub-projects. Secondly, by increasing water supply reliability and resilience through the integration of managed natural systems ensuring water security incorporating managed natural systems into urban/rural water infrastructure as well as at source. Thirdly, by improving ecosystem services and biodiversity conservation through the focus on enabling environment by explicitly addressing institutional and financial challenges related to developing water reuse sub-projects. Fourthly, by promoting climate adaptation to account for co-benefits and to manage trade-offs in a more holistic manner. Finally, by integrating water reuse as a key component of developing climate resilience and lastly by addressing policy, legal and regulatory frameworks that are necessary to ensure the systematic investment in water reuse as part of climate resilient development.

The following adaptation co-benefits have been identified:

- **Co-benefit 1: Environmental** Through the implementation of the WRP there will be improvement in the environmental quality of water bodies in South Africa as a result of the establishment and operation of wastewater treatment works, with these waters being redirected to water reuse plants. In a number of instances, this will reduce nutrient loads in water bodies. The construction of Advanced Treatment Plants would have significant impact in reducing these nutrient loads. Also, with the reduced demand upon local catchments the water requirements of aquatic ecosystems may be met without compromising other uses and thereby improving their ecological health. This is in line with the Act and supporting water use authorisations. The eligibility criteria for sub-projects under the WRP have been designed to ensure that they are consistent with the objectives of the Act, the Act on energy and nutrients and in so doing these sub-projects will determine their contribution to reduction in GHG emissions.

- **Co-benefit 2: Economic** With municipalities having the mandate to drive local economic development, improvements in water security would enable the maintenance of economic activities including agriculture, industry and tourism. For example, the effects of droughts in Cape Town and in Nelson Mandela Bay have demonstrated the severe impact of these events on the economy.

- **Co-benefit 3: Gender** In many communities women and girl children are often responsible for the collection of water. Yet, women in South African communities are generally subjected to unequal development opportunities and are often denied access to education and other services that would enable them to enjoy an equal social, cultural, economic, and political status in their communities. Improvements in water security will contribute towards gender equality by improving the provision of water service standards, as well as local livelihoods. The engagement of women, youth and marginalized groups in planning, investment and decision making from the initiation of sub-projects under the WRP would ensure that these groups are not only beneficiaries but also have building resilience along value chains and in local livelihoods. Nevertheless, women are often not engaged in water management decisions at all levels. The engagement of women will contribute to raising awareness and the building of capacity, the engagement of women in the implementation of water reuse projects will be critical for ensuring that their needs and concerns to be addressed. Improvements in water security will also be supportive of their local economic activities that typically involve women such as local and community agriculture.

### B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

#### Programme Context and Market

The WRP is a national intervention to address climate induced water insecurity in South Africa and forms part of a larger NWPWIP. With the establishment of the NWPP being approved in principle by the Council of Ministers on 18 December 2013, the National Water Act (Act No. 36 of 1998) has been amended to establish terms of the Infrastructure Development Act which includes as its main objective to “provide for the facilitation and co-ordination of public infrastructure which is of significant economic or social importance in South Africa” and in so doing, such development should be consistent with the National Water Strategy.


---
**Page 29**
---

(NIP 2020) prepared by 'ISA' in the office of the Presidency and issued by the Minister of Public Works and Infrastructure at the time of the Infrastructure Development Act in August 2021.

The NWWP establishment was mandated in 2021 with the support of National Government and key sector partners to address a range of water sector challenges with a particular focus on innovative technical and institutional solutions. The NWWP is aligned with the GCF Strategic Plan 2023-2025, which aims at scaling up and in creating a new asset class under the WRP. The WRP will be the initial programme under the banner of the NWWP. It will focus on water reuse and recycling projects, including urban water reuse, private sector engagement in water and sanitation services, off-grid sanitation services and rural community water services.

The WRP is aimed at identifying, encouraging and assisting municipalities with the scaling of their water reuse sub-projects and programmes. South Africa is a water-stressed nation, and its national water resource system is continually being subjected to pressures, with a potential 17% water deficit forecast by 2030. Similarly, the country faces challenges in terms of climate change, increased water scarcity compounded by rapid urban growth and population density. The NWWP aims at addressing these challenges through coherent boundaries to develop efficient and robust urban water systems to improve supply resilience for future generations. In this regard, the Department of Water and Sanitation has developed a National Urban Water Strategy that provides a framework within which green infrastructure and sustainable development requires improved climate and risk-sensitive development plans, supported by improved governance and capacity as well as coherent systems, services and resources.

Likewise, South Africa is becoming increasingly energy constrained with rolling load-shedding over the last decade. The NWWP aims at diversifying the energy mix while addressing the country’s contributions to Greenhouse Gas emissions™. There are opportunities to realise sludge beneficience with this being a potential secondary criterion for selection (Criterion 2) and also providing additional co-benefit options for sludge such as energy, sludge to gas, composting and fertilisation. These would be determined and developed on a case-by-case basis considering the existing infrastructure, available resources and opportunities in each municipality. The NWWP will provide a framework within which the introduction of this beneficience as a secondary eligibility criterion will encourage these interventions.

Historically, water systems have been developed using a linear design approach i.e., source, treat, transport and use. This has resulted in fragmented urban water systems that are technically inefficient and results in primary technical solutions and the fragmented management of the urban water cycle i.e. water management silos. Complex challenges of climate change, increased water scarcity compounded by rapid urban growth and population density have led to a need for coherent boundaries to develop efficient and robust urban water systems to improve supply resilience for future generations. In this regard, the Department of Water and Sanitation has developed an Integrated Urban Water Development Framework that provides a framework within which green infrastructure and sustainable development requires improved climate and risk-sensitive development plans, supported by improved governance and capacity as well as coherent systems, services and resources.


| B |  |
| --- | --- |
| PAGE 26 OF 150 |


---
**Page 30**
---

The need for reuse as a means of augmenting and diversifying the water mix in South Africa is widely acknowledged in national policies and guidelines yet with limited uptake to date. There are a range of technologies available that can be used to develop bespoke solutions for local contexts (E.g., Water Reuse from Municipal Wastewater Treatment Works [WWTWs] as an alternative water supply has largely been ignored, but industry sees potential). However, the industrial use of water in South Africa is estimated to be only 10% of total water consumption. This represents a gap in the expansion of the reuse of treated water to other sectors, particularly for the domestic and agricultural sectors.

Globally, there is recognition that public acceptance of water reuse, in the context of Direct Potable Reuse (DPR), is a significant issue and a recent Water Research Commission study on water reuse noted the importance of developing awareness campaigns and knowledge building tools as an essential part of any DPR project. This study survey found that only 48.5% of respondents would support direct potable use in times of extreme need, with 30.7% saying they would not support it under any circumstances. The results from this study demonstrate the need for significant effort to address these issues at sub-project level. Hence, the development of a communication strategy and plan for the project is essential.

South Africa’s current WWTWs produce and discharge approximately 21 million litres per year (ml/yr) of treated effluent to receiving waters”. There is a strong correlation between potable use and effluent discharge in cities, where approximately 60% of the annual average daily demand (AAD) is required for industrial purposes. This presents an opportunity for reuse in growing cities, as efficient quantities generally increase in proportion to increased AAD. However, this also means that there will be a greater need for tertiary treatment and in some instances will require improved secondary treatment performance.

Various water reuse archetypes that may be considered as potential project implementation options within the WWP include:

* **Direct Potable Reuse (DPR):** The treatment of final wastewater effluent in an advanced treatment plant prior to potable use. This is a relatively new technology and can be expensive, but it does not damage the environment, while being a generator of a side stream waste in the form of sludge. However, with a move to Circular Economy thinking and Water Sensitive Urban Design, there is a push towards DPR as it allows for the recovery of energy, water, minerals, metals, salts, gases, and nutrients. As such, WWTWs may be viewed as Water Resource Centres, where one is able to extract these resources from the waste stream while reducing the pressures placed on non-renewable and other stressed resources. Options for the WWP include:
    * Direct Potable Reuse (DPR): The treatment of final wastewater effluent in an advanced treatment plant prior to potable use.
    * Indirect Potable Reuse (IPR): The treatment of final wastewater effluent in an advanced treatment plant prior to discharge into a bulk water reservoir. This will then be used as part of the supply for a bulk water reservoir where it will blend with raw water before treatment in a bulk water treatment plant.

These projects tend to have high capital and operational costs due to the advanced technology and extensive controls required to ensure the strict water quality standards.

## Characteristics of water reuse (the lighter bolded row indicates a more favorable solution)

| Reuse characteristics | Direct Potable DPR | Indirect Potable DPR | Industrial | Irrigation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Advanced treatment prior to reuse | High **| Very High | High | Medium | Low |
| Range of viability of water | High | High | High | High | High |
| Capital Cost | High | High | High | High | High |
| Operational Cost | High | High | High | High | High |
| Public Acceptance | Medium | High | High | High | High |
| Development Experience Required | Low | Low | Low | Low | Low |

An important part of potable reuse projects is the need to conduct regular highly specialist effluent quality check that is over and above conventional laboratory testing. These specialist tests are particularly necessary for projects where there has been a significant change in the water quality, such as when the water quality standard, South African National Standards (SANS), 241 defines the minimum water quality requirements for potable reuse. The SANS standard is based on the WHO Guidelines for Drinking Water Quality and is not extensive enough to cover the specific water pollutants found in potable reuse projects. For instance, it does not have a standard for phosphorus or organic Chemical Oxygen Demand and the presence of these substances can be an indicator that there are other contaminants present.

The approach to these will need to be developed with the South African Bureau of Standards and the WHO. The WHO guidelines note a complex array of criteria with focus on the CECS. This is increasingly complex and the WHO's Potable Water Resource Guidelines" notes that:

* Water quality standards for potable reuse are based primarily on microbial performance targets (log reductions of pathogens in sources waters) as the primary health-based target for potable reuse.


---
**Page 32**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29 OF 156

## Detailed performance targets identified are 8.5 log reduction of enteric bacteria, 9.5 log reduction of enteric viruses and 8.5 log reduction of enteric protozoa. They do not represent guideline values and are not intended to encourage pathogen monitoring in drinking water, which would be impractical and of little value.

While there tends to be considerable interest in CECs, such as pharmaceuticals and personal care products (PPCPs), these are not considered a concern at this time and generally do not warrant setting of new guideline values in national or regional standards.

In specific circumstances, where a chemical with no guideline value is identified as a concern, appropriate action should be taken. This may include further investigations into potential risks and the need for implementation of additional control measures.

It is important to note that the Municipalities, as Project Owners, will be responsible for the attainment of water quality standards in their projects and the DPR Assessment Team will undertake reviews of municipal progress in this regard.

From the market study undertaken of 14 water reuse projects that are operational, it was focused on DPRs and sub-projects. The following criteria were being isolated to demonstrate a similar mix of water reuse archetypes. In order to assess the potential pipeline of sub-projects that the WRF could undertake in its initial phases, the following eligibility criteria were considered:

* **Climate Vulnerability:** Climate vulnerability as a result of climate change, considering the impact of bulk water transfers and the importance of these to the local social economy, is the primary criterion for support by the WRF. In this regard, a projected climate induced water stress index (WSI) of 10 or greater is considered an important indicator of the technical viability of a potential water reuse sub-project.

* **Treatment Plant Design Capacity:** The size of the WWTP as it directly impacts on the size of a water reuse project and its ability to support a large-scale water reuse treatment project. Greater than 20 Mlday is feasible in supporting a water reuse advanced treatment plant.

* **Compliance:** DWB publishes a periodic report containing the overall effluent compliance for each municipality. The WRF will use this information as an important indicator of the technical viability of a potential water reuse sub-project. Best practice dictates that final effluent should maintain a very high level of compliance for a reuse project to be viable. Greater than 95% compliance is considered a good indication of overall municipal compliance; therefore essential.

* **WWTPs:** WWTPs are essentially two types of WWTP in South Africa, namely biofiltration plants and activated sludge plants. Biofiltration is an older technology that has little to no current market interest as it is not cost-effective for new projects. Activated sludge plants have a high level of compliance; this is often variable. As a result, biofiltration plants are not recommended for potable or industrial reuse projects and treatment plans with this technology were excluded from the list.

* **Reuse Projects:** The WRF will consider only those water reuse projects which include Direct Potable Reuse (DPR), Indirect Potable Reuse (IPR), Industrial Reuse (IRR) and Irrigation Reuse (IRR). DPR is considered a priority for support by the WRF. In addition, some projects could likely include support to industrial reuse, particularly where industries are operating with municipal jurisdictions. However, the selection of the project archetype will be appropriately context specific.

* **Already Initiated Reuse Projects:** Where municipalities have already initiated these projects there will be an understanding in the sub-project component as well as greater possibilities for innovation.

## Example Project Summary for an Indicative Project

A summary of the project is provided below in relation to a potential wastewater industrial reuse project to be implemented as a Public Private Partnership (PPP). For the Municipality, site-specific future climate projections indicate the following for the 2011-2040 period under both a moderate climate change future (RCP 4.5) and high climate change future (RCP 8.5).

### Table 7: Site-specific future climate projections for the Municipality 2011–2040 under two scenarios

| Climatic Variable | RCP 4.5 | RCP 8.5 |
|---|---|---|
| Temperature (annual mean) | +1°C | +1°C |
| Aridity (annual mean) | -3% | -7% |
| Water discharge (annual mean) | -2% | -6% |
| Water discharge (annual mean) | 4% | 8% |
| Length of longest dry spell | A small increase (<5 days a year) | A small increase (<5 days a year) |
| Water quality | Slight decrease (+20% change) | Small decrease (-20% change) |

Thus, the Municipality is projected to face rising temperatures from climate change. In addition to decreases in rainfall, runoff, and discharge, the rising heat is likely to drive an increase in aridity, leading to somewhat drier conditions.

As a result, water deficits are being projected as:
- 20–40% deficit under medium population growth scenarios by 2050,
- >80% high water stress by 2040 as predicted by the WRI Aqueduct Water Tool.

The feasibility study indicates that there is, on average, at least 26Mm³ of available tertiary treated used for the proposed project. The Municipality has identified a potential industrial user for the product water and identified a total of five potential end users who could potentially purchase up to 71.7 Ml per annum of the product water.

A total of 5 potential technical options and configurations were identified and analysed through a comprehensive weighing and assessment system. The option of a new regional treatment (capacity 75 Mm³/year) was selected as preferred.

A preferred site for the treatment plant has been selected noting the proximity of to takes as well as a range of environmental considerations. Noting the brine to be produced by the plant the proximity to the ocean for disposal is also considered in the selection process. The relevant legislation includes the National Environmental Management Act (Act 107 of 1998) and the Integrated Coastal Management Act (Act 26 of 2008). A total of 25 ha will be required for the regional treatment facility.

Pilot plant testing has been undertaken and indicated that the effluent can be treated to an acceptable standard using a membrane process (presumably ultrafiltration) and reverse osmosis processes. Some additional pre-treatment steps will be needed.

The total capital cost estimate in 2017 (including land costs) amounted to R1 736 209 178. The Unitary Reference Value was estimated at R10.82/m³.


---
**Page 35**
---

The potential for biogas generation was also included in the study and it was recommended that thermophilic digestion for CHP energy be implemented as part of the project. It was however stated that the viability of the project was not dependent on the biogas electricity generation. Furthermore, composting of sludge was also recommended.

A collection system consisting of five pumpstations and rising mains will be required to collect and transfer the treated effluent from the WWTP to the treatment plant. Presently there are 10 existing pipelines with a total of 31.6 km of pipelines varying from 500mm to 864mm pipelines will be required. A total installed power of 585 kW will be required for pumping.

The users will be divided into two quality streams. One of the larger users will receive lower quality water re-use water directly from the WWTP (30 Mld). The other users will receive a higher quality water from the tertiary treatment plant (45Mld). Treatment at the regional facility will take place in three treatment modules as follows:

* **50 Mld domestic WWTFW**
    * 35 Mld Industrial treatment work
    * 15 Mld Tertiary treatment works for higher quality water

A 50 Mld Biological Nutrient removal activated sludge plant will treat the raw sewage diverted from the local WWTFW. The 35 Ml industrial treatment plant will treat industrial effluent from the pulp and paper mills using conventional biological nutrient removal process (anaerobic digestion + nitrification + denitrification) followed by advanced oxidation (Ozonation) and Granular Activated Carbon.

The distribution network to off-take users will be via a gravity driven network. This is made possible by the large diameter pipes that are available. The network consists of 27 km pipelines ranging from 250mm diameter to 783mm diameter pipes will make up the network.

As part of the feasibility study, the feasibility of implementing value adding technology in the form of the potential for producing biogas energy and the potential to convert sludge to a useful end product were investigated and found to be feasible producing sufficient power to significantly reduce the load drawn from the national grid.


Benefits to Ecosystem Services

Conventional effluent from the macerators and WWTFW at the municipality is discharged into nearby streams, natural water courses or directly into the ocean through deep sea outfalls. High volumes of discharge can have a significant impact on these ecosystems as well as the marine environment. The quality of water bodies, on coastal systems, and on the composition of the water in these sources. A decrease in the amount of effluent released this surface water sources can go a long way to reducing adverse environmental impacts that are associated with conventional effluent discharge. This will have a positive impact on these environments as increased wastewater recycling, can significantly lessen the deterioration of ecosystem health and hence improve wildlife substrate in these areas.

For purposes of project approval, it is assumed that the implementation of the project shall divert the use and demand for potable water and therefore present an economic cost avoided to the value of R129 million at an 8% social discount rate.

## Secondary criteria: At the secondary level, five exclusionary criteria will be applied to ensure that the potential water reuse sub-projects selected would be able to make a strong case for inclusion in the WRP pipeline project. These criteria are listed below and must all be addressed for a project to obtain WRP funding.

### a) Treatment plant design capacity: Only sub-projects with a WWTP with a design capacity of equal to, or greater than 20 Mld would be supported by the WRP.
### b) Compliance with discharge standards that achieve greater than 50% compliance with discharge standards will be supported.
### c) WWTP Technology Type: Only activated sludge plants will be supported to ensure the consistency and reliability of the technology.
### d) Environmental and social safeguards: All sub-projects shall comply with both GCF and SDGS Environmental and Social Safeguards Standards, with only Category B or C levels of impact being acceptable.

## Tertiary criteria: Noting that the selection to this point would provide potentially a longer list of sub-projects, tertiary criteria will be applied to assist in prioritising sub-projects. These would include the following criteria:

### a) Linkage to other projects: Priority will be provided to water reuse sub-projects in supporting of strategic projects.
### b) Linked agreements: Priority will be afforded to sub-project where the existence of a contract/agreement is assured.

## Financial viability: All sub-projects that move beyond project preparation into implementation must be financially viable, as well as required to be demonstrated in the Project specific feasibility studies / project information notes. The financial viability study will need to demonstrate affordability which will include the need for certainty around Project offtake and/or revenue streams. A minimum senior debt service cover ratio (DSCR) will be fixed during the financial assessment stage of project preparation and shall be fixed prior to the sub-project being taken to implementation.

The criteria for the selection of beneficiaries and borrowers are provided in Table 10 below.

## Eligible recipients/borrower: public sector

Public sector entities (including municipalities, water boards, water user associations and other public bodies) are eligible to apply for funding from the GCF if they are responsible for the treatment of sewage effluent and/or wastewater, including water reclamation, recovery and reuse, and act as project owners and/or sponsors.

### (a) Public sector entities (including municipalities, water boards, water user associations and other public bodies) mandated and responsible for the treatment of sewage effluent and/or wastewater, including water reclamation, recovery and reuse, and act as project owners and/or sponsors

### (b) Public sector entities (including municipalities, water boards, water user associations and other public bodies) which are publicly owned, publicly managed or publicly financed, and which have a mandate to provide services in the area of water supply, water treatment, sanitation, water reclamation, recovery and reuse.

## Eligible recipients/borrower: private sector

Private sector entities (including project SPVs) that obtained a concession from the public sector for the preparation, finance, construction and operation of projects related to the treatment of sewage effluent and/or wastewater, including water reclamation, recovery and reuse, will act as project sponsors on behalf of the public sector.

### (a) Project SPVs mandated and approved by the public sector for the preparation, finance, construction and operation of projects related to the treatment of sewage effluent and/or wastewater, including water reclamation, recovery and reuse sub-projects.

### (b) Private sector SPVs which has a mandate from the public sector to prepare, finance, construct and operate water reclamation, recovery and reuse sub-projects.

## Application of proceeds

Proceeds will be used to prepare water reuse sub-projects, including conducting feasibility studies, project assessment, scoping investigations, preparation of detailed design, feasibility studies, structuring and implementation of the component t. Proceeds may also be applied to capacity building as part of the component t.

## Process

### (a) Applicants will be required to apply for such funding from the WPO who will assess their eligibility and determine whether funding will be made available. The WPO will prepare project preparation and/or capacity building proposals, which will enter into an agreement with the borrower on the relationship and the process of implementation.

The first application of funding (i.e., project preparation or capacity building) will be to conduct a detailed feasibility study and preliminary screening criteria on the subproject. If the feasibility study indicates that the sub-project will benefit from GCF funding, then it may apply for funding support, but if the criteria is

### (b) Borrowers will apply for funding support from the WPF through the Water Partnerships Office. The WPO will assess their eligibility and determine whether GCF loan funding can be made available to the projects as part of the intended finance solution.

Should sub-projects request funding without having been supported with project preparation, feasibility studies or preliminary screening criteria required to meet the primary screening criteria.

## Financial assessment criteria

GCF funding will be utilised in a manner that ensures the GCF concessionality will reach and benefit the beneficiaries of the sub-projects. The DSBA and the IBCF will do the assessment and will have access to all pertinent information such as the NPV, financial model, etc. A physical inspection may in certain instances be performed.

### Minimum Target DSCR

* **The individual project level cashflows (Expenditures and Revenues) will be assessed over the life of the project** to determine the Financial Net Present Value (NPV) of each sub-project and the Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) on each sub-project investment. The financial analysis will demonstrate the financial viability of the sub-project with and without GCF funding and credit enhancement.
* **A financial assessment (using the WRP project financial model) will be done on a sub-project by sub-project basis** to determine the individual project level cashflows, NPV, FIRR, and DSCR. This will include assessing the individual sub-project level and comparing each project's minimum DSCR against the Target Minimum Senior Debt DSCR.
* **If the individual sub-projects' DSCRs fall below the minimum Target Senior Debt DSCR**, indicate a potential funding gap and these sub-projects will be eligible for GCF concessional loans in order to improve their financial viability.

This assessment is done on an ongoing basis, and the DSBA will provide feedback to the IBCF when sub-projects are considered for funding and GCF will be appraised of these considerations for their input through the financial structuring process, prior to finalisation.

### Minimum Concessionality

* **Employ the minimum level of concessionality to address financing requirements, market entry barriers or incremental costs in line with the WRP's financial model for utilising concessional funding.**

All water reuse sub-projects proposed for financing by the DSBA (ind) utilization of GCF funding for the WRP are assessed for eligibility within the DSBA investment decision making structures. The DSBA applies an internal assessment process to determine whether or not a project is eligible for funding under the sub-projects. Sub-projects are screened according to the DSBA Environmental and Social Safeguards Standards (E&SSS) Framework.

DSBA has a well-established set of tools and procedures to classify green operations and assess and mitigate environmental and social projects, supported by internationally accredited environmental and social safeguards standards which align with the legislation in all of the jurisdictions in which the Bank operates (Figure 10).
