## DISA ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL STANDARDS

During project preparation, the WRP must consider various issues (Figure 11) and considerations that influence project structuring, with a number of parameters influencing what is achievable and feasible in the local context. There are several technical, social and environmental considerations.

### 1. Scope

The scope will be determined by the type of project (e.g., design, build, operate, finance which influences the risk allocation, determines roles and responsibilities, legal and contractual arrangements etc.).

### 2. Key Performance Indicators Framework for establishing Key Performance Indicators and motivating for the funding arrangements.

### 3. Partnership

The WRP will need to identify intercontracting with either Private sector or public sector and the associated procurement strategy.

### 4. Performance

Determining how performance will be measured is this is especially relevant for the underpinning water input from the water services provider, and reclaimed water output to be provided to off-takers. All performance requirements must be identified as these need to be priced into the project cost.

### 5. Funding mechanism

Outlining how activities in the contract will be paid for, there grant funding for the project will be required against the supply of reclaimed water. Clarifying who will use and pay for the reclaimed water (off-takers) against the supply of reclaimed water.

### Project Site: Determining where the sub-project infrastructure will be developed and operated.

The WRP must determine the type of project, the extent to which it is a stand-alone project or part of an existing project, interface with other projects, the extent to which it is subject to any termination, interface with water services provider functions, environmental compliance requirements and if it is subject to any other agreements (e.g., public private partnerships).

### 6. Cost

Determining the capital cost of land acquisition where the sub-project infrastructure is developed. Noting that under certain contractual arrangements (i.e. public private partnerships) there is no requirement for the WRP to determine the cost of land acquisition as this will be determined by the unrepresented interest of access.

### Water Quality

The quality of local water quality and its management and monitoring will be an essential consideration noting that water quality standards for water inputs into advance treatment plants and water quality discharge standards for water from reuse plants need to be adhered to and be supported by ongoing monitoring and testing.

WRP project preparation process
A methodology has been conceived for the development of the project pipeline, with the aim being that based on certain constraints and exclusionary criteria, potential water reuse sub-projects may be assessed for full project development.

The project preparation process will follow the following high-level stages: the 3-stage and 5 Case model (strategic, economic, commercial, financial, management) Model (SCM) process which develops over time. The SCM concept is based on a phased approach to project development, starting with the Strategic Case (SC), then the Full Business Case (FBC). Each one of the cases comprises a Strategic Case, Economic Case, Commercial Case and Financial Case. These are developed in parallel by the respective teams of the EBC, IFC and FBC and are progressively developed as one proceeds through the 3 stages (Table 11).

| Scoping | Pre-feasibility study to motivate stakeholders. Apply secondary criteria and more holistic approach. | EBC: Business Case |
| Feasibility | Options analysis, feasibility study & preparation of project strategy and planning | EBC: Financial Case |

## Programmatic Support

With the intention to strengthen local climate adaptation through the preparation and implementation of water reuse projects under the WRP, the following activities will be undertaken:

- The establishment of a WPO as a centre of excellence that will provide transversal project preparation support and will help create a pipeline of water reuse projects.
- The identification of suitable water reuse sub-projects with tangible climate impacts and significant ecosystem-water dependencies (impacted by climate change) and the development of bankable feasibility studies for these projects. These feasibility studies are essential to bankability. The GCF grant support will be essential for project preparation and will be co-financed by funding from project sponsors, project owners (municipalities), the DBSA, the SA Government and other relevant stakeholders.
- The identification of suitable water reuse sub-projects with tangible climate impacts and significant ecosystem-water dependencies (impacted by climate change) and the development of bankable feasibility studies for these projects. These feasibility studies are essential to bankability. Funding from project sponsors, project owners (municipalities), the DBSA, the SA Government and other relevant stakeholders will be co-financed.
- The introduction of a blended finance solution that will allow municipalities an alternative (and complementary) approach to financing water reuse sub-projects in addition to existing funding programmes. The proposed Blended Finance Solution (BFS) is designed to finance water reuse sub-projects in a way that: i) optimises use of concessional finance, and ii) enhances the use of private sector investment.
- The undertaking of capacity building and awareness creation activities that will strengthen the relative support for water reuse projects.

Water reuse sub-projects under the WRP will not be limited to only water reuse but may include all related opportunities, assessed on a project-by-project basis including i) water reclamation (including associated infrastructure), ii) wastewater treatment processes, iii) energy generation, and iv) upgrading wastewater treatment processes, thereby realising potential mitigation co-benefits on a case-by-case basis. These opportunities will only be identified during the project preparation of individual sub-projects.

As a consequence of implementing water reuse, there will likely be no increase in GHG emissions from the facility as the ATP will be additional to an existing WWTW. However, there will be opportunities to reduce the direct and indirect emissions associated with the WWTW and ATP design and operation. The extent of these works is shown in Figure 12 presents the proposed plan to reduce the additional emissions due to water reuse. The reduction in emissions from the WWTW and ATP as a consequence of water reuse will be quantified. Opportunities to mitigate the increased emissions will then be investigated. These include optimisation of the direct emissions and energy consumption of the WWTW and ATP design, and assessing the potential for sludge beneficial use to reduce GHG emissions.

Programme Components

This WRP is being developed through the implementation of 3 components. A first component comprises institutional activities to support the establishment of the WRP, its governance arrangements and the operationally focused WPO that will develop the project pipeline and provide project preparation support.

This component will result in a comprehensive programme and policy framework to drive water reuse. This second component will provide technical and financial transaction advisory services to the implementation of sub-projects through procured and contracted service providers and will support these projects with financing, including debt, equity and grants. The third component is an institutional component of institutional capacity building, knowledge exchange and information sharing that will complement and support components 1 and 2, and importantly strengthen the adaptive capacity of institutions to implement water reuse sub-projects.

### Component 1 - Establishing a WRP project pipeline

The establishment and resourcing of a WRP will provide an opportunity to support municipalities as primary custodians of local climate adaptation plans to undertake innovative and targeted projects that will undergo climate resilience assessments. The WRP will identify, finance and implement water reuse sub-projects at scale to ensure water security due to the impacts of climate change on water resources in urban areas, while also providing economic benefits for households and communities. It is envisaged that this WRP will become the "one-stop solution provider" for municipal water reuse sub-projects in the country. The proposed establishment of the WRP will include:

- The procurement and management of a panel of service providers to assist in the sloping and preparation of water reuse projects;
- The standardization of contracting documents, procurement of professional service providers, consultants, and contractors;
- The application of best practice design and innovative climate-smart technologies including seeking ecosystem-based solutions;
- The establishment of a centralised communication platform (in support of Component 3).

The WRP will work closely with the water sector and environmental agencies to strengthen the regulatory framework for wastewater reuse and ensure that it is not used as a substitute for discharge. This application will work together with the national certification programmes to support the ongoing uptake of water reuse as a critical information source for decision-making at local levels.

Activities under this component include:

## Sub-component 1.1: Establishment and Operationalisation of the WRP

The WRP will be a partner framework under the NWWP, with the NWWP aiming to provide support to other aspects of the water value chain and will be further developed over time. The programmatic approach to the WRP is based on the premise that there are many existing national programmes and initiatives in municipal water-related programmes with certain aspect of the WRP being leveraged to support the other programme areas. However, it should also be noted that there may be a need for new or additional needs at municipal level very technically and in scale and, with many municipalities needing support, it is necessary to have a phased and progressive approach to developing the WRP and providing project identification and prioritization guidance.

The DBSA will play several roles in the WRP. It is the accredited entity of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). It is a recognised developer of national programmes (through its Programme Development Unit) and provides programme management services for the GCF. The DBSA will be responsible for managing all aspects (e.g., the renewable energy and student accommodation programmes). It is an independent development financier and manager. If an application to GCF is successful, it will sign the Funded Activity Agreement with the OC. The DBSA will also provide project identification and prioritization guidance as the managing agent of the NWWP (executing authority), being sure to keep its roles separated. It will provide all necessary support for the WRP including but not limited to: development of a database, management processes, procurement of panels of service providers, entering into legal contracts with service providers and employees. It will also operate a ring-fenced bank account for the WPO function and serve as the secretariat of the OC. The DBSA will be responsible for reporting on a quarterly basis – approved annually by the OC.

### Activity 1.1.1: Establish the entity within the DBSA

A Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) between the Programme Development Unit and SALGA has been signed. This sets out the objectives of the NWWP, WPO and respective WRP, the roles and responsibilities of the respective parties, funding arrangements, and the establishment of a steering committee to oversee implementation of the Committee (OC). The DBSA will facilitate this contracting process and once concluded will act as secretariat of the OC to facilitate contract implementation until such time that the WPO has been formally established.


---
**Page 47**
---

Activity 1.1.3: Establish Oversight Committee and develop governance modalities. The Terms of Reference for the OC will guide the establishment of this committee. Other interested stakeholders (Department of Cooperative Governance [DCOG], MISA, National Treasury and DFFE) may be invited by MWS and DBSA to participate in the oversight role. The OC will have a Chairperson who will be appointed by the WPO managing agent. DBSA will act as management agent of the WPO for a period of five years or until such time that the WPO is fully operational. The OC will not have any executive authority and its personality during this initial period and this will only be developed over time in conjunction with the NWPP.

Activity 1.1.3: Appoint staff and operationalize the WPO and technical Water Reuse Unit. It is envisaged in the WPO PIP that a Water Reuse Unit (WRU) will be established within the DBSA to support the Water Reuse Unit (WRU) which will house the technical support for the identification, preparation and implementation oversight of water reuse sub-projects. As such the DBSA will support the development of the WRU in terms of staff recruitment, appointment and training. The WRU will be responsible for managing staff benefits, the contractual arrangements to support staff appointments, as well as the development of a professional development plan for the WRU.

Activity 1.1.4: Develop PIP and annual workload and budgets. The WPO PIP will be developed and submitted to the OC for approval. Once approved, the WPO will implement and report against this. This plan will include the identification of sub-projects, their respective budgetary allocations, as well as the capacity requirements for WRP development and water reuse sub-project preparation, as well as the capacity requirements and how they will be met. It will include the budgeting of the various units, differentiating between the sub-programme budgets and the main programme budget. The PIP will also outline the annual workload and budgets for the WRP. The WPO will include this detail in the PIP. In so doing alignment between the NWPP and the WRIP (as well as later programmes) will be ensured. An initial WRP implementation timeline is provided in Finance Annexure 1.

A key element of the annual workload and budgets will be the assessment of risk as well as outlining the mitigation measures that will be undertaken to manage these risks. For example, water use licensing and environmental impact assessments are required for all sub-projects. The identification of these sub-projects and work under Component 3 will address institutional and regulatory alignment. Undertaking targeted actions on the WRP will ensure this.

In regard to the mitigation of identified risk, the support of Lead Ministries such as the DWS, DFFE and National Treasury has been clear in the development of the WRIP and they will be central in assisting to ensure that risk is mitigated. The DBSA as AB will also play a key role in supporting the WPO to address these.

## Sub-component 1.2 Project pipeline generation

The WPO will undertake a suite of actions to support sub-projects and the implementation of the Programme. This will include the identification of projects in the first instance, supported by the procurement of service providers to support project preparation and programme implementation. The Programme will have two main types of projects: large-scale water reuse sub-projects that are directly aligned with the WPO and implement water reuse sub-systems. There will be a number of standardised steps to be followed, with varying levels of detail required for each type of project. These will be defined by the WPO and the Project Owners to follow due process by establishing transversal standardized operating procedures.

### Activity 1.2.1: Design and launch an open-ended Request for Proposal (RFP). The WPO will have outlined the process to support the development of a robust project pipeline for the WRP. During the programme design phase, the WPO will develop a list of potential projects that are aligned with the WRP’s objectives. The WPR will only support sub-projects that directly address clear climate vulnerabilities. The WPO will therefore identify and prioritise large-scale water reuse sub-projects in the first instance to ensure alignment with the WRP project meet this criterion, then the WPR will thereafter provide support to municipalities to assist in the conceptualisation and prioritisation of their large-scale water reuse sub-project prior to the project preparation phase. The WPO will also engage with a panel of experts to provide technical assistance on the issue with various role-players in the market to select and prioritise projects for the WRP noting that the initial suite of sub-projects will be critical in providing input to this process.

### Activity 1.2.3: Provide technical assistance to project structuring and conceptualisation. Technical assistance will include supporting the development of strategic cases, and full detailed business cases. In certain instances it may be of value to the sub-project to appoint service providers to assist projects from end to

end, however, this will need to be in alignment with South African procurement laws and regulations. In preparing these projects due diligence in terms of maladaptation will be essential and will cover maladaptation linked to infrastructure, to institutions and to behaviours. This will be incorporated into project selection criteria and the project preparation process.

Effective engagement with stakeholders will be an imperative aspect of ensuring that the risk of maladaptation is duly covered during this preparatory stage.

The capacity support could also be expanded to the procurement of implementation service supports if the decision is made to implement a new asset class. These implementation service providers will be selected from a pool of service providers who will work directly with the project owners in the project preparation process. This structure will assist in the delivery of the key value proposition of the WRP, that the WRP will enable continuity of care between stages on the project preparation process. This capacity will further enhance the achievement of the objective of becoming a centre of excellence.

**Component 2 - Implementing water reuse sub-projects**

The WRP will support climate innovation and market transformation through the implementation of a range of projects, including the development and implementation of water reuse sub-projects and the establishment of water reuse infrastructure as a new asset class. The WRP will establish a panel of service providers who will be directly to support municipalities in undertaking their project preparation activities including scope, feasibility and procurement. Although procured and contracted separately from each other, these service providers will work together on behalf of the project owners in the project preparation process. This structure will assist in the delivery of the key value proposition of the WRP, that the WRP will enable continuity of care between stages on the project preparation process. This capacity will further enhance the achievement of the objective of becoming a centre of excellence.

The WRP project owners are primarily the municipalities as water services authorities for their areas of jurisdiction. The project owners will engage the WRP for support and engagement will be through verbal verbal communication, written correspondence, meetings and/or workshops, signed by the required authority, as required at various stages and gates of the project preparation process.

To support the implementation of sub-projects, the approach will be to create an enabling blended financing environment through alternative funding solutions. These include infrastructure finance focussing on:

* **Securing long-term revenue streams:**
    * Providing cost savings and revenue flows to be found in improved water use and energy efficiency such as biogas production at wastewater plants.

Identifying and promoting opportunities to attract financing through traditional methods (such as through municipal bonds, public-private partnerships (such as municipal bonds, PPPs), project bonds and impact investing). The opportunity exists to develop an alternative funding solution to support the implementation of water reuse sub-projects in SA. The key outcome associated with the development of such a funding solution includes:

* **To create a new asset class by using credit enhancement to crowd in private sector funding targeted towards developing debt capital market (DCM) instruments around a specific asset class. The asset class will be defined as infrastructure projects that are aligned with the United Nations’ Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) impacts and help to meet the United Nation’s (UN’s) Sustainable Development Goal 6 – clean water and sanitation, Goal 7 – affordable and clean energy, Goal 10 – reduced inequality, Goal 12 – more sustainable consumption and production; and Goal 13 – combat climate change.**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 48 of 156

## The risk credit enhancement to support the blended finance approach through the employment of first loss / subordinated facilities, loan tenor extensions, or the employment of guarantees.

The proposed BFS is designed to finance water reuse sub-projects in a way that (a) optimises the use of climate finance, and (b) enhances the use of private capital, thereby taking this critical infrastructure to market. It achieves this by blending numerous sources of capital and their underlying instruments, for each distinct sub-project. Blending numerous sources of capital is not straightforward given the multiplicity of conditions associated with each source of funding. These include: (i) Equity and/or Subordinated Debt; (ii) Private Debt Capital; (iii) Programmatic Development Capital; and (iv) A Partial Credit Guarantee.

On the surface, blended finance is both a novel and attractive approach to financing infrastructure in South Africa. However, it is important that the BFS is not seen as an alternative to the existing WRC funding model for water reuse projects. Furthermore, whilst it is being proposed that the financing of water reuse happens at a sub-project level, there needs to be protections in place at the programme-level to ensure a consistent and standardised approach across all projects.

The WRC has been identified by the World Bank as one of the “best practices” or “world class examples of excellence” in terms of infrastructure finance solutions.

Applying a developed and agreed upon suite of blended capital principles for the WRC, an independent service provider contracted into the WPO, working alongside the Water Reuse Unit and the Project Preparation Panel to source and appropriately blend capital for each sub-project. The service provider will be responsible for preparing the project briefs and technical aspects of each sub-project. The WPO will then prepare the term sheet and project prospectuses for lenders which will include the following details, inter alia: the financial aspects of the project, its technical aspects and legal aspects. The BFS will then solicit bids from lenders to finance the sub-project on a competitive basis with a pre-determined selection for each sub-project.

The ticket size will be determined on a project by project basis and the principle of least concessionality will be applied to determine the GCF contribution at sub-project level.

Activities under this component include the following:

### Sub-component 2.1: Provision of Technical Assistance for Project Procurement

This sub-component covers all stages of the WPO to continue to support the Project Owner to take the project through procurement, to contracting and financial close. Support will continue to be provided by the WPO by the panel of service providers procured to support Project Implementation. The Project Preparation Panel will provide technical assistance in terms of the following: (a) identifying the necessary technical, legal and financial support to the Project Owner including developing a detailed project brief; (b) providing advice on the procurement strategy for each sub-project; (c) guiding the process and decision-making process. The approach will depend on the recommended way forward in the Feasibility Study undertaken during the project preparation stage.

Activities under this component include the following:

### Sub-component 2.1.1: Technical Assistance to Design and Implementation of sub-projects

Utilising the findings of the feasibility study, technical support will be provided to undertake the detailed design of the water reuse sub-project including the development of an implementation plan. The Service Provider shall immediately engage with the Project Owner to develop a project brief for each sub-project covering all aspects relating to the financing and delivery of the water sector services and facilities as well as any proposed means for the implementation of the sub-project. As part of this, the service provider will also provide advice on the regulatory framework applicable at both governmental and international sources. To achieve this regulatory review, the service provider will consult with applicable local, provincial, national and international regulatory agencies to advise them on the sub-project and to solicit their input into the necessary activities.


---
**Page 52**
---

At this stage, the Service Provider must initiate discussion between the Project Owner and the DWS to discuss the water use licensing implications and the potable water reuse standards/guidelines that will be used in this sub-project. Once the market study is complete and the toll rate arrangements are known, at that time the Service Provider will negotiate with the Project Owner and the DWS agree on the financing requirements and the associated water reuse standards/guidelines.

Activity 2.1.2: Facilitate procurement and contracting. The Service Provider will draft and submit to the Project Owner for approval the necessary capacity and budget plan required to undertake the procurement of recommended option. The procurement plan must identify and schedule all anticipated procurements, including any pre-qualification requirements. This plan will reside with the DBSA’s Supply Chain Management Unit who will oversee and monitor compliance.

It must include a procurement strategy and key contracting structures and alignment to the risk identified in the Value Assessment process. If an external procuring or a PPP solution is decided on at Council/parliament level, the Service Provider will ensure that all relevant legislation is adhered to including the necessary technical, legal and financial support to achieve financial closure of a Public Private Partnership in accordance with the National PPP Framework, the National Procurement Act, the National PPP Regulations and PPA Guidelines (Module 5) and the Municipal Asset Transfer Regulations. The Service Provider will ensure that all procurement processes are conducted in accordance with the relevant public sector procurement law, policies and guidelines, and that are in accordance with the required tendering processes of the Project Owner. This means that all procurements will be undertaken in terms of the Project Owner’s procurement policy.

Activity 2.1.3: Monitor, evaluate and report on project development. Irrespective of the type of contract concluded the Project Owner must monitor performance. The Service Provider will, in close coordination with the Project Owner, ensure that the agreed milestones are met within the timeframes set out. They will define all procedures required for monitoring the performance of the agreements concluded during the life of the project and prepare a monitoring plan to be accounted for as part of the procurement process once concluded, and the guidance provided by the PPP Unit. The Project Owner will be required to report on a monthly basis to the WRU regarding sub-project progress, with the WRU in turn reporting to the WRC. The Service Provider will also provide regular updates to the DBSA on the status of all project sub-projects. The CC will report twice annually to key stakeholders. The DBSA, in its capacity as Accredited Entity and/or Entry point will use the following controls:

As part of the ongoing project evaluation process, controls will be used to ensure that any materials or technology procured under this sub-project are only used for the purposes intended and are not diverted or misused for unauthorized, improper or illicit purpose.

Sub-component 2.1 Development of the Blended Financial Solutions

It is envisaged that the Project Owner will use a Blended Finance solution, which will make use of: (a) pooled programmatic capital; (b) private capital through different instruments; and (c) credit enhancement instruments from DBSA and others.

Activity 2.1.4: Facilitate development and approved protocols for developing the BFS. A BFS concept has been designed with a sequential logic in mind, using the various equity, debt and guarantee instruments. The BFS is not intended to be a standalone institutional fund or separate incorporated legal entity. Instead, it will act as an umbrella structure that will provide access to blended finance solutions. It is envisaged that all projects under this sub-component will be in accordance with a Blended Finance protocol, to ensure that the objectives of the DBSA and GCF, as well as the Project Owner are met. The Service Provider will facilitate development and approval of protocols that will include approaches to blending capital and how GCF funding is to be blended with private investor funding, to enable private funders and municipalities to lower their risk exposure on a project-by-project basis.


---
**Page 53**
---

project basis: Principles will cover approaches to effective blending, to efficient blending and independent blending.

Activity 2.2.2: Project finance support through the BFS. The BFS is designed to provide blended project finance to a portfolio of sub-projects in the WPR, on a project-by-project basis. Each sub-project will be provided with a separate loan facility, which will have its own terms and conditions reflecting the varying magnitude of that will be allocated to the sub-project through the BFS. The types of financial instruments that may be used include but are not limited to: (i) loans from multilateral development banks, (ii) subordinated loans, equity, and local government budget allocations. In addition to these project finance instruments, the WPR will also provide a blended financing solution for each sub-project in order to further reduce the portion of unsubsidized debt. The deliverables of this activity are: (i) project finance analysis, and blended finance allocation report, per project; (ii) Term Sheet of instrument pricing, per project, as well as (iii) Project Finance Agreement.

Activity 2.2.3: Establish the IBCF function and appoint specialist independent service provider. To give effect to the abovementioned blended capital principles for the WPR, the role of an IBCF is recommended. The role of the IBCF will be to ensure that all projects are aligned with the WPR’s overall objectives. The IBCF and the Blended Finance Solution will be formalised as well the contracting arrangements and period.

Activity 2.2.4: Provide financial structuring services on a project-by-project basis. The IBCF will in terms of its mandate, provide financial structuring services to each sub-project in order to ensure that it meets all the distinct prerequisites, conditions, and due diligence required from the WPR, to enable the sourcing of determination, and flow of different capital sources, across all three components of the BFS, to each WRU separately. The IBCF will be responsible for: (a) identification of the project’s financial structure; (b) allocation of the Programmatic Concessional Financing. Critically, it must include the requirements of determination, and flow of different capital sources, across all three components of the BFS, to each WRU separately; and (c) allocation of the Programmatic Concessional Financing. Critically, it must include the requirements of determination, and flow of different capital sources, across all three components of the BFS, to each WRU separately.

The IBCF mandate, and the usage of the climate finance element of BFS is what sets the Water Reuse Programme apart from other local infrastructure development financing facilities, and is therefore an essential component in ensuring that the programme has a significant impact on capacity in developing countries.

Activity 2.2.5: Audit and report to measure impact of the BFS use and efficacy. The IBCF will create an Impact & ESG Framework with a two-fold purpose: (a) to key measure key impacts metrics that may be relevant to the WPR, and (b) to ensure that all projects are aligned with the WPR’s overall objectives. The IBCF factors into their portfolios. The IBCF will coordinate the continual measurement, verification and reporting of these metrics on a quarterly basis to the WPO. The WPO will be responsible for providing feedback to the IBCF as well as ensuring that all projects are aligned with the WPR’s overall objectives. The IBCF will be responsible to report on all of its understanding on a quarterly basis to the WPO, as well as provide an overview of the BFS’s use, status and efficacy. In support of the WPO, the IBCF will prepare supporting documentation and reports in line with the agreed timelines and milestones set by the IBCF according to their timelines.

Component 3 - Building capacity and creating awareness

A national and regional public awareness and education process will be undertaken in preparation of existing stakeholders and key decision makers on the WPR and its objectives. These knowledge change interventions will undergo the development of awareness regarding climate change impacts at local levels and will support the successful scaling and implementation of water reuse projects in South Africa as an additional benefit to the country’s overall sustainability goals. The DBSA will be responsible for implementing and coordinated by the WPR. While the DBSA will take the lead in the rollout of this component due to its strong institutional position in the water sector, the WPO will underlie supportive buy-in activities. It is important to the important role of IBCF in supporting these important processes, particularly as the water sector leader.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 51 OF 126

## Climate change impacts and the severity of water security issues at local levels requiring innovative water reuse interventions to address local water development needs.

### Public perception, social, cultural and religious beliefs often prevent direct potable reuse options from being considered as a viable option for addressing these challenges.
- Indirect potable reuse and blending options are available that may be socially more acceptable than a direct (top-to-top) potable reuse options (perceptions).
- Current water reuse projects in South Africa have had unintended consequences due to these interventions often being unplanned, uncontrolled and unmonitored with negative environmental and health impacts. These projects have also not been adequately monitored or evaluated for their ability to manage risks, as well as monitor and control quality for realizing impact in adapting to climate change.

The WRIP will develop a strategy on how to educate and communicate the details of water reuse as a means of improving the public's perception of the approach.
- The key drivers affecting water reuse choices include climate change, population growth, urbanization, economic development, competition between water supply options and the social, cultural, and religious perceptions. In support of this, as well informing Government policy on water reuse, the WRIP will develop a strategy to inform and educate the public about the benefits of developing innovation and supporting knowledge exchange.
- Equally, for specific sub-projects this group will be asked to provide strategic review and guidance.
- Lastly, it is essential to maintain strategic partnerships with key stakeholders in order to ensure that South Africa moves towards improved institutional and regulatory frameworks that support adaptation and resilience building.

Activities under this component include the following:

### Sub-component 3.1: Communications and awareness creation
South Africa needs to understand its country's water future. This requires everyone, from the general public to decision makers, to understand South Africa's climate vulnerability and the impacts that this will increasingly have on the water situation.
- Efforts to build this understanding are important in catalyzing broader support for adaptation and resilience building as well as improving the overall approach to climate adaptation and improving water security.

#### Activity 3.1.1: Develop, manage, and maintain the WPRU brand
Communication is a key enabler of the WPRU's work. The WRIP will develop a communications strategy that will ensure that the WPRU's message is consistent with the overall WPRU brand.
- The WPRU and the NRWU will support providing through a content of excellence' will need to be communicated via a communications strategy that aims to improve public knowledge, understanding and awareness about water reuse sub-projects. This will include supporting municipalities, and to empower municipalities to communicate clearly, consistently and transparently about water reuse sub-projects.
- This will include the development of a corporate identity as well as the establishment of a brand ambassador. The exchange will be an essential part of this process.

#### Activity 3.1.2: Development of knowledge products and content
The WPRU will develop a range of materials and knowledge products that will assist in improving the understanding with regard to climate change impacts and water reuse sub-projects as an adaptation response.
- The WPRU and NRWU will work together to ensure that water reuse sub-projects are to be implemented, to build awareness and understanding of their stakeholders.

#### Activity 3.1.3: Undertake outreach and awareness creation events
The WPRU and the NRWU will utilise various climate and water sector events as well as arrange specific market engagement sessions to provide information and create awareness regarding the WPRU and the intervention support that is offered by the WPRU.

#### Activity 3.1.4: Facilitate and manage a community of experts forum
The WRIP will identify and invite members to participate as a Community of Experts. This forum will include water reuse sector experts


---
**Page 55**
---

provide technical support to the preparation, design and structuring of water re-use sub-projects. The Committee of Experts will report to the WPO and will provide strategic guidance and input into the works done by the service providers on water reuse sub-projects, taking into consideration the scope of work and work programme of the WPO. The Committee of Experts will not be able to provide technical support or give strategic guidance and input and it will not be able to alter the scope of work, budget, timeline or design of any project. The Committee of Experts will also not have any role in the selection of service providers.

The participation will be regulated by a terms of reference which will be issued by the WPO to the experts.


Activity 3.1.5: Provide strategic review and guidance on specific sub-projects and interventions. The WPO will facilitate and coordinate the support that the Committee of Experts will provide. This will include such activities as providing technical advice, reviewing and commenting on project proposals, designs, reviewing and making recommendations to the WPO on interim and final deliverables produced by the Service Providers, and providing guidance on any other matter or process that may impact on or be relevant to the programme design, deliverables and intended outcomes.


Sub-component 3.2: Strengthen institutional and regulatory frameworks.

While the DWS will be engaged in the WPO oversight via the OC, there is an ongoing need to continue reaching out to key stakeholders at all levels of government and at local level stakeholders. These engagements will not only build capacity of these key institutions but will also support in engaging with these stakeholders on water reuse as a strategic priority for South Africa in building adaptive capacity and in entrenching water reuse as a strategically important part of climate resilience.

Activity 3.2.1: Develop a key stakeholder engagement framework. The development of an overarching stakeholder engagement framework is essential to guide the approach to ensuring regular interactions and communication with all stakeholders, including government departments, private sector entities, building adaptive capacity and in entrenching water reuse as a strategically important part of climate resilience.

Activity 3.2.2: Undertake targeted strategic outreach. Aligned to the stakeholder engagement framework the WPO will undertake focused engagements with developers, commercial banks, DFIs and other key stakeholders on water reuse sub-projects. These engagements may be undertaken in terms of unlocking financial and technical aspects of sub-projects or in introducing innovative approaches.

Activity 3.2.3: Establish and maintain strategic partnerships with key actors and institutions. The maintenance of key strategic partnerships will be essential and could include Government departments, private sector entities, financiers, developers, amongst others. These partnerships will work collectively at building longer-term institutional capacity as well as addressing specific technical issues and other barriers to the development of adaptive capacity and the roll-out of water reuse sub-projects.

## National mechanism to establish the NWPP and the Memorandum of Agreement for the support of the NWPP between DWS, DBSA and SALGA was signed in May 2022.

As programme custodian the DBS will contract with DBSA, in its capacity as experienced programme developer and as GCF Accredited Entity and Executing Entity for the WPO, to establish and support the implementation of the NWPP. The DBS will also be responsible for establishing the WPO.

At a strategic level, an OC has been established by DBS, SALGA and DBSA which will govern the operations of the WPO. It may include representation by invitation from other stakeholders such as National Treasury, Water Research Commission, Department of Water and Sanitation, and others. The OC will have with sector expertise to guide the OC. Core functions of the OC include: to provide strategic direction; oversee operational management; maintain institutional linkages; and undertake periodic monitoring and evaluation.

At an operational level, the DBS, SALGA and DBSA have by agreement established a programme governance structure for the WPO. The DBS will act as GCF Accredited Entity and Executing Entity and the WPO is established in the DBA. All programme contracting and procurement will be undertaken by DBSA thereby providing shared services and systems that will assist in the operationalization of the WPO. SALGA will provide technical support to the DBS and DBSA, and will also establish a community of experts. It will also have access to the panel of service providers who will be procured for the WPO. The DBS will act as the programme custodian and will manage the overall implementation of projects. Water re-use sub-projects will be implemented by agreement with the local government structures who are empowered by law to implement water reuse sub-projects in their respective jurisdictions.

### Figure 15: WRP Memoir Documents

#### Financial Flows

The WRP is proposing the use of significant GCF concessional capital, which is US Dollar denominated (USD). This concessional capital will be managed by the DBSA as the AE, which is a South African denominated entity. All debt repayments under this agreement will be in ZAR and the currency exposure from this funding request to repay the GCF concessional capital will be managed at a DBSA borrowing level. The WRP will ensure that all debt repayments are made directly into the account of the Accredited Entity (the DBSA), which facilitates the GCF loan(s) into the underlying sub-projects, as required by the GCF funding agreement. The DBSA will then use this money to make payments on behalf of the borrower (underlying project approach). And the DBSA on-lends to the underlying sub-projects, per their Funded Activity Agreement (FAA), which is subject to the GCF capital commitment and repayment terms. This ensures that no losses will be incurred by the GCF due to foreign exchange rate variations as this will be managed by the DBSA's treasury division.

Assuming that an underlying sub-project has been approved and allocated, only then would a pro-rata portion be drawn from the GCF concessional capital account into the DBSA's general USD CFC account. The funds in the DBSA's general USD CFC account will be used as per normal. A deal will be booked on treasury system (Quantum) containing the project name, project ID, project support bank account, SARF approval number etc. Soon thereafter, it is assumed that a utilisation request would be received from sub-borrower.

The WRP's funding flows are provided in Figure 16.

## WRP Funding Flows

It is important to note that:

1. The GCF does not directly finance the underlying water reuse sub-projects. The GCF provides loans (at a Programme Level) to the Accredited Entity (the DBSA), who facilitates the GCF Concessional loan(s) into the underlying sub-projects, as required. The GCF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement with the DBSA for the disbursement of the concessional capital from the GCF to the DBSA subject to the repayment risk of Loans. The GCF will provide the Concessional Loans in USD to DBSA in two tranches (i) a public sector tranche amounting to USD130m and ii) a private sector tranche amounting to USD270m. The concessional capital provided by the GCF will be used to finance water reuse sub-projects as fixed in SA Rand (ZAR), which is overseen by the DBSA. The DBSA will enter into an agreement with the project developer for the provision of the ZAR equivalent of USD37m for project preparation (USD 30m), capacity development (USD5 m) and WRP office core operating costs (USD2m). The swap equivalent pricing will be obtained from a commercial bank, but no hedging agreements will require to be entered into by the DBSA. The GCF will not provide any funding for the currency exposure from this funding required to repay the GCF concessional capital will be managed by DBSA. The DBSA shall transfer to the GCF all loan repayments in US Dollars and manage the foreign exchange risk associated with such payments.
2. A GCF grant of USD 35 m is primarily intended for project preparation (USD 30m) and capacity development (USD 5m). The ZAR equivalent of USD 37m from the GCF concessional funding will co-finance the ZAR equivalent of USD 37m through Grant/Budget allocations for project preparation (USD 30m), capacity develop (USD 5 m) and WRP office core operating costs (USD2m). The legal and contractual arrangements for the WRP are provided in Figure 17, below.

## Legal and contractual arrangements

There is likely to be significant risk from fluctuating currency between ZAR and USD, over the repayment periods of the debt capital (even if the relevant currency management arrangements are based on the DBSA's extensive experience in managing hard currency denominated funding provided by International Finance Institutions. This risk may be exacerbated where the relevant currency management arrangements are not fully effective to mitigate short term. A shortfall in ZAR may arise as a result of the Accredited Entity having to unwind relevant currency management arrangements and having to convert relevant ZAR amounts recovered from or paid by the relevant Borrower into USD, with the resulting foreign exchange solution being provided by the GCF Loan to the relevant Sub-Borrower, and for and prepayment by the relevant Borrower, in each case under a Sub-Loan, where the relevant Borrower has not received sufficient ZAR amounts from the Sub-Borrower. The Accredited Entity would have received pursuant to the relevant currency management arrangements had the Sub-Borrower paid relevant amounts when due under the relevant Sub-Loan. The following foreign exchange solution will apply:

* **If**, during the first nine (9) years (i.e., end of year 9) following the effectiveness of the FAA (the "Reserve Period"), a Residual foreign exchange shortfall materializes, the relevant amount shall, in accordance with the terms and conditions of the GCF Loan, be used to reduce the Available Reserve Amount to the extent that the Available Reserve Amount is sufficient (such such deduction being a reduction in the Available Reserve Amount). The Accredited Entity may then use the Reduced Residual foreign exchange shortfall (to the extent that the Available Reserve Amount is sufficient). Each Reserve Utilization shall reduce the available commitment under the GCF Loan by an equivalent amount.

* **If** at the end of the Reserve Period, the Available Reserve Amount is more than zero US Dollars (USD), then the difference between the Available Reserve Amount and USD 0.00 shall be used by the Accredited Entity until the closing date of the GCF Loan (closing date to be defined in the FAA).

* **If** the Available Reserve Amount falls to zero USD (USD 0), the risk of any subsequent Residual foreign exchange shortfall will be borne by the Borrower.

The terms of the public sector sub-loans shall comply with the following provisions given in Table 12.

# Table 12: Terms of public sector Sub-Loans (Component D)

## Facility Type
Public Sector Sub-Loan

### Approval Process
The Sub-Loan will be assessed by the WFP Climate Screening Committee which will assess applications; the committee will include internal (DSSA) and external members. The committee will also review the project proposal for its alignment with the WFP’s climate change basis, based on the relevant climate change information alongside other development considerations.

Projects which are able to demonstrate that climate change is a significant factor in water security and/or food security will be prioritized for funding. Projects which complete their project preparation phase (PP) but with insufficient climate change or water demand information will be reviewed for potential project programming later on, subject to the availability of funds.

All projects will undergo final review and approval by the WFP Oversight Committee. The Accredited Entity Investment Committee will have the final decision on the approval of Sub-Loans and will also be responsible for the disbursement of the loan.

The Accredited Entity will act as the lender of record of the Loan.

## 3. Amount
The aggregate amount of the GCF Loan can not be lent by the Accredited Entity for the Public Sector Sub-Loans will be up to one hundred and fifty million Dollars (USD 150,000,000).

For any Sub-Project, the committed concessional debt, including from DFIs, shall not exceed the commitment level of the GCF Loan.

In instances where DBSA or other Co-financers will provide credit enhancement into Sub-Projects, the DBSA loan and/or other subordinated loans will be on pari passu with GCF.

The amount of each individual Sub-Loan at the time of its commitment shall not exceed the lesser of:
(1) 50% of the cost of the Sub-Project itself; or
(2) USD 30 million

The credit exposure (loan co-financing ratio) for each investor in the Facility is GCF, the Accredited Entity and any other Co-financer shall not exceed 75% of the total project cost. The loan shall be maintained throughout the availability period of the Loan.

## 4. Purpose
Financing of public sector Sub-Projects that meet the Eligibility Criteria of the Programme.

## 5. Borrower
Public Sector Sub-Borrowers must have the Eligibility Criteria of the Programme.

## 7. Currency
ZAR

## 8. Repayment Schedule
Quarterly or semi-annually for all refinances during the relevant tenor of the Public Sector Sub-Loan, and thereafter on a monthly basis until maturity.

## 9. Pricing
Pricing shall comply with the principles indicated in item 14(q)(ii) of this Term Sheet and the principle of risk-adjusted pricing.

## 10. Tenor
In each Sub-Project, the tenor of the Public Sector Sub-Loan shall not exceed the lesser of:
1) Twenty (20) years from the effectiveness of the relevant Public Sector Sub-Loan Agreement.
2) The tenor of the Water Purchase Agreement (WPA) for the relevant Sub-Project.
3) The term of the loan provided by any other Co-financer.

The remaining tenor of the FAA.

## 11. Grace Period
Projects under the Public Sector Sub-Loan is subject to a maximum grace period of three (3) years, to be decided on case by case basis by the Accredited Entity taking into account the needs of the Sub-Project and the availability of funds. The grace period will commence from the date of disbursement.

Interest repayment of the Loan, as set out in this Term Sheet shall be made:
* a maximum grace period of three (3) years.
* The Sub-Projects Commercial Operations date

12. **Currency conversions** At each Disbursement by the DSBA will enter into a currency management solution covering the USD to GCF or GCF to USD exchange rate for a particular disbursement to be used throughout the duration of the facility.

13. **Pre-payment** The Accredited Entity shall decide on any applicable prepayment penalty or fee. Any prepayment penalties or fees charged by the Accredited Entity shall apply proportionally to each co-financed transaction.

14. **Security structure** Standard and customary for this type of international project finance transactions of this nature, including but not limited to: (a) a security interest in all assets of the Sub-Project; (b) a pledge holding on the project, assignment of project contracts, documents and approvals.

15. **Fees** The DSBA shall be entitled to receive fees for services rendered starting from financial close of the relevant Public Sector Debt Loan, on the commencement of the drawdown period or at such other time as may be agreed between the DSBA and GCF. Upfront fees are 0.2% of the loan amount. Upfront fees will not apply if the loan amount is less than $15 million. The DSBA shall be entitled to receive a fee for services rendered starting from financial close of the relevant Public Sector Debt Loan, on the commencement of the drawdown period or at such other time as may be agreed between the DSBA and GCF. Upfront fees are 0.2% of the loan amount.

16. **Waterfall provisions** Any cash surplus after meeting all (i) operating and statutory expenses; (ii) senior debt obligations due to Borrower’s parent or any related party; (iii) interest on the DSBA Loan; (iv) interest on the GCF Loan; (v) Creation and/or top-up of reserve accounts (including but not limited to DSBA, any other applicable reserves); (vi) repayment of the DSBA Loan in accordance with the terms set out above; (vii) Cash sweep in the following manner: Pro-rata between the debt providers i.e. LFA and the Accredited Entity, based on their respective shareholding in the Sub-Project. The pro-rata shareholding is determined by the debt ratio/equity ratio of the relevant Sub-Project.

17. **Eligibility criteria** The Accredited Entity shall be required to meet all eligibility requirements with the Eligibility Criteria.

The structuring criteria for each sub-project shall include customary requirements for international project finance transactions or balance sheet lending transactions. Total Project Debt (including any debt and equity) shall not exceed the following considerations:

- (a) The amount that is equal to a maximum Total Debt Service Coverage Ratio (TDSR) of 120:35 as at Borrowers, provided that:
    - A minimum TDSR of 80:35 will apply for any Sub-Project where the relevant Borrower has an equity interest greater than five percent (5%) of the relevant outstanding amount of the total debt is repaid which point the DSBA Loan is drawn down.

## Current Risk Insurance
The Accredited Entity shall include insurance conditions (project) insurance against loss, damage and destruction of the project assets, including any replacement costs, in the terms and conditions for the Sector Sub-loans in financing agreements and such provisions shall be exercised in a manner which is consistent with the requirements of the GCF.

## Sub-Projects
The Sub-Projects shall maintain project insurance against loss, damage and liability in a manner and with insurance coverage levels that are consistent with the requirements of the Sector Sub-Loan.

## Other terms
Substitution of payments
To the extent losses occur in excess of the limit (i.e. the Debt Service Reserve Account ("DSRA")), those losses will be allocated to any Co-financing parties, if any, and, if none, to the Borrower. To the extent that the DSRA is insufficient to cover all such losses, the Borrower shall provide substituted loan funds in accordance with the terms of the Sub-Project.

## Treatment of losses
To the extent losses occur in excess of the limit (i.e. the Debt Service Reserve Account ("DSRA")), those losses will be allocated to any Co-financing parties, if any, and, if none, to the Borrower. To the extent that the DSRA is insufficient to cover all such losses, the Borrower shall provide substituted loan funds in accordance with the terms of the Sub-Project.

## Reserve Accounts
The Debt Service Reserve Account ("DSRA" will be set up 6 months at least of total debt service for loan: interest and principal) to ensure that the DSRA is sufficient to cover operation and maintenance costs of the Sub-Project.

## Payment of Interest and/or Principal Frequency
Interest under the revolving portion under the Loan shall begin to accrue as of the date of first drawdown. The Borrower will be responsible for paying interest on the revolving portion, quarterly in arrears.

## Taxation
The Accredited Entity shall provide gross-up (interest or other approved payments) in the relevant legal agreements for the Loans, so that it receives the full amount of Reimbursed Funds under the GCF Trust Facility.

## Purpose of the Facility

### Sub-Borrower
- **DBSA**
- **Private Sector Borrowers** that meet the Eligibility Criteria of the Programme.

### Loan Tenure
Quarterly or semi-annually for all reflores under the relevant tenor of the Private Sector Sub-Loan.

### Repayment Schedule
The repayment schedule will be determined by the terms and conditions of each Sub-Project.

## Pricing

Pricing shall comply with the principles indicated in Item 14 (prc) of this Term Sheet and the principles set out below:

## Tenor

In each Sub-Project, the tenor of the Private Sector Sub-Loan shall not exceed the lesser of:
- (a) Twenty (20) years from the effectiveness of the relevant Private Sector Sub-Loan Agreement;
- (b) The maximum term of the relevant Project Agreement (as provided in the relevant Sub-Loan Pogramme);
- (c) The maximum term of the F&A.

## Grace Period

Principal repayment of the Loans will be subject to a maximum grace period of three years, to be determined by the terms and conditions of each Sub-Project. For the avoidance of doubt as to the grace period does not apply for interest payments and fees.

Interest repayment of the Loan shall be decided on case by case basis by the Accredited Entity taking into account the needs of the Sub-Project but shall not exceed the lesser of:
- (a) The Sub-Project Completion Operational date;
- (b) The expiry date of the Facility.

## Currency Conversions

At each drawdown, the Borrower will be provided with currency management solution converting the USD received from GCF to ZAR, securing an exchange rate for a particular disbursement to be used for the duration of the facility.

## Tranches and Prepayments

The Accredited Entity shall decide on any applicable prepayment penalty or fee. Any prepayment penalties or fees charged by the Accredited Entity shall apply proportionally to each co-financed loan.

## Security

Standard and customary for the type of international project finance transactions of this nature, including but not limited to:
- (a) A pledge over all assets owned by the Borrower;
- (b) A charge over all receivables due from the Borrower;

## Fees

- **Commitment fee** of minimum 0.25% (based on the year(s) passed) accruing starting from financial close date and payable in arrears at the end of each quarter.
- **Private Sector Sub-Loan**, to be payable on each payment date, together with interest rate payments.

- **Upfront fees**: an amount to be decided by the Accredited Entity which shall not exceed one per cent (1%) of the relevant loan amount.

## Additional Fees

In case any additional fees are charged by the Accredited Entity under the Loans, the Accreditated Entity will charge such fees on a pro rata basis in accordance with the ratio between the Accredited Entity proportionally to the amount of their co-financed/re-channeled loans under the Loan.

## Upfront Fees and other fees

If any is to share pre-rate between the DBSA and GCF, the Borrower will be required to pay upfront fees and other fees as per the terms agreed with the DBSA and GCF.

## Waterfall provisions

Any amounts due from the Borrower under the Loans (including but not limited to interest due to LFs, and all obligations) to the Accredited Entity under the Loans, will be deployed by the Borrower in accordance with the following waterfall:
- (1) Creation and/or use of reserve accounts (including but not limited to DSIA, any other reserves or escrow accounts);
- (2) Repayment of any amounts due from the Borrower under the Loan provisions (i.e. LFs and the Accredited Entity as the lender of record of the Loans) for the repayment of the relevant equity ratio on the relevant Sub-Project.

# Eligibility criteria

The Accredited Entity shall only approve Sub-Projects that comply with the Eligibility Criteria.

## Structuring criteria

The structuring criteria for each sub-project shall include customary requirements for international development finance institutions and/or other financial intermediaries, including:

* Total Borrower debt (i.e. senior and subordinated) to be sized based on the following considerations:
    * The amount that results in a minimum Total Debt Service Coverage Ratio (TDSR) (in relation to the relevant Loan) of 1.25x or more.
    * A maximum DSCR of six (6) times for the first two months of total debt service for the Loan until such time as the TDSR is met, and thereafter a minimum DSCR of four (4) times at which point the DSCR requirement may reduce to a maximum of six (6) times debt service coverage.
    * A maximum loan term of 15 years.

## Equity support

* Minimum equity of twenty percent (20%) of total Cost Project costs provided by the relevant Equity Provider.
* The base equity will be either:
    * Contributed as part of the Sub-Project as a condition presented to the first disbursement under the relevant Loan; or
    * contributed in the form of debt disbursements under the relevant Loan, subject to the balance supported by the project cash flow and any other sources of funding acceptable to the Accredited Entity. Note: such credit shall not be available to recourse Sub-Project costs.

In addition to the base equity, on a case-by-case basis the Accredited Entity may assess the need for have done equity (i.e., equity that is provided by the relevant Equity Provider in advance of project operations).

## Share retention

The participating sector investor and/or participating Municipality will together, directly or indirectly, own/manage an interest of one hundred percent (100%) of all equity interests in the Sub-Project. All equity interests in the Sub-Project shall be held by the relevant Equity Provider.

In addition to the base equity, the Accredited Entity may assess the need for have done equity (i.e., equity that is provided by the relevant Equity Provider in advance of project operations).

## Restricted payments

Any sale of the economic interest by the participating sector investor and/or participating Municipality will be subject to prior written approval of the Accredited Entity and shall be to such a party which, at all times during the term of the Sub-Project, is in compliance with the terms and conditions set out in this document. For the avoidance of doubt, sales made by a participating private sector investor and/or participating Municipality will not be subject to prior written approval of the Accredited Entity.

Any sale of the economic interest by the participating sector investor and/or participating Municipality shall be executed in a manner which is not prejudicial to the interests of the Accredited Entity. No dividends, bonuses or extraordinary compensation shall be permitted to be declared or paid under any circumstances other than as set out in this document.

The following conditions must be met before any sale of economic interest by the participating sector investor and/or participating Municipality:

* (a) the construction phase completion has occurred;
* (b) the project has been financed with a Project DSIGC that is not less than 1.25x;
* (c) all financial accounts filed under the terms of GCF Loans have been made according to the requirements set out in this document; and
* (d) no event of default or of non-compliance has occurred and is continuing under any other conditions to be added based on the specific structure of each Sub-Project;
* (e) the payment is in line with the cash sweep provisions under item 16.

## Pre-execution conditions

The Accredited Entity shall endeavor to include pre-execution conditions in financing agreements and/or loan documentation for a Sub-Project, as appropriate. These conditions may be included:

* Prior to or at the time of execution of loan documentation for a Sub-Project, all the required debt shall be fully and legally committed to the Sub-Project.

**Figure 18:** Simplified project finance approach

The simplified approach has the following key features:

* **Ownership:** The ownership model will be either a local government authority (municipality) or special purpose vehicle (SPV)—the latter comprising municipal, the private sector, and/or possibly a national government entity (depending on size). In both instances, the municipalities will be managing the investment through their own resources, which may include cash, debt, land, in-kind human resources, and possibly capital budgets. It is also assumed that the owner will be the primary risk bearer.
* **Engineering Procurement Contract (EPC) / Operator:** The construction and operations of the Water Reuse sub-projects will be undertaken by the private sector. This can either be undertaken through a traditional EPC contract or an operator agreement, depending on the specific financing structure of the sub-project. The operators of the plant, against a strict performance specification, will be responsible for the operation and maintenance.

* **Capex:** A capex value of USD $69 million has been used to illustrate the splits between different capital sources. The simplified sub-project finance approach assumes that the opex will be covered through revenue streams.

* **Equity / Grant:** The split between debt and equity based upon desktop research and informal market sourcing. This is a very rough estimate, as there are no formal studies available. It is the instance when the project owner is likely to be an SPV. However, when the sub-project is wholly owned by the municipality, the capex will be fully funded from Capex. The equity will be covered through a budgeted grant, in keeping with their integrated development planning. In this scenario, the same split is applied in the simplified project finance approach.

* **Debt:** The key feature of the simplified project finance approach is where there is a significant opportunity to apply blended finance principles. A distinction is made between three types of debt: (a) Senior, (b) Subordinated, and (c) Concessional. These can be applied differentially.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 66 of 152

## Guarantor as a guarantee instrument to facilitate private sector investment into water reuse sub-projects forms part of the financial structure of the WPF. Potentiel funders and guarantor providers are being approached to fund the guarantee instrument.

### Blended Finance Model
The blended finance model is designed to achieve a blended finance approach which can be achieved by using various equity, debt and guarantee instruments. Importantly, the instruments are not mutually exclusive but rather complementary in nature. The use of guarantees as an instrument in bringing in subordinated debt will likely reduce the interest rate on senior commercial debt, thereby increasing the attractiveness of senior commercial debt. The appraisal will be to maximize principal investment in the WRFIP and ensure that the blended finance model is applied. Therefore, the senior, commercial debt is prioritised higher than all other debt types, and the Programme Financial Model has been designed to solve for a higher private capital split in each underlying sub-project.

A cut-off date for project preparation on a project-by-project basis will be determined project-by-project basis but at minimum on funding from non-project specific funds will be 10%. For more financially strong municipalities a higher percentage will required (up to a maximum of 50%).

## Proposed Partial Guarantee
Partial credit guarantees (PCG) are widely used internationally to assist in alleviating funding constraints faced in public infrastructure projects, specifically in environments where short to medium term liquidity risk is high. In some cases, PCGs are issued by the project owner or their parent company, or they may be provided by a third party. In South Africa most project owners have limited or no access to affordable, unsecured funding and as such, the use of PCGs is not common practice in the country. The combination of municipal and provincial level creates an added level of complexity and risk for funders who will look to the municipality/province to cover the risk of repayment.

The PCG is designed specifically for the purpose of providing security to the lenders by substituting part of the risk of the project owner and public sector contracting partner with that of the issuer of the PCG. The guarantee is envisaged to be for adverse market events only, during the operations period of the underlying sub-projects, which will be defined as the period from the date of completion until the end of the 20-year repayment period.

In addition, the guarantee fund envisaged for the WIRP also serves to lower the risk to both the lender and project owner in respect of delayed payments by the public sector (short term liquidity risk). Lastly, the partial credit guarantee also substitutes a portion of the lender’s risk in the event of default.


## Structure
It is envisaged that the guarantee will be a fully cash collateralized guarantee. The security of the PCG is provided to the senior debt issued via the blended finance solution. It will be administered and managed by the DBSA, and the DBSA will be the guarantor on record for each of the sub-projects that require a guarantee instrument. The guarantee fund will be established in accordance with the terms set out in the Blended Finance Solution. The PCG’s cash collateral should only be reduced under one of the following adverse events:

* A liquidity event;
* A public sector default event; or

#### Figure19 Proposed early partial credit guarantee concept
With reference to the PCG, the DSBA will open a separate USD account with a reputable commercial bank. The guarantee shall be cash collateralized and provided through a letter of credit from the selected commercial banks. The guarantee amount is calculated as 50% of the total loan amount. They require that additional due diligence (DQ) is conducted on the DBSA, which should be avoided to ensure the process is simpler. The cash collateral is supplied by a development corporation , according to pre-agreed terms.

The selected commercial banks costs to custody the guarantee cash collateral will be included in DSBA cost. The DSBA will open a separate ZAR account with a reputable commercial bank. The guarantee amount on that is swapped into ZAR and disbursed according to the guarantee agreement at a sub-project level.

Furthermore, it is envisaged that the guarantee fund will support project owners during a “liquidity event”. The Liquid Portion is a small portion of the total cash collateral that is available to cover a “liquidity event”. This is because the Project Owner has already made their monthly loan repayment on time, meaning the Project Owner can make his/her loan repayment on time. In this instance, the liquid portion covers this month’s loan repayment and recovers the exact cost from the Project Owner when they are called upon.

The Liquid Portion is shown as distinct from the total cash collateral, because it needs to be available to be paid out as soon as possible (ie within 24 hours). This is because the Project Owner may have a fixed-income, below-market interest rate loan. As a default event typically takes many months to materialize. This is to ensure the cash collateral generates meaningful interest when it is not being called upon. The cash collateral should be protected against the erosive effect of inflation. If the interest rates on the cash collateral are positive in real terms, the fund’s collateral should be protected against the erosive effect of inflation.

### Adverse Events
