# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 88 of 150

The capital risk of the PCGF will be exposed to a number of risks, including credit risk in respect of the project owner who is entering into a loan; credit risk of the municipality/province that will be contracting with the project owner; and liquidity risk in respect of temporary non-payment by a municipality or province.

Many of these risks can be managed. The PCGF should aim to diversify its portfolio as much as possible by avoiding significant exposures to the following categories:

* A single municipality, and
* A single project owner

The PCGF will take on the following obligations in the event of a liquidity event or default (Table 14).

| Event | Description | Obligation of the PCGF | Project Owner's obligation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Liquidity event | Payment from public sector entity to the project owner is delayed by more than one day (for example, 60 days) | The PCGF will hold capital and interest | The project owner remains liable for outstanding debt and interest upon receipt of overdue payments() to the PCGF |
| Public sector default event | Payment from the public sector entity to the project owner is delayed by more than six months | The full amount guaranteed, minus any payments made in respect of the collateral, will be transferred from the PCGF to the municipality/province. Payments will be capped at the outstanding debt and interest on this portion | The project owner remains liable for repaying outstanding debt and interest upon receipt of overdue payments() to the PCGF |
| Project Owner default event | The project owner fails to meet debt repayments in the absence of a public sector default event | The full amount guaranteed can be transferred from collateral held by the PCGF to the municipality/province to reduce the project loan. | The project owner remains liable for repaying outstanding debt and interest upon receipt of overdue payments() to the PCGF |

According to the Programme Financial Model, by combining GCF concessional funding with a partial credit guarantee, the minimum GCF funding requirement decreases by 7%, to R295 million or US$ 21 million, representing 20.7% of total project-related debt funding or 15.3% of total project-related capital at the point where the PCGF is fully funded. The maximum GCF concessional funding requirement increases from 48% to 60%, with concessional cash as a proportion of the total implementation capital: however, in exceptional cases, up to 50% will be reviewed where a particular sub-project requires it to ensure the ongoing viability of the entire WRP.

**AE: Executing Entity Experience**

DBSA is a national entity based in South Africa with national activity (www.dbsa.co.za). It is a development finance institution that has been established since 1962. DBSA provides infrastructure project financing to finance both private and public sector activities at national and regional levels in Africa. DBSA provides sustainable infrastructure project preparation, finance and implementation support in order to improve the population's quality of life, accelerating the sustainable reduction of poverty and inequality. DBSA has been a member of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) since 2015 and from April 2017 it has been implementing the Green Fund, with a focus on the climate finance dimension.

From an infrastructure development and service delivery perspective, DBSA has a track record in providing technical assistance to governments for project preparation, acceleration and monetization of development impact projects from its experience in conceptualising, establishing and implementing large-scale national and regional programmes, including the Gaden Housing Infrastructure Programme. DBSA was accredited by the GCF in 2016, for large projects.


---
**Page 72**
---

From a climate finance perspective, it is currently implementing as Accredited Entity and Executing Entity for two GCF approved proposals, including the Imbedded Generation Investment Programme (FP106 – 2019) and the DBSA Climate Finance Facility (FP588 – 2018). (Development Bank of South Africa [DBSA])

DBSA as accredited entity will ensure monitoring and reporting as required by the GCF, including the participation of Climate Finance Unit and the Monitoring and Evaluations Unit of the DBSA. The DBSA will be responsible for ensuring that all relevant requirements are met in terms of the ESIA and ESMP, which has been successfully done for other programmes such as the successful Renewable Energy Independent Power Producer (REIPP) Programme. The DBSA will also ensure that the project is aligned with the principles of WRP and will guide such aspects as recruitment and supply chain management and procurement. Likewise, the DBSA’s policies with regards to gender, sexual harassment and sexual exploitation and abuse, amongst other issues are in place.

DBSA will also coordinate the work of third-party auditors engaged in the Programme to monitor and evaluate results. During the programme implementation DBSA will ensure that any subsidiary agreement that they enter into with a project entity or sub-contractor is subject to the terms and conditions of the GCF, including the terms and conditions of the FAAs that may be applicable or relevant to the Executing Entity or the National Designated Authority.

### Sub-project compliance with Environment and Social Monitoring Framework (ESMF)

The ESMF which has been prepared for the WRP aims to avoid and minimize negative environmental and social (E&S) impacts and to enhance positive aspects of the Programme. The ESMF contains environmental and social safeguard policies, including those related to gender equality, health and safety at work, etc., that the DBSA and the project owners (municipalities) are committed and able to comply with the revised GCF Environmental and Social Policies[ES-Policy2021:2018] and DBSA Environmental and Social Guidelines[ESG2021:2019]. The ESMF will be prepared at each sub-project level. The WRP will provide support to Municipalities to prepare project proposals that are aligned with the ESMF, and the ESIA and ESMP will be prepared in line with this.

At sub-project levels the Social and Gender Oversight Officer and the Environmental Control Office (Figure 26) will report regularly on adherence and compliance with the ESMF requirements and safeguards at both programme and sub-project levels.

#### I.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

According to a study commissioned by Business Leadership South Africa, infrastructure investment in South Africa has fallen sharply over the past six years, from above 20% of Gross Domestic Product (GDP) in 2015 to below 18% in 2019. From the National Development Plan's target of 30% of GDP further ahead, and although significant progress has been made on infrastructure development since 2014, the focus of continued investment has primarily been on renewable energy projects in South Africa through the South African Renewable Energy Independent Power Producer Procurement Programme (REIPPPP). In addition to this Programme, the development of bankable projects, especially in the water and water reuse sector, has been challenging.

The commitment of South Africa through local implementing municipalities have little financial capacity to meet the initial costs of establishing a programme of this nature. Municipal-scale projects are capital-intensive and access to funding can be a major constraint, particularly for small, less financially stable municipalities. The cost of infrastructure projects is also highly variable depending on the quality of materials used.

Capital costs range significantly depending on efficient quality, treatment requirements and the reuse sector. For example, the cost per cubic meter of water produced in South Africa ranges from R120 million per Ml/d produced, while these for inorganic effluent cost in the order of R 1 to 15 million per ML/d produced (Unisa, 2019). The need for significant concessional funding, in addition to the concessional loan portion of the requested GCF funding is necessary to overcome a combination of financial, technical, institutional and market barriers that together prevent climate-vulnerable locations in South Africa from accessing infrastructure finance.

Existing capital pools in SA are at sign of buyer market potential for infrastructure investing. Collectively, the deployment of private sector balance sheets is sized at over ZAR 20 billion). More than 80% of invested capital pools are concentrated with Banks (36%), Pension Funds (29.5%), and Life Insurers (17%). The duration of assets held may vary; opportunities exist for this capital to find new assets as infrastructure.

B

## South African Funding Pools (Sources: SAVCA, National Treasury, ABSA)

Blended finance in the form of concessionary funding with private sector finance will enhance government capacity to increase investment into the water sector. This intervention is required at a time when little direct public sector financing for water infrastructure projects is available. According to the World Bank4, National Treasury (NT) of South Africa is in the process of reviewing municipal and national PPP frameworks to leverage greater public-private partnerships (PPPs). In its latest 2012 Budget Review5, NT affirms that it will continue to support the development of a framework for the financing of private sector participation. According to NT, the Medium Term Estimates Framework (MTEF) investment in water over one year is R3 billion. This level of investment alone would not be sufficient and require substantial gearing from private sources and concessions if it is to maximise impact on the sector.

Water PPP projects in South Africa have struggled to attract investment from the private sector, as contrasted with other countries such as Brazil where there has been a significant increase in private sector participation. In future, the sector will require more early-stage project development support from DFIs to de-risk projects, paving the way for sound bankable assessments. This constraint is further compounded by the lack of consistent and reliable data on the Weighted Average Cost of Capital (WACC) on projects, making projects more economically viable.

## Figure 23: Private Sector Financial Exposures to Infrastructure Section (Source: ABBA, 2021)

In the case of the WPP's proposed Blended Finance Solution, GCF concessional funding will coexist with increased levels of private sector finance. Private financiers will be able to leverage increased exposures to climate change adaptation projects on the back of strong anchor investors, such as the GCF and DFIs. The expected result is a significant increase in the volume of private capital available for investment, increasing confidence and raising the profile of water projects amongst willing investors. A number of letters of support for the WPP by potential investors has been appended in the Annexures.

The blended finance solution will allow for increased levels of GCF concessional funding without crowding out the private sector. Lessons learnt from the Renewable Energy Independent Power Producer Programme (REIPPPP) have demonstrated that infrastructure projects that overcome construction phase risk to become operational and generate revenue can be refinanced in both primary and secondary markets. Figure 23 illustrates the transition of typical infrastructure project finance through a series of debt instruments, including concessional loans, equity, bonds, and similar listed instruments, crowding in other institutional investors, thereby recycling capital for further use in other projects. Figure 24 provides a listing of recent green bonds issued by both the private and public sector, demonstrating that there is already significant interest from investors in this market. This creates an ecosystem that is self-reinforcing, with new instruments and different sources of capital, creating opportunity where there is currently no market niche.

## Figure 24: SA Private Sector Financial Exposures to Infrastructure Sectors (ASISA, 2021)

Further on the regulatory environment for development and SA supporting further investment in infrastructure is encouraging. The market regulator has taken steps to increase investment in infrastructure.

On 01 January 2023, pension funds will be able to invest 45% of Assets Under Management (AUM) into infrastructure assets. This is a significant step forward as it allows for greater diversification and liquidity under-funded sector.

Water infrastructure therefore requires a compelling investment thesis if it is to attract sufficient private sector funding. The South African water sector has been underfunded historically, given that SA is a water scarce country, with amplified risks brought by climate change hazards. Demand for secondary market infrastructure assets is expected to increase, supporting the business case for further supply.


---
**Page 77**
---

According to the World Bank, the Build Operate Transfer (BOT) model is a preferred financing vehicle favoured by the private sector in the circular economy. As per the GCF Funding Proposal, we expect to see the use of SPV structures supporting this claim, as the Water Reuse Programme increases the supply of unconventional water resources.

Overall, the private sector market in SA is well regulated with financial service providers requiring licenses and regulatory approval in order to legally operate. Capital markets possess the depth and track-record to raise capital for large infrastructure projects. The South African Infrastructure Fund (SAIF) is showing support for a growing infrastructure investing market to come. The existing market has room for further expansion, but capital is scarce for the bulk of capital requirements of the WRP.

The Water Reuse Programme has thus critiqued that it cannot create the new asset class and allow for the diversification and augmentation of South Africa’s water mix. In response to the impacts of climate change on the water resources in South Africa, only through the leveraging of GCF funding will the requirements of the WRP be met. This will lead to the creation of scaled development of water reuse sub projects nationally and ultimately improving South Africa's resilience.

Broadly, GCF funding will allow for three key enablers to be realised, namely:

- Enabling an environment providing enhanced access to climate capital: By leveraging the GCF funds, it is expected that this will enable private funds and municipalities to lower their risk exposure on a project-by-project basis. Increased participation from the private sector in these projects will lead to more efficient use of resources and greater financial returns for investors without continued GCF participation. However, GCF funding is critical to initially mobilise the Programme and create the new asset class for future participation by the private sector.
- Enabling a replicable approach to water reuse implementation: The cost of long-term infrastructure financing, the cost of finance amongst the biggest obstacles to capital formation. GCF funding will be used to implement initial pilot concept projects that enable the programme to lead to reduced costs in terms of both financial and non-financial costs. This will reduce the overall cost of financing on a project level to be lowered, and these lower financing costs (and potential savings) can then be passed onto municipalities.
- Enabling a programmatic approach to water reuse implementation: The establishment of a replicable programmatic approach to the development, finance and implementation of water reuse infrastructure projects will enable South Africa’s most vulnerable municipalities in South Africa's most vulnerable municipalities to enhance water security and climate resilience.

The quantum of GCF funding for the Programme will be managed by the AE on a project-by-project basis. GCF Funding will be used to implement initial pilot concept projects that enable the programme to lead to reduced costs in terms of both financial and non-financial costs. This will reduce the overall cost of financing on a project level to be lowered, and these lower financing costs (and potential savings) can then be passed onto municipalities.

Accordingly, the GCF funding is expected to be catalytic in enabling a public perception shift in relation to water reuse and bringing about a transformation in the manner in which South Africa manages and utilises its wastewater. While South Africa is currently classified as an upper middle-income country (World Bank, 2020), South


---
**Page 78**
---

Africa continues to face increasing levels of socio-economic pressures due to growing unemployment, political instability, and corruption.

As a result, South Africa’s macroeconomic indicators have weakened (including the downgrading of national credit rating to sub-investment grade by major rating agencies), with the deficit rising in financing of infrastructure. The WRP is therefore increasingly challenging for governments. GDP growth which averaged 3.5 percent during both 2001 and 2010, has since fallen to an average of circa 1.5% between 2011 and 2014. This trend is expected to continue in the short term. In addition, many municipalities face considerable economic pressure, reducing overall public financial availability to support the planning and execution of projects under the WRP. The investment by GCF is thus viewed as being critical in the establishment of this Programme.

The proposed WIP is critical for enabling municipalities to better manage water wastage and for enabling the crowding-in of private finance to facilitate investments into water reuse infrastructure and increase water resources. This will be achieved through a combination of demand-side management, supply-side management and potentially ecosystem based approaches, thus directly (positively) impacting on the agricultural sector of South Africa. The WIP also has a strong focus on the creation of green jobs in the micro-enterprise fabric, as well as its central role in food security, rural development and employment creation.

South Africa also has a high proportion of urban and rural communities living in informal settlements that have no access to basic services such as water and sanitation. These communities are often poor, unemployed, women and children are often most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change as they are often more reliant on access to natural resources that are required to support livelihoods. These communities are often also located in areas that are prone to flooding or drought, which can be exacerbated by climate-related extreme risk. The request for financing to GCF is premised on the understanding that an increasing number of South Africans will be reduced access to portable water as a result of climate change related assets.

It is well noted that national governments, through their banks, have a critical role to play in supporting investments into water infrastructure and the WRP. However, many of these institutions have historically been lacking sectors such as the water and reuse, as these sectors may be viewed as unprofitable, and are seen as “a public good”. This lack of investment over time significantly contributes towards the development of a fragmented water sector in South Africa, which is not conducive to innovation or private sector innovation. Thus, GCF funding, coupled with state action will play an extremely important role in catalyzing private sector investment.

The AE is seeking to secure funding through grants and concessional loans from GCF. Without GCF involvement to catalyse this Programme and enable co-funding, South Africa will be unable to undertake the scaling up of existing projects and programmes that are already being implemented by municipalities for water access in the drier, more climate vulnerable South Western regions of the country. GCF support for the WRP will enable additional investments that will allow the scaling up of other parallel existing efforts, contributing towards the achievement of the objectives set out under the WRP. This will also contribute to the wider participation and involvement from South Africa’s relatively well-established private sector (through the development of a more integrated water sector) between sectors and ultimately creating a new sustainable asset class for continued future investment.

In the absence of GCF grants, 7 out of the proposed 10 initial sub-projects were assessed to have target minimum financial viability. The remaining three projects are considered to be at risk due to their impact on the financial viability of the individual sub-projects but also the overall viability of the WRP. Hence, even with this initial high-level assessment, it can be seen that GCF funding brings additionality to establishing this Programme and water reuse as an asset class.


---
**Page 79**
---

The Government of South Africa through local implementing municipalities have little financial capacity to meet the initial costs of establishing a programme of this nature. GCF grant and reimbursable grant funding, in addition to the concessional loan portion of the requested GCF funding is necessary to overcome this constraint. The proposed WRP will provide a framework for the development of water reuse systems at suitable locations in South Africa from adequately building resilience to increasing climate hazards. For example, the Western Cape has experienced severe droughts and it is expected that these conditions are likely to continue and sustain their water usage during increasingly more frequent drought periods (such as the droughts experienced in the Western Cape between 2016 and 2018).

The longer-term programme sustainability is important to address climate vulnerabilities in the longer term. To this end, the WRP and WWIP have been designed so that the design of the WWIP has addressed the aspects outlined below.

Political support and ownership

The WRIP will be a sub-programme of the overarching WWIP with the establishment of the WWIP being approved by national government. The ownership of the framework for the WRIP will be by DWS and has the approval of the National Treasury. The mandate of the WWIP is to develop, implement and monitor the mandate to ensure sustainable water resource management and development, as well as oversee/ regulate private sector engagement in the WRP. The WWIP will also provide a framework for the GCF grant supporting the implementation of various climate and water sector strategies the ongoing support of national government is secure.

Partnerships

DWS and MISA play a critical role to support municipalities in providing effective services. With local government having an important mandate to drive local socio-economic development and the provision of services, DOWS has a key role to facilitate across different sectors of government in facilitating support provided by DWS to municipalities. The WWIP will provide a framework for DWS to develop and implement accelerated municipal infrastructure and service delivery. Through this support MISA aims to provide technical advice on water reuse systems development and implementation. This includes providing the technical support to targeted municipalities, thereby improving infrastructure planning, implementation, as well as operations and maintenance. MISA have been actively engaged in the development of the WRP and are supportive of its implementation.

Importantly, The National Treasury and DFFE are both supportive of undertaking projects that underpin the ability of the country to endure resilience against the impacts that climate is having on the national ability to ensure sustainable water resource management and development. This includes supporting public sector institutions in realising long-term implementation of water reuse sub-projects across South Africa to address climate change vulnerabilities. It has been agreed by all parties to ensure these actors are partners to the development and the implementation of WWIP and WRP.

Private Sector Engagement

While private sector participation is an essential part of developing a sustainable policy, regulatory and institutional framework for the support of ongoing water reuse sub-projects, the private sector are an important part of ongoing project implementation. While the blended finance solutions will provide a mix of public and private sector finance, the private sector is equally critical in guiding and providing technological innovation to ensure that the WRP can be implemented on a large scale with minimal reliance on contractual arrangements. A key element of the WWIP will be the ability of the programme to ensure that this engagement is visible, improved and desired for future projects. There has been some degree a level

## Sustainable and innovative technologies

The National Water Reuse Strategy (DWS, 2011) indicates the importance of selecting and implementing appropriate technologies as a critical precursor to successful water reuse sub-project implementation. The WPO will develop itself as a centre of excellence that will plan and implement technologies that are functional, sustainable and have achieve the required levels of performance and will take into account the lessons learned from previous projects. To ensure the sustainability of this project, a Community of Experts has been established to provide for engagement and knowledge exchange with relevant experts towards continued improvement.

## Operations and maintenance

The structuring of the programme has been such as to incorporate clarity as to operational aspects across the programme's lifecycle including programme development and set-up, implementation, evaluation, institutional capacity building and monitoring and reporting. The governance model is based on that outlined in an operational case that provides for the governance modalities including aspects of oversight and accountability supported by monitoring and reporting as well as aspects that enable the operational implementation of projects.

## Organisational and institutional capacity

A key element of the programme's sustainability is the development of sufficient capacity and competencies to support the management and implementation of the programme as well as that of the projects themselves in terms of human resources, organisational structures, systems and processes (technical, financial and project management) within the WPO as well as at project level has been recognised as key part of the ability to ensure project implementation as well as taking such projects to scale, across the country.

## Funding stability

The key lenses, structures and arrangements of the WPRP's Financial Architecture are developed to ensure the programme meets term financial needs of the programme. The design and establishment of the WPRP is premised on a phased approach whereby, during the first 3 years of establishment, the DBSA will be the Implementing Agent, and the WPRP Core Costs will be covered by grants via the National Treasury and the CDF. After this period, the WPRP will transition into a fully self-sustaining financing mechanism as part of the financing project implementation. This enables the programme to have a secure financial base during its development phase prior to transitioning into a more self-sustaining model as the pipeline of projects develops over time.

## Monitoring and evaluation

The operational case for the WPRP sets out a clear programme governance regime with an OC that will oversee the strategic, senior management and level-ordinator management support and guidance to the WPRP. The OC will ensure there are the necessary operational checks and balances, effective systems of internal control and risk management, as well as promoting a 'governance culture' of transparency and accountability which will be underpinned by the implementation of the OC's oversight role. The establishment and utilization of the internal systems of the DBSA will further underpin the programme's sustainability.

## Programme exit strategy and longer-term ownership

A successful exit strategy will ensure continuity of projects under the WPRP, the implementation of best practices in terms of project management and the transfer of knowledge to other stakeholders. This will guide the strategy to undertake phased development of the programme to enable the scale-up of these

### Due diligence assessments of co-financers is not possible at this stage of the programme development and will be undertaken during the first year of the programme and then will be undertaken on an annual basis as projects move into the implementation phase.

## C.2. Financing by component
While there has been some engagement with co-financers in the development of the WIRP, there has been no formal agreement to date. Broadly speaking, co-financers will take on three forms:

- **Equity / budget allocation grants:** These are likely to come from participating local authorities, who own and/or are mandated to run a particular WWTP. Municipalities have been engaged numerous times in the past with regard to the WIRP and have expressed interest in engaging with the WIRP. These municipalities have a range of financial instruments available to provide these grants.
- **Concessional loan:** DBSA will undertake market soundings with other DFIs to provide support to sub-projects, as project preparation support is being provided to ensure the production of bankable sub-proj
- **Senior debt:** Commercial banks have been engaged throughout the preparation of the WIRP and have informed the structure of the Blended Finance Solution. How projects could be used, and what might be required in terms of guarantees, has also been discussed with commercial banks. Some municipalities have indicated interest in providing debt and equity to support WWTP sub-projects.

In terms of process risk assessments and due diligence on co-financers, this is a requirement of the IBCF and will not be undertaken at the design of each blended finance solution.

## Outcome 1A – Core Costs:
Costs represent the establishment and operational cost of the Water Reuse WPO, which are estimated at USD 2 million over the first three years of establishment.

### Subsidies & Preparatory and Technical Advisory (TA) Costs
The WPO's primary focus is the preparation of bankable water reuse sub-projects. The proposed USD 30 million grant from the GCF will be allocated alongside USD 30 million of capital from other participating organizations, to leverage USD 90 million in private sector financing for each of the three phases of the programme.

### Outcome 2 – Implementation Costs
Costs will be covered by a Blended Finance solution comprising:
- **Equity**: USD 800 million from private development partners (based on a D.E ratio of 75%)
- **Senior Debt**: USD 650 million from private debt capital partners such as commercial banks and institutional investors, currently representing 50% of the total implementation cost cover.
- **Subordinated Debt**: USD 150 million from the DBSA and DBF Partners with the discrete purpose of financing the sub-projects within the programme.
- **Concessional Loans**: USD 290 million from the GCF to target the gap financing requirements and ensure the financial viability of individual sub-projects across the programme.

### E. Budget Allocations & Grants
USD 150 million from participating authorities and the SA Infrastructure Fund.

## EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A priority element of the success of the WRP will be the ability to realise impact and as such is a priority investment criterion.

The WPO as the lead entity for the WRP will play a central role in initiating, procuring, and overseeing interventions undertaken by the WPR. As such the WPO will require a minimum threshold of capacity and comprehensive knowledge of water resource management. The WPO will also be required to have a strong WPO as being recognized for its technical expertise, planning ability, project management capability, financial control and reporting systems. In addition, the WPO will need to have a strong relationship with the technical and institutional complexity of water reuse the WPO must be able to demonstrate the capability to champion and facilitate the implementation of water reuse sub-projects. This will require the WPO to have a strong understanding of the socio-economic and environmental impacts of water reuse. The WPO will be the core advocate for adaptation working closely with project owners to ensure catalytic change.

The WRP will be developed in phases whereby the first phase targets sub-projects with the highest potential impact. These projects are expected to be located within the most vulnerable areas, where the concept and build confidence in the WRP as well as the technology. Clearly the initial list of sub-projects is subject to change based on the results of the feasibility studies. There will also be a few that have a strong wastewater and water treatment capability who may wish to participate in developing a project. These first phase Municipalities could thus come from the following list, which is purely indicative:

* City of Cape Town Metropolitan Municipality;
* Nelson Mandela Bay Metropolitan Municipality;
* City of Ekurhuleni Metropolitan Municipality;
* City of Johannesburg Metropolitan Municipality;
* City of Tshwane Metropolitan Municipality;
* eThekwini Metropolitan Municipality;
* Mangaung Local Municipality;
* Dikgang Local Municipality;
* Indicative Project A; and
* Indicative Project B

Of these, 6 are Metropolitan Municipalities and the most significant growth nodes of the country, all of which face socio-economic challenges. Of these projects 80% face climate induced vulnerability (see Table 1 and 4).

The value of water resilience to businesses can be provided by the vulnerability of economic output to a cut-off in water supply. That is, the value to a business from reducing the risk of interruptions to water supply is a function of its reliance on water supply to produce economic output, defined here by gross value added (GVA). The estimated value of water resilience to businesses can be provided as an estimate of the value of economic output lost due to water restrictions. The value of economic output varies from one sector to another, in function of climate stress in each municipality. The estimated value in Gross Value Added (GVA) reduction is provided in Table 15.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 84 OF 156

The economic Internal Rate of Return (IRR) is a function of the contribution of the WRP to reducing the risk of water shortages in the event of a drought. The WRP contributes to minimising the water supply deficit and the associated cost of water restrictions by increasing the resilience of South African municipalities. The IRR is applied to the value at risk to households and businesses across all municipalities to generate an estimated net present value of the WRP in terms of the avoided cost of water shortages. This is summarised in Figure 25 below.

| Figure 25: Annual average of the avoided cost of water restrictions resulting from the WRP (ZAR) |

The average annual avoided cost to households and businesses that would have resulted from water restrictions in the absence of the WRP is over R130 million (USD 8.1 million). The annual value of avoided costs is discounted at a rate of 5% per annum, which is summed with the net financial cashflows for the programme and discounted over the period to 2050.

* **The Economic IRR for the WRP**, where a value is 28%, where the Financial IRR for the programme as a whole is 13%.

* **The Net Present Value** for the programme as a whole factoring in economic co-benefits ranges from ZAR 13 billion to ZAR 34 billion (USD $17 million to USD 2.1 billion), justifying the investment from an economic perspective.

## D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The development of water reuse sub-projects has been limited to date with climate related hazards influencing impacted municipalities to explore these options to address increasing vulnerability and increasing demand for water in South Africa. The WRP provides an opportunity for municipalities to take advantage of this opportunity to upscale this to improve water security. As such, the development of a pipeline of water reuse sub-projects will provide a number of benefits including the creation of new jobs, increased investment in improved water security as well as the development of improved levels of adaptive capacity that enable climate responsive planning will enable the WRP to have a transformational impact on the water sector. The WRP will also support the implementation of the National Water Act (NWA) 2018 through providing funding, financing, and technical support to suitable water reuse sub-projects in South Africa in multiple phases, supporting the country's 8 metropolitan municipalities as well as 44 district municipalities (and, based on


---
**Page 88**
---

The need for this opportunity, the 226 local municipalities). Within the broader structure of the WRP, a key portion of the GCF’s funding will be directed towards the project preparation function, to ensure that the projects identified optimize climate change adaptation benefits and are designed with climate resilience considerations. The WRP will also support the development of a national water sector strategy for enhancing the resilience in South Africa's water sector through every project preparation process.

Noting the importance of introducing the paradigm of a circular economy, the WRP has the potential to enhance the economic value of water resources and increase the competitiveness across the country. It will do so through demonstrating viable business models for water reuse amongst industrial, commercial, and residential sectors by leveraging advanced water reuse technologies and ecosystem-based approaches to the South African market.

By developing typologies of water reuse infrastructure interventions that match certain context-specific characteristics (e.g., population served, projected growth in local water demand, predominant water use patterns, etc.), the WRP will be able to identify a set of comparable municipal contexts and sites where suitable project types could be replicated, not only in terms of technology but also in terms of business models. The development of these typologies and replicable models will enable the programme to scale-up the roll-out of projects over time, with weaker municipalities benefiting from the knowledge creation and experiences from early pathfinder projects.

The WRP will provide a platform for the sharing of lessons learned through project implementation and stakeholder-driven processes that provide an impetus to behaviour change by users. Further, the WRP will, through a centre of excellence and its WPC, enable municipalities to have increased access to a pool of expertise in water reuse, finance and legal advice on projects, financing options, procurement and project implementation.

The WRP will demonstrably change incentives for climate change adaptation in water management by providing treatment and/or financially viable and cost-effective solutions for municipalities. The WRP will facilitate the creation of a new asset class, which will allow for investors to invest in water reuse infrastructure that is not only environmentally sustainable but also provides standardized contractual and procurement process, thereby gaining efficiencies and iteratively mitigating inherent project related risk, thus enhancing the likelihood of increased investment into such subsequent sub-projects.

Through the management of strategic governmental partnerships, the WRP will be in a position to engage with key government agencies to address a range of policy, strategy, institutional, and regulatory aspects that fall outside the scope of the WRP but are critical for the successful implementation of projects aimed at improving water security. The implementation of water reuse sub-projects will require a number of regulations around DWs operating this programme and providing oversight, it will be an important sponsor in addressing these issues.

There is a legacy of reluctance to charging for ecosystem maintenance and restoration that underpins a large component of the water sector south. South Africa recognises that it needs to fix the economics of water services if it is going to achieve its objectives around water security. The Water Services Pricing Strategy is currently under review with the aim to finalise this by March 2023. The WRP will help support the development and implementation of a pricing strategy for water reuse, which will be critical in addressing water pricing and ecosystem services. It will not replace such work but it will work to promote transparency to ecosystem services and it will support the changing the policy landscape wherever strong water market signals. This integration of ecosystem services and infrastructure finance is a new paradigm for South Africa.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 86 OF 152

## B

Under the African governance framework, municipalities have a critical role in driving local socio-economic development. The WRP is designed to deliver a range of benefits to water users such as households and industries, and to municipal governments, and thereby to South Africa. The intended co-benefits include:

- Environmental co-benefits: through the reduction in water pollution, improvements in water quality and improvements in soil and water quality management in agricultural areas and urban farms. The sub-projects will help strengthen South Africa’s resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards by improving water security for households and industries, improved water supply reliability and reuse. There will also be mitigation co-benefits from greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reductions and improvements in air quality through the reduction of pollution from industrial processes. Improved sanitation, improved ecosystem maintenance in upper catchments. Further environmental co-benefits may also be achieved through the incorporation of ecosystem-based approaches to sustain environmental services.

- Economic co-benefits: through the expansion of markets for treated wastewater, revenue-generation opportunities and cost savings on water supply infrastructure. The WRP will assist municipalities of creating alternate water supply from freshwater sources to meet the same demand, and improved industrial and commercial productivity in targeted municipalities due to more reliable water supply.

- Gender-sensitive development impact: through a reduction in household water insecurity, which corresponds with increased access to safe drinking water for women and children. The WRP will increase women’s participation in the programme itself. Women direct beneficiaries are estimated at 50% of all households served by the project.

- Contribution to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): through support to a number of SDGs, the WRP will primarily help South Africa make progress towards:

o SDG 6 – ensure availability and sustainable management of water and sanitation for all (especially target 6.3: improve water quality by reducing pollution).

o SDG 11 – make cities more inclusive, safe, resilient, and sustainable (especially target 11.8):.

o SDG 12 – ensure sustainable consumption and production patterns (especially target 12.5:).

o SDG 13 – take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts (especially target 13.2:).

It is important when dealing with water and ecosystem services to ensure sub-projects focus on the interconnectivity of all SDGs and to ensure no significant trade-offs occur between goals. WRP investments will primarily be in areas where co-benefits for people and the environment are highest and as such modelling coupled with adaptive management approaches will likely be necessary.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 87 OF 156

## South Africa is facing rising temperatures together with declining rainfall and a significant increase in spatial and temporal variability of rainfall resulting in more frequent floods and droughts. South Africa is warming at more than twice the rate of global temperature increases**. Due to the variability of rainfall and rates of evapotranspiration across the country some parts of the country will experience these vulnerabilities differently. The impacts of climate change on agriculture, water resources, health, and biodiversity can have far wider levels of impact. Overall, there is robust evidence that slow-onset climate hazards like droughts are likely to be more damaging than extreme events such as floods or heatwaves. Droughts are likely to occur multiple times in a century**. South African settlements are susceptible to the effects of climate change and will experience significant impacts. The country has already been affected by more than 22 million South Africans being exposed to the effects of USD 6.81 billion economic losses**

### As in most under-resourced countries, climate change will disproportionately burden the poor and most vulnerable communities in South Africa. Large populations living in impoverished circumstances, informal settlements that are located in sites vulnerable to extreme weather and natural hazards, further perpetuate poverty and inequality. The country has a high level of informality with limited access to water and access to power. Nearly 20% of the country's population lives on less than USD 2.00 a day, and almost 60% live below the national poverty line**. South Africa is one of the most unequal countries in the world in recent years, based on the Gini coefficient. Compared to all other emerging markets, income inequality has remained high in South Africa, decoupled markedly from the average for such markets.

### Unemployment levels in South Africa are exceptionally high, at 28%, but even higher at a staggering 53% for youth. The country is also experiencing an economic crisis with the COVID-19 pandemic and related economic contraction, with a GDP growth rate reduction of -7.2%. The country's budget deficit is at a record level of 40% of GDP. South Africa has been rated as one of the most vulnerable countries to speculative (junk) status by many rating agencies, with a negative outlook. Its debt-to-GDP ratio of 81% is rapidly approaching unsustainable levels. The country has limited access to international sources of financing for potential adaptation measures which are regarded as having high risk and negligible returns on investment—from commercial or private lenders. Therefore, South Africa must look at innovative funding mechanisms such as blended finance, green bonds, health measures, post COVID economic recovery, and continued support to social protection programs.

### While South Africa has retained its institutional capacity in some areas, over the last decade there have also been significant challenges in terms of capacity building and institutional strengthening. The National Water Commission recognized that in many service areas delivery has fallen dramatically short of expectations. Municipal face challenges in meeting even basic service-delivery needs; they are not well placed to develop and implement long-term strategies for climate change adaptation, nor do they have the skills or sophisticated financial structuring requirements. This requires national government to strategically guide the development of a comprehensive water sector strategy that includes capacity building and institutional strengthening. The National Water Commission has identified the need for capacity-building to beneficiary municipalities to introduce these important climate adaptation and resilience building measures. South Africa is also in urgent need of a long-term, integrated approach to water resource reconciliation strategies to develop an up-to-date understanding of the current and future water supply challenges as well as the impacts of these upon the national and local economies. These studies will inform the development of a comprehensive water sector strategy.

### It will be imperative to fully understand the contextual needs at sub-project level, noting that these can be distinctive. The provision of preparation support, both from a technical and financial perspective, to ensure that projects are well-structured in terms of risk, and financially viable will be a critical success factor for the WRP.

D.5 Country ownership (max. 300 words, approximately 1 page)
The Constitution of South Africa Water guarantees the right of access to sufficient water, and this is supported by the National Water Act (Act 56 of 1998) and the Water Services Act (Act 108 of 1997). These laws provide a range of strategic and planning instruments that enable the sustainable development of South Africa’s water resources. The National Water Resources Strategy (NQRS), National Water Management Strategies, and Water Services Development Plans and Integrated Development Plans. The Second Edition of the NWRIS in 2013 outlined a National Water House Strategy to support the development of the NQRS. In 2018, the Department of Water and Sanitation (DWS) published the National Status of water resources. The National Water and Sanitation Master Plan was developed in 2018 to consolidate the various strategies and plans into one document. Climate change adaptation is a high priority for the country. Furthermore, water reuse has been outlined as one of the key approaches to adaptation in the NCCAS, which is South Africa’s de facto National Adaptation Plan. The NCCAS urges water users to adopt water efficiency measures and to consider water reuse where possible. South Africa’s updated draft NDC “reinforces this commitment further through its Goal 3: Implementation of the NQCS and Water Services Development Plans and Integrated Development Plans (ISDPs)”.
The implementation of the WRP is therefore well aligned with the NCCAS and the recently updated NDC.

The WRP will fall under the auspices of the NWPP that will be hosted and supported by the DBSA as Executive Agency. The NWPP was endorsed at a meeting of the PIC in May 2013 and the PICC recognised and approved that the NWPP will become the “enabler” of various government planning and coordinating instruments such as the NQCS, NCCAS and NDC.

It should be noted that although the NWPP will ultimately comprise of priority sub-programme initiatives, at present only the WRP has progressed materially, and as such the WRP and its success will play a crucial initiative in the overall development of the NWPP. The WRP is therefore being developed in an integrated manner. This provides a pragmatic approach to the development of the NWPP. Noting the importance of the WRP in supporting these national strategies and planning instruments, the engagement of national government departments and agencies is critical for the success of the WRP.

Section B.4. The DBSA is the owner of the NWPP and the WRP and will be engaged in the oversight committee as a member.

The DBSA is well-placed to drive the hosting, financing and structuring of the WRP; for the Government of South Africa, as the accredited entity and executing entity. The DBSA has a strong track record with the QCP, and is suited to steering a large, nationwide, infrastructure programme requiring significant financial resources and technical expertise.

The DBSA has a strong track record in the management of programmatic risk and the effective use of safeguards.

The WRP has been discussed with other key and supportive organisations who have interest in participating in the design and implementation of the WRP. These organisations include:

* GTAC and the National Treasury;
* Local government departments including DOFE as the Nationally Designated Authority, as well as DWS and DOOG;
* Public institutions including Infrastructure South Africa and MISA.

### 6.8 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

GCF intervention into the WRIP is critical, as outlined above in Section B.5. While it appears that there will be government and other private co-funding that will be made available, current municipal level appetite and budget for a project of this scale is not clear. The municipality’s budget is insufficient to capitalize on WWTPs's and develop new ATP plans to increase the potential supply of water.

According to the International Monetary Fund, as of end-2020, the Government of South Africa had a total gross debt stock of 134% of GDP. This means that the government has borrowed more than it can afford to pay back in interest and principal payments on its existing debt. As such, the Government of South Africa is unable to increase investments into climate resilient water programmes, which not only impact growth and development, but could also hamper or constrain future economic growth prospects.

## Value for Money

The efficiency of the WRIP can be assessed using several Value for Money metrics. The GCF traditionally seeks to understand how the programme under consideration might perform against similar programmes in the region, and to which the GCF has provided funding. To this end, the financial architecture can provide preliminary Value for Money metrics outlined in Table below.

| Value for Money Metric | Cost/Beneficiary |
|---|---|
| GCF Cost per Beneficiary (Public Sector Project) | USD 50.54 |
| GCF Cost per Beneficiary (Private Sector Project) | USD 71.59 |
| Total cost per beneficiary | USD 411.08 |

These metrics demonstrate that the WRP supports adaptation impact with more efficient use of GCF capital than other similar programmes. For example, the recently awarded Programme in Jordan - Building resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector – demonstrates a GCF cost per beneficiary of USD 117.70.

It is noted that the grace periods for different instruments underlying projects vary between 2 and 4 years, based on the construction period. The table above reflects the mid-point for illustrative purposes only. The credit margin for senior debt instruments decreases roughly by 50-80 bps when GCF funding is accompanied by a partial credit guarantee.

It is envisaged that the GCF will provide concessional loans to both public sector and private sector project owners (see Section above). In this instance, it applies different terms and conditions for outgoing concessional loans, depending on, among other factors, whether the borrower is public or private. The WRP will include sub-projects in its portfolio with both public sector and private sector project owner. Whether a project is classified as either publicly owned or privately owned, is based on the majority equity

**Page 96**

Best Available Technology

The Programme will require the use of the latest international best practice in water reuse design approaches and technology selection. By ensuring that the reuse treatment train designs are vetted by a panel of independent experts, including representatives from the South African Water Research Commission (SAWRC), follow the World Health Organization's (WHO's) Potable Reuse guidelines (as well as SANS 241), achieve a minimum pathogen reduction of 99.9% and removal of all chemicals of emerging concern.

For Direct and Indirect potable reuse, the selection and agreement of the treatment train will be a key component of the project vetting process and a clear Hazard Analysis Critical Control Points (HACCP) will be established to ensure that the water is safe for human consumption. This includes pathogen reduction and micropollutant removal. South Africa doesn't have a regulated potable reuse minimum standard and it is proposed that the programme adopts the California State Department of Health potable reuse standards for pathogen reduction (12/18/10) and micropollutant (Chemicals of Emerging Concern) removal.

- Increased resilience (adaptation)

This will lead to a significant improvement in the awareness and capacity for planning and preparation, as an important intervention to support climate adaptation (Outcome 5). | The programme of programmatic support and capacity building (Outcome 4) will assist municipalities to up-scale their water reuse sub-projects with water reuse sub-projects with a potential increase in the awareness and capacity for planning and preparation. This would often lead to a significant improvement in the awareness and industrial water reuse as set out in the National Water and Sanitation management |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 95 of 120

## Replicability
The existing reuse projects are generally (with some exceptions) small scale, but it is evident that there may be a large number of medium-sized projects which appear to be much larger. An economy of scale exists in the implementation of these projects; however, there is a reluctance to invest a large amount of resources into them because they have not been proven or there are inadequate skills at the local level.

## Sustainability
The realization of Government of the need to address increasing climate vulnerabilities and the lack of capacity to implement sustainable solutions for water reuse, adaptation and water sector strategies. The WRP will work with government and municipalities to build their capacity with regard to the design and implementation of water reuse projects (Outcome 1). Additionally, improvements in strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks at national level are required to ensure that there is sufficient financial resources available to drive such interventions.

## Medium
The paradigm shift would be realized when innovative project implementation is ensured through the transfer of knowledge and technology transfer on water reuse. The move to larger full-scale projects may be made possible by the economies of scale benefits of a programmatic approach (Outcome 2).

## Medium
While the WRP will work with government and municipalities to build their capacity with regard to the design and implementation of water reuse projects, it is also important that they are able to transfer lessons learned to other sub-projects technically, operationally and financially (outcome 3). The programmatic approach will support this by ensuring that there is sufficient scale within the private sector to support these interventions.

E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation (approximately 1 page)

Monitoring and Evaluation of the WRP will be aligned to the DBSA Development Results Reporting Framework and the requirements of GCF as detailed GCF master accreditation agreement. This supports 'inwardly' focused reporting to strengthen processes and the achievement of outputs and outcomes, whilst also enabling 'outwardly' focused reporting to the GCF and other key stakeholders on the attainment of impact.

The broad monitoring and reporting requirements are as follows (and in Figure 26):

- **Project level monitoring**:
    - Undertaken as per the project specific log frame that will be compiled for each sub-project funded by the WRP.
    - The project teams will be required to report to the WRP biannually based on the project log frame.

- **Programme level monitoring**:
    - Information from sub-projects will be collated into the Annual Project Report (APR) and submitted to GCF at the end of the calendar year.


---
**Page 112**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 108 OF 120

## WRP WPO will compile the APR based on information received from individual sub-projects, together with the broader programmatic objectives towards realizing the identified impact and goals.

### Project level evaluation
- Mid-term and terminal project evaluations will be undertaken as per the GCF requirements. This will utilize the paradigm shifts and enabling environment scorecards.
- The evaluations will be undertaken by an independent evaluator/consultant at the cost of the Programme.

## WRP level evaluation
- A mid-term evaluation will be undertaken on the performance of the WRP as per the indicators identified in the programme's log frame.
- At the end of the term of the WRP a terminal evaluation will be undertaken on the programme’s performance as per the log frame indicators and other qualitative information.
- The evaluations will be undertaken by an independent consultant appointed by the DBSA.
- Both evaluations will incorporate the paradigm shift and enabling environment scorecards that would have been developed.

In addition, the DBSA independent evaluation function, the Operations Evaluation Unit (OEU) provides advisory and oversight services to the WRP. The OEU provides a framework for the monitoring and evaluation of all the DBSA’s operations.

On a month-to-month basis the WRP WPO will report to the OC on the day-to-day and operational business of the WPO and the status of the WRP, including progress towards the phase established and development of the WRP. This reporting will be particularly important in the sustainable set-up of the WRP, ensuring that all required documentation and protocols. This more operationally focused reporting will be against annual work plans that the WPO have developed and agreed upon with the OC.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

The risks that exist in South Africa's water sector currently undermine the country’s ability to use water resources efficiently and effectively to respond to water security challenges as a result of climate change. The WRP will manage these risks and mitigate their impacts as tabulated below. In addition, it must be noted that:

- No sub projects will be undertaken in any jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council Resolutions (UNSCRs).
- No individual or entity that is listed on any UN Security Council sanctions list, including the UN Consolidated List, shall participate in any manner with the sub-project or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer, or beneficiary.
- There are no intentions to directly distribute or disburse funds to beneficiaries.

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Generating project pipeline

| Category | Probability | Impact | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | Low | Medium | Low |

**Description**

Municipal capacity is often limited at technical and operational levels, with most staff often being challenged in the ability to conceptualize and prepare water reuse sub-projects that are bankable. This is further exacerbated by weak capacity to support the procurement and contracting required to undertake these infrastructural solutions. As such, these innovative projects that are important to address local climate and water security challenges will be challenging to implement without adequate technical and financial support. The Water Services Authority Business Health assessment found 78% of municipalities to be extremely and highly vulnerable in their ability to provide water services. Generating a sufficient project pipeline to support the WRP under these circumstances, will be challenging.

### Mitigation Measures

The WRP has undertaken a market study that provides a strong initial pipeline that will enable the WRP to be initiated through these initial sub-projects with very little risk and stress on its continued funding as well as development of the project pipeline. The WRP will provide technical assistance in M&E capacity to conceptualize and prepare bankable projects. The WPD will be supported by the procurement competencies of the DBSA thereby enabling the provision of contracted technical and financial support for project preparation.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Public Perception

| Category | Probability | Impact | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | Medium | Medium | Medium |


---
**Page 115**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 112 of 156

## Description

Despite water re-use being increasingly accepted as a technical response to climate vulnerability and water insecurity, public perception relating to the reuse of wastewater effluent is still a significant obstacle for the project. In some instances, communities have rejected the use of co-located water treatment plants to develop, operate and maintain infrastructure. Local case studies quote several instances where this poor public perception has resulted in the failure of direct water reuse sub-projects.

### Mitigation Measures

Given the scale of South Africa’s water challenges and the investment required to diversify the current water mix, it is crucial that the WRIP’s implementing partners communicate clearly, consistently, and effectively about South Africa’s water situation. The Water Reuse Implementation Plan (WRIP) for South Africa will be rolled out. Water re-use at a policy level is already recognized as part of the water mix (NWSPM, DWS 2018) and already successfully implemented in some municipalities. This will be leveraged by the WRIP to ensure that all stakeholders are aware of the benefits of water reuse.

The WRIP has developed a robust Communication Strategy to address these challenges. The strategy includes a comprehensive communication plan for the launch of the WRIP and to develop an improved awareness of the importance of these interventions to support ongoing growth and development.

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Partner risk

### Category | Probability | Impact | Priority
---|---|---|---
Financial | Medium | High | High

### Description

While municipalities often have significant capacity constraints at the technical level, which hinders the conceptualisation and preparation of water reuse sub-projects, there are often significant challenges in terms of financial resources. These include limited access to capital funding for infrastructure development as well as political interference that results in weak governance that can impact on the financial status or ability of a municipality as well as its ability to adhere to formal agreements. This is often exacerbated by Municipalities often facing competing demands due to weak financial circumstances and many competing demands on limited financial resources.

### Mitigation Measures

The WRIP will be initiated with partners who are committed to the project outcomes and have experience in implementing similar projects. This will support the WRIP in strengthening municipal competencies with time, as well as enabling the WEP to embed its protocols and practices. The Project owners will be the municipalities themselves, which will ensure that they are fully engaged in all aspects of these interventions. Ensuring alignment with Municipal Integrated Development Plans (IDPs) will be a key element of this engagement. The progress of each project will be monitored, measured, and reported. Engagement will be through formal written correspondence, signed by the required authority, as required at various stages and gates of the project preparation process, and will


---
**Page 116**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.8 | PAGE NUMBER 115 OF 126

allow for the sub-project to be stopped at any stage of project preparation, if appropriate in the circumstances.

The municipalities will sign project funding agreements, as separate agreements between the project owners and the lenders. The structure of these agreements will depend on the structure of the funding arrangements and underpin finance arrangements to mitigate these partner risks.

At this stage no due diligence assessments have been undertaken as this would only be appropriate during this project preparation phase. These assessments are expected to be completed by the national Auditor-General and this does provide for the level of due diligence needed for these sub-projects. These assessments will also be supported by the findings from the Municipal Strategic Self Assessment analysis and the Green and Blue Drop performance assessments, all of which are led by the DWS.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Project Implementation risks

| Category | Probability | Impact | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Financial / technical | Low | High | Medium |

### Description

There are a range of risks associated with project implementation that can have financial implications as well as implications for the achievement of programme/targets. As such there could be delays in project delivery due to contractual, commercial and operational issues. These risks will be managed through risk management plans and risks associated with municipal support and buy-in. While the initial stage of project preparation should ensure that these risks are well outlined with a clear mitigation strategy, these risks could nevertheless surface.

### Mitigation Measures

Project implementation will be undertaken as a key element of the project preparation phase and these risks will be outlined with clear mitigation measures during that stage and in conjunction with the Municipalities. As sub-projects move through the project pipeline, formalised agreements and contractual arrangements will be established with the project owners as well as the service providers to ensure that these risks are managed appropriately.

## Selected Risk Factor 5: Sufficient investment to support GCF investment

| Category | Probability | Impact | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Financial | Low | High | Medium |

### Description

In response to the impacts of climate change on the water sector, only through the leveraging of GCF funding will the realisation of the WFP be achieved enabling the scaled development of water reuse sub-projects nationally and ultimately improving South Africa’s climate resilience. While GCF funding will be specifically considered on a project-by-project basis within the larger programme context, the WFP will also be dependent upon a range of co-investments that will underpin the differing components of the programme.


---
**Page 117**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 114 of 150

## Mitigation Measures

The design and establishment of the WRFP will be premised on a phased approach, whereby during the first 3 years of establishment, the DBSA will be the Management Agent, and the WRFP's Core Costs will likely be covered by grants. However, over the medium-term, the WRFP's Core Costs will be covered through a repayment mechanism from project revenues. In addition to this, the WRFP will be financially supported, supplemented by grant funding from National Treasury to support the WRFP, which will allow for key enablers that are subject to an external annual co-investment required and this mechanism will ensure there is no cross-subsidisation of GCF funds.

### Selected Risk Factor 6: Foreign Exchange

| Category | Impact | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Financial | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

The WRFP will use GCF concessional finance, which is US Dollar denominated (USD). This concessionary capital will be provided to the DBSA who will manage the USD exposure. Furthermore, the WRFP's underlying project financing will also be in USD. So, the exchange risk associated with debt servicing from sub-projects will be in ZAR with DBSA managing the foreign exchange risk of the GCF funding on a sub-project basis.

### Mitigation Measures

With GCF funding being provided through limited recourse loans, the currency mismatch between the funding from GCF and the loan to sub-projects will be managed by the DBSA on a sub-project basis...

The concept will only be finalised after review by the GCF, with the finalised concept terms to be included in the FAA, after the Full Funding proposal has been submitted and approved by the GCF.

### Risk Factor 7: Compliance (AML/CFT) risk

| Category | Impact | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Financial | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Instances of fraud and other corrupt practices, as outlined in the GCF's Policy on Prohibited Practices, will undermine the programme's due diligence efforts as well as negatively impact upon the reputation of the GCF, the Trustee (as fiduciary entity), and the Enabling Entity as well as the credibility of the WRFP.


---
**Page 118**
---

# Mitigation Measures

Issues could ultimately impact upon the levels of investment in the WRP, particularly impacting upon the blended finance solutions that the programme aims to develop.

The South African legislation, through the PFMA (Act of 1999) and Municipal Finance Management Act (Act of 2003), provide for regulatory oversight and procedures in country prohibited practices. These are supported by various national and provincial level audit processes. In the development of the WPO, the procurement processes, standardized procedures and supporting guidelines will ensure alignment with the CFTF of the DFAT. The relevant policies and procedures to be followed in the implementation of the WPO are provided by the DBSA, as Accredited Entity. The Accredited Entity shall apply its own fiduciary principles and standards for the management of financial resources and assets in accordance with the United States Financing of Terrorists (CFT), financial sanctions imposed by the United Nations Security Council which should enable it to comply with the Policy on Prohibited Practices and the Financial Management Policy.

The relevant policies and procedures will be developed consistent with the principles of the AML/CFT Policy. In this regard, the DBSA Whistle Blowing Policy will be utilised to enable staff members to raise concerns where they have reasonable grounds for believing that there is fraud, corruption, corporate crime or impropriety.

Additionally, the WPO will develop thorough risk management protocols linked to a comprehensive governance arrangement that will ensure effective oversight and reporting.


---
**Page 119**
---

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Noting the importance of water reuse to diversifying the "water mix" in South Africa, and the challenges and barriers that exist for municipalities to implement water reuse sub-projects, the development of a focused programme to address these challenges and ultimately implement pathfinder sub-projects is critical to contributing towards building a more climate-resilient water future. The design of a National WRP aims to support municipalities in implementing water reuse projects through improved access to water reuse approaches and infrastructure in municipalities and through improved management of Ecological Water Reuse (EWR) sites.

At the highest level, the WRP will encourage and support municipalities to implement water reuse and reclamation initiatives that would also include nexus issues such as combined heat and power (CHP) installations, energy efficiency improvements, and other co-benefits. The WRP is designed to be flexible and ready-to-launch towards demonstrably achieving climate change resilience objectives (by strengthening the country's adaptive capacity against water stress and scarcity), and maximize measurable climate change impact in line with the South Africa National Determined Contribution (NDC) and the goals of the Green Climate Fund.

Four water reuse archetypes that may be considered as potential project implementation options within Water and each water reuse sub-project that will be supported by this Programme will follow a rigorous appraisal procedure and will require an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment as well as a Gender, Sexual Orientation, Gender Identity and Sexual Harassment and Indigenous Peoples Assessments. The core aims of the programme are to:

- Ensure Climate adaptation as a principle objective of the programme.
- Mainstreaming climate resilience into the water use and reuse sector.
- Assist municipalities in building capacity for managing climate change in the water and wastewater services sector.
- Encourage accelerated development of water reuse sub-projects at municipal level.
- Create a new asset class around water reuse infrastructure.
- Improve EWR water security and to meet climate mitigation objective strategies.

An Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared for the Programme with an emphasis on ensuring that all project activities are aligned with the NDCs, and in enhancing positive aspects of the Programme (Annex 6). The ESMF contains environmental and social baseline information at the national level. At the municipal level, a Municipal Environmental and Social Management Plan (MESP) for each beneficiary is committed and able to comply with GCF Environmental and Social Policies, and DBSA has prepared a generic MESP for all beneficiaries of this Programme. In addition, a generic Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) has been developed for the Programme. The ESMF will be updated as required during project implementation. The ESMP sets out the project specific measures and actions required to comply with the ESS&V over a specified timeframe.

This ESMF has identified the generic high level environmental and social negative impacts and will serve as a baseline against which all projects. The ESMF has determined that the archetypes may have negative environmental and social impacts, and proposed sub-projects should follow a minimum due diligence procedure as follows:

- Identify the environmental and social conditions in the targeted municipality along with the applicable ESFs assessments.
- The due diligence for the project must include a dedicated environmental and social risk assessment.


---
**Page 120**
---

* Establish the conditions and measures required to ensure that all the negative environmental and social impacts are properly and effectively mitigated.

A checklist of Environmental and Social Eligibility Criteria for Potential Projects has been developed to be completed for each water reuse sub-project that will be supported by the Programme and be used to determine whether a sub-project is eligible. If the answer to any one of these eligibility criteria questions is yes, then the sub-project would be classified as a Category 1 sub-project (with potential significant adverse social or environmental impacts on a broader, diverse, irreversible, or unprecedented) basis.

A checklist for the Environmental and Social Risk Management Committee will be utilised during the first round of selection of sub-projects as a first step to evaluate the applicability of the DISBA / GCF environmental and social standards safeguards. Planning Phase considerations should also be taken into account in conjunction with the planning and site selection phase and provides various environmental aspects and impacts that may be applicable.
