## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

In addressing the current water security issue, a gender lens to address the broader challenges was required, that not only aligned to global and GCF measures, but also strengthened the country's approach to transformational change. The Programme will be implemented within the existing national gender framework that will underpin the approach of the WRP to achieve gender equality in the undertakings of the programme. This is supported by the Women’s Charter for Accelerated Development (WCAD), and the Codes of Good Practice on Broad Based Black Economic Empowerment (BEE)***.

The BBBEE codes address affirmative action both in terms of race and gender equity and address this through various mechanisms such as BEE scorecards, Skills Development, Preferential Procurement, Enterprise Development and Social-Economic Development. South Africa in 2008 signed the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development, which commits all SADC member states to ensure that at least 30% of women are represented in decision-making bodies in the public and private sectors by 2015. The National Framework for Gender Equality in Water Resources Management (NFGEM) was developed with the aim that management echelons must be women. This national framework has provided Government with its gender representation targets for a number of years***, with this being supported by racial representation targets of 50% for all levels of management and 30% for all levels of staff in the public sector. Women were given right to vote prior to April 1994, as outlined in the BEEB codes.

With the development of the sub-project pipeline for the WRP being only at scoping level, detailed baseline data on the gender composition of the workforce is not yet available and would form a key part of the detailed vulnerability assessments that would be undertaken by the WRPP for each sub-project during project implementation. The current status of women in the water sector is that they are underrepresented in all levels of wastewater treatment works does look at technical skills availability at each plant, it does not gather data on a gender manner. Likewise, the 2018 Municipal Strategic Self-Assessment study that assesses performance against the National Development Plan (NDP) and the SADC Protocol on Gender Equality has no staff capacity. However, this again does not provide gender data. The development of gender baselines will be undertaken as part of the scoping exercise for each sub-project.

Efforts to drive gender equity is supported a number of mechanisms for transforming gender relations collectively known within government and civil society as the “National Gender Machinery”. Together, these mechanisms are designed to ensure that women’s rights are protected and promoted in all spheres of life. The National Gender Machinery has structures at different levels in national government, legislature, and statutory bodies (Figure 27) and the DWSS, DBSA and the WRP will work closely with this machinery to promote gender equality within the programme and sub-project and to support intersectoral and sectorial partnerships.

While the targets and approaches to achieve gender equity and inclusion are outlined across these various instruments, there is a long way to go in terms of policy, practice, and outcomes. Despite historical gender-based violence and lack of employment opportunities in senior leadership positions that continue to hamper women from achieving economic parity. The water sector has significant impact on gender disparities, and the challenges identified are complex and inter-linked. These are elaborated below.


---
**Page 122**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 119 of 158

## Figure 27: Structure of the national machinery for the advancement of gender equality in South Africa

- **Gender and Climate Change:** Women and girls in developing countries are often more vulnerable than men to climate change, due to their greater dependence on natural resources. They also have less access to pre-existing gender inequalities regarding political leadership, access to information and resources, and decision-making power.
- **Gender and Water, Sanitation and Hygiene (WASH):** Menstrual hygiene continues to receive limited attention in South Africa despite the fact that women are disproportionately affected by WASH programmes failing to consider women's need to manage menstruation. Furthermore, latrine design often overemphasizes privacy at the expense of accessibility for girls. In cases where hygiene promotion programmes exist, many fail to include the issue of menstrual waste management and do not particularly focus on hand washing practices.
- **Gender and rural development:** The provision of hygiene and sanitation are often considered women's tasks – especially in rural contexts. Women and girls are most often the primary users, providers, and managers of water in their households and tend to have the main responsibility for household chores such as cooking, cleaning, and fetching water. This means that they may be required to travel long distances over many hours to meet their families' water needs. Women and children often bear the brunt of these challenges.
- **Gender and the Labour Market:** Historically, from low skilled women are less likely to seek employment in the utilities sector (for instance, energy, water, gas, electricity etc.). Notably in 2015, South Africa recorded a gender pay gap of 34.7%. In addition, women make up only 8% of the construction sector; low-skilled women comprise 3.2% of all jobs.

**Page 123**
---

The Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.8 | Page 10 of 150

The stakeholder engagement process involved consultations at different levels, not only through the review of documents, but also through targeted engagements as well. International, National and local-level discussions were had to gain insights on how to ensure a robust gender-sensitive programme design.

Despite these broader challenges and progressive achievements through policy development in South Africa, gaps still exist between women’s lived realities and their representation within the WFP trajectory towards improved gender equality and women's empowerment. Through the various activities as outlined in the Gender Action Plan, the WFP will aim to address the current social and gender inequalities that will impact upon the success of the programme, such as:

- The programme ensures an equitable development under tone which requires an approach that recognises the interdependent vulnerabilities of age, race, class, and physical ability create overlapping and independent vulnerabilities in the face of climate change. By addressing these multiple aspects into the WFP will cement the systematic inclusion of women’s voices and their conditions, situations, and needs of women and men with diverse gender equality concerns into the programme design.
- Addressing various stereotypes and norms that lead women to internalise and doubt their ability to assume leadership roles in the field. The programme will focus on building a culture of mentorship and leadership in the field to guide and encourage women to navigate through gendered barriers and challenges, including those through the Communication Plan and through empowerment approaches at sub-project levels.
- Applying quotas for female and vulnerable groups to participate in water committees, Boards and agencies.
- Ensuring a 30:40 target for the beneficiaries of the programme to be women.
- Integrating gender equality into all aspects of project design and appropriate gender budgets.
- Raising public awareness campaigns aimed at creating an appreciation and understanding of the benefits of water resources.
- Engaging with stakeholders—from the implementing agencies to the beneficiaries—to build requisite skills and knowledge for gender-sensitive services and management.

The programme will thus endeavour to explore a multitude of complex gendered issues and other activities and objectives that support sustainable and equitable outcomes. It will actively engage diverse perspectives and experiences in order to identify, understand, and address gender differences and barriers. Understanding gender differences along the waste and water value chain will inform the design of services and products for women and men, as well as the delivery of appropriate services for all. To identify and design for these gender differences, the programme will be established with a strong focus on gender equality and inclusion. As the programme evolves, greater in-depth research will need to be conducted to complement this current gender assessment report. Each sub-project under the programme will be required to develop context specific gender action plans that address issues such as sexual harassment, as well as being addressing issues relating to indigenous people and cultural heritage.

The WFP is committed to applying a gender lens to its project design and execution. The team is also actively aware of the possible failures that may occur when limited consideration is applied to understanding the gender inequalities in a country and at a programme level. These inequalities will hamper the effective

### G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The implementation arrangements outlined in section B.4.6 clarify as to the roles and responsibilities for the WPO linked to the NWPP. Annex 10 provides a procurement plan for the first five years, noting that the mid-term review process will guide the implementation of the next five year period.

The financial management between the AE and EEs of the WPO will be governed by DBSA’s finance manual and the GCF’s Financial Management Manual. The WPO will be an OJCSA Accredited Entity of the GCF and is a Programme developer (through its Programme Development Unit) and provides project procurement services to the WPO. The WPO will also act as the implementing agent for the NWPP, with the DBSA acting as the implementing agent of the NWPP and will act as management agent of the WPO for a period of 5 years or until such time that the WPO is legally instituted/registered. DBSA will provide all administrative services such as human resources, finance, procurement, contract administration, project implementation, monitoring, procurement of panels of service providers, entering into legal contracts with service providers and employees, managing the budget, preparing financial statements, and making payments on behalf of the WPO. Key instruments that will guide the financial management are:

#### National Water Partnerships Programme Memorandum of Agreement

An agreement between the Programme owners DWS, SALGA and DBSA/DBE has been signed. This sets out the roles and responsibilities for the parties involved in the programme, including project implementation, funding arrangements, and the obligations regarding the WPO. It includes a term of reference for the OCE. DBSA facilitated this contracting process and will act as secretariat of the OC to facilitate contract implementation until the WPO has been legally instituted/registered.

#### Water Partnership Office Implementation Plan, including budget

The WPO PIP will be developed by the WPO annually and submitted to the OC for approval. Once approved, the PIP will be submitted to the DBSA for review and approval. The PIP will include a detailed programme implementation, including NWPP and WRF development and water reuse sub-project preparation, as well as the capacity requirements and how they will be met. It will include the budgeting of the various units, differentiating between programme budgets (i.e., each sub-programme will be ring fenced with its own costing and funding structure).

#### Water Reuse Unit Implementation Plan, including budget

The WRF implementation plan will be developed by the WRU and submitted to the WPO for consideration and approval. The PIP will include a detailed project schedule and capacity budget requirements for the WRF. The WPO will include this detail in the WPO’s PIP.

#### Project Owner agreements with WPO

The WRF project owners are currently the municipalities as water services authorities for their areas of jurisdiction. They will continue to engage the WRU for support through the WPO and for specific water reuse support through the WRU. Engagement will be through formal written correspondence including on-

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 123 of G

## Service provider service level agreements

The WPO, through the DBSA, will procure two key service providers: namely the panel of service providers to provide project preparation services and the service providers who will be competitively procured and contracted by the DBSA to address the specific performance requirements of the WRP. The contracts will set out scope of anticipated work and remuneration schedules. Service providers will only be engaged and paid when there is specific work for them to perform, and they will not be required to provide services that are not specifically allocated to them.

## Project Funding Agreements

Project funding agreements will be concluded between the project owners and the lenders. The funding agreements will be formal agreements between the project owners and the lenders. The structure of the document will depend on the specific lending arrangements. The WPO and the DBSF will facilitate the process of arranging and securing project funding.

## Advisory Terms of Engagement

The OC will identify and invite members to participate in the Advisory Committee. The OC will develop and issue a terms of engagement document with engagement requirements and roles and responsibilities.

Sub-projects will be owned and implemented by the Project Owners. Sub-projects can be procured in several ways. They can be procured following traditional procurement strategies, including compliance with supply chain management guidelines. Alternatively, the Project Owner may choose to competitively procure separate service providers to design, contractors to build, and operators to operate the project. As a result of this decision, the WPO will not have any role in the project preparation phase or preparation before the procurement strategy is determined. It does however still require a proper project preparation process including scoping and feasibility. The decision as to which procurement strategy to follow will be made after completion of the project preparation stages (see Figure 28). The procurement strategy followed will inform the contracting structures.

The WPO will adopt DBSA's accounting systems and will be audited independently (auditors selected through a competitive bidding process where Terms of Reference are approved by the DBSA Finance Unit) on a yearly basis.

## The study's output in The Green Book, one of South Africa’s most highly regarded and well-respected sources of climate risk analysis, was used to identify the municipalities with the highest vulnerability scores. This information was then used as a basis for additional analysis and numerical risk score estimations. The Green Book is especially apt as a climate information source for South African backdrops.

### 9 downscaled climate projection scenarios for the country from 1 km x 4 km grid, providing high spatial resolution and reflecting the most recent climate model projections available at the time of writing.
- It integrated municipality-specific data on vulnerability, profiling each individual municipality in terms of demographic and socio-economic vulnerability factors.
- It was developed primarily to assist municipalities with evaluating their climate change risk – and the methodology was considered appropriate for use by other stakeholders as well.
- It provides readily available data and information on specific climate-related hazards and vulnerabilities at the appropriate scale for this analysis.
- It facilitated additional analysis to convert the primary climate variables into critical hazard levels for several climate hazards including drought, and
- The ability of the underlying climate models to realistically simulate present-day southern African climates has been extensively demonstrated through a plethora of peer-reviewed publications.


---
**Page 130**
---

The regional climate model used by the Green Book is the conformal-cubic atmospheric model (CCAM), a variable resolution general circulation model (GRCM) developed at the Canadian Centre for Climate Modelling and Analysis (CCCma). CCAM runs coupled to a dynamic land surface model CASE (Climatic Atmosphere Biosphere Land Exchange model).

For the Green Book, six GCM simulations of the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase Five (CMIP5) and Assessment Report Five (AR5) were used. These are: HadGEM2-ES (Hadley Centre for Climate Prediction and Research), MIROC5 (Japan Meteorological Agency), MPI-ESM-LR (Max Planck Institute for Meteorology), NorESM1-M (Norwegian Earth System Model), RCP4.5 (IPCC 5th medium / medium emission scenario) and RCP8.5, or a low mitigation high emissions scenario.

The Green Book SCAM, downscaled from the Green Book suite includes the Australian Community Climate and Earth System Simulator (ACCESS1-0), the Geophysical Fluid Dynamics Laboratory Coupled Model (GFDL-CM3), the National Centre for Meteorological Research Copied Global Climate Model (NCCM2), the Canadian Earth System Model (CanESM2), the European Centre for Medium Range Weather Forecast Model (NorESM1) and the Community Climate System Model (CFSM) (Francis/Engelbrecht et al., 2015).

As noted in the Green Book's technical report, which clarifies the methodology:

- The simulations were performed on supercomputers of the Centre for High Performance Computing (CHPC) of the University of Cape Town. The model was run at a resolution of approximately 3 km over South Africa and 20 km globally.
- The model was initialized with the base-corrected SSTs and sea ice fields of the GCM. The bias is corrected by subtracting for each month the Reynolds (1984) climatological mean from the monthly mean of the Green Book simulation, and adding this difference to the corresponding field in the GCM.
- The model was run with a 30-day spin-up period followed by a 25-year integration. The first 5 years were used for equilibration, after which the model was forced using the same forcing as that of the Reynolds (1984) dataset. Hence, the initial variability and climate signal of the GCM are not included in the Green Book simulations.

A multiple-model strategy was followed to obtain the 3 km downscaled simulation. After completion of the 50 km simulation, a series of downscaling runs were performed using the same model configuration but with different resolutions: about 8 km (0.18°) degree in latitude and longitude. The high-resolution run of the model domain was about 2000 km x 3000 km. The downscaled simulations were performed by applying a digital filter over a 400-km length scale, followed by the application of a digital filter over a 80-km length scale. The latter was applied on six hourly intervals and from 900 to 1500 m above sea level.

On the matter of model validation (e.g., testing models against observed climate), no two GCMs are fully scientifically assessed because they all have different strengths and weaknesses. However, the Green Book simulations were validated for their ability to replicate current climate: The model's ability to realistically simulate present-day southern African climate has been extensively documented in a number of studies (e.g., Engelbrecht et al., 2015; Mabogo et al., 2016; Wittenberg et al., 2016, Englebrecht et al., 2018) (page 9).

A comprehensive validation study was conducted by the Green Book team to assess the performance of the model. The results can be found in a technical report on the impact of climate change on droughts (Barnes, A., 2016). This report is available at <https://www.greenclimatefund.org/sites/default/files/atoms/files/GreenBook_DroughtTechRep.pdf> (last accessed November 2021).

The Green Book project was funded by the Green Climate Fund under the National Water and Sanitation Masterplan. Volume 2: A plan of action Department of Water and Sanitation, Pretoria, South Africa.

In response to the Green Book project, the Department of Water and Sanitation (DWS) developed a National Water and Sanitation Masterplan Update under the Park Action Update (September 2021): Pretoria Department of Forestry, Fisheries and Environment.

We refer to the Project Preparation Facility proposal regarding the SA Water Reuse Programme (WRRP) as included in the concept note submitted by Development Bank of Southern Africa to us on 25 September 2019.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Department of Environmental Affairs, the National Designated Authority/focal point for South Africa.

Pursuant to the GCF Project Preparation Facility (PPF) decisions B.08/10 and B.13/21, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the Project Preparation Facility request as included in the PPF Proposal.

The government of South Africa has no objection to the Project Preparation Facility request as included in the Project Preparation Facility (PPF) Proposal; and

the PPF Proposal is in conformity with South Africa's national priorities, strategies and plans; and

in accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the PPF activities as included in the PPF Proposal are in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the PPF Proposal has been duly followed.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP209

## Proposal name: Climate Change Resilience through South Africa’s Water Reuse Programme (“WRP”)

### Accredited entity: Development Bank of Southern Africa

### Country/[ies]: South Africa

### Project/Programme size: Large

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. **The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:**

   - **Strengths**
     * The programme will support the structuring of blended finance options and the creation of a revenue stream in a water-scarce country that is ranked among the countries most vulnerable to climate change.
     * The aim is to transition South Africa’s approach on wastewater, from a predominantly public investment model to a private sector investment model.

   - **Points of caution**
     * GCF will be exposed to high credit risk due to its sensitivity in the loan structure. However, this is not unique to the GCF and the broader paradigm shift potential and high leverage of private sector financing.
     * This is also applicable for other development financial institutions because they are likely to follow a similar tranche with predetermined targeted risks.

   - **The introduction of a water reuse programme will create a dedicated asset for investing in climate adaptation measures, integrating climate risk assessment into planning with local water security agencies and providing key established capacity-building.**
     * This thereby contributes to a paradigm shift in South Africa’s Financial and Water sector towards the implementation of climate adaptation measures at scale and encouraging private sector investment.

   - **The project could demonstrate the benefits for future innovative financing structures through the WRP model, which could be expanded to water and sanitation services, off-grid sanitation services and rural/community water services.**

     * The proposal identified the first 10 locations with preliminary viability studies and analysis.
     * The project will be developed in phases, and it is critical to develop a robust implementation plan based on lessons learned from earlier phases to inform the subsequent phases.

     * This innovation comes with potential risks, however, potential delays (especially in the early stage) may be expected.


---
**Page 140**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

1. South Africa is a water-scarce country, and the sustainable provision of water is among its most pressing challenges. Despite water-related services, including the provision of potable water and access to sanitation and stormwater drainage, are not readily available to a significant proportion of the population, with more than three-quarters of municipalities having high to extremely vulnerability to water scarcity and the associated risks due to increasing growing population and urbanization.
2. The challenges facing water provision in the country are compounded by extreme weather events induced by climate change, especially the observed increased frequency and severity of droughts and floods over recent years. South Africa is ranked as one of most vulnerable countries in climate change and, in particular, its water sector is climate sensitive. Since the 1960s the average temperature of South Africa has risen by 1.5°C, and there is an observed increase in rainfall variability with more frequent extreme events.
3. To avoid the projected water deficit, the country needs to diversify its water supply sources and improve water use efficiency. This can be achieved through, among other things, the “re-use of effluent from wastewater treatment plants, water reclamation as well as desalination and treated rainwater harvesting.” The South African Water Sector Master Plan (2013) identifies South Africa in particular, this project is a high priority for South Africa to address water scarcity and transfer of technology. This project will be the first of its kind in South Africa that would attract private sector investment in the country, with potential for replication elsewhere.
4. The objective of the proposed programme is to establish and operationalize a national water reuse framework in South Africa, which will provide a conducive environment for South Africa and to attract private sector investment, paving the way for future growth by treating water reuse as an economic opportunity. This project aims at stimulating and activating the water reuse market. More specifically, the programme aims to:
   - Encourage the scaled development of water reuse projects at the municipal level;
   - Support municipalities in scaling up their existing water reuse projects by providing support for identifying, conceptualizing and prioritizing large scale projects during project preparation and in the development of blended finance options to fund implementation.
5. Assist municipalities to develop diversified projects that not only support water reuse but also benefit from co-benefits such as water reclamation or remediation through nature-based solutions and algae production, as well a energy generation from biogas.

This project will therefore address the following key barriers:
- Fragmentation and inconsistency across the sector; and often involving duplication of effort on water scarcity initiatives;
- The inability of municipalities to technically conceptualize and prepare water reuse projects.

## Limited municipal capacity to procure and structure contracts to support new business models

### Limited access to funding for adequate project preparation

### Lack of private sector engagement in the financing, development and ownership of climate-resilient interventions and

### Limited understanding of climate impacts on water security and the interventions required.

This programme will deliver outcomes related to each of the GCF investment criteria, as follows: (1) mitigation of climate change through expanding private investment in climate-friendly infrastructure; (2) providing a replicable model for building national-specific capacity to scale up climate-resilient investments; (3) contributing towards achieving the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs); (4) access to clean water, renewable energy generation and energy efficiency; (5) improving the terms of energy access and access to clean water, sustainable cities and communities, and taking climate action in a manner that is gender-responsive; (6) expanding national capacity related to climate-friendly and clean water infrastructure; (5) expanding national capacity to achieve established climate goals working through local stakeholders; and (6) increasing market efficiency by providing a replicable model for building national-specific capacity.

The indicative total budget value of the programme is estimated to be USD 1.42 billion, comprising USD 30 million in project preparation costs, USD 950 million in co-financing, USD 1.4 billion that provides blended financing solutions to actual project investments and USD 10 million dedicated for communications and awareness creation among the stakeholders of the programme and other relevant actors in South Africa.

Of this total, GCF is requested to provide USD 35 million in grants to share the cost of project preparation activities. The balance will be provided through concessional development finance loans to allow for a lower overall cost of financing on a project level, thereby leading to a more attractive co-financing package.

Co-financing will take four forms: (1) equity/budget allocation gains coming from participating municipalities that own and/or are mandated to run a particular wastewater treatment plant; (2) concessional loans from the World Bank; (3) concessional loans from Development Bank of Southern Africa (DBSA) and other development partners; and (4) blended financing solutions, including GCF co-financing, to attract a greater portion of senior debt and reduce the portion of subordinated debt.

The programme will only invest in projects that meet climate eligibility criteria with environmental safeguards. The project preparation phase was completed on 15 July 2021 and received GCF financial support through the Project Preparation Facility (completed on 14 July 2021, after 24 months including extension).

### Component-by-component analysis

The programme will shift the development pathway of South Africa's water sector by supporting implementation of three components: (component 1), creating innovative technologies and mobilizing finance at scale; (component 2), sharing knowledge of successful innovations at scale and developing climate expertise; (component 3). 

Component 1: Establishing a WRP project pipeline [Initial cost USD 6.2 million, GCF cost USD 30 million]

Component 1 will support the establishment of the WRP with clear governance arrangements. The component aims at establishing a programmatic approach to the


---
**Page 142**
---

development and implementation of water reuse infrastructure by supporting municipalities to identify, prepare and structure suitable water reuse projects. This component will establish, operationalize and resource a WRP that will provide an opportunity to support municipalities as primary project proponents in developing and implementing water reuse infrastructure projects that will underpin climate resilience interventions towards improved water security.

The component will be implemented through two inter-related components: Component 1 is the establishment of a national Water Partnerships Facility (WPF), which will support and facilitate a programmematic approach that will guide project identification and prioritization, drive project preparation; facilitate project implementation and ensure sustainability.

In addition, the financial arrangements and reporting regimes will be established under this component. The WPF will provide technical assistance to municipalities in preparing their project proposals for submission to the World Bank’s Green Climate Fund (GCF) for multi-year reporting that would underpin them. The programme will apply the financial management policies and guidelines for capital that are already used by the accredited entity (AE) through loans from the International Development Association (IDA). The WPF will be managed by the Capital Facilitator who would manage other capital in alignment with IDA processes. The financial management of the WPF will be supported by a dedicated Financial Management Unit.

The purpose is to ensure the continuous monitoring of all relevant covenants throughout the lifecycle of the project, including environmental and social safeguards.

Component 2: Implementing the WRP providing technical assistance for project procurement and developing the blended financing solutions (total cost USD 1.4 billion; GCF cost USD 200 million)

This component will provide technical and financial transaction advisory services during the development phase of projects to be supported by the WPF. The WPF will identify and support this through the deployment of blended finance solutions such as concessional loans, guarantees, etc., providing guarantees for green or de-carbonized sector investments.

This component is expected to support the implementation of the approach that will be used to enable blended financing through alternative leveraging solutions. It will identify and promote opportunities to attract financing through traditional methods (e.g., public-private partnerships) as well as innovative approaches (e.g., municipal bonds, public-private partnerships (PPPs), project bonds and impact investing). The WPF will also support the development of a green asset class for water reuse by using credit enhancement to crowd in private sector funding targeted at water reuse projects. This will be achieved through the creation of an asset class that will accept commercial returns but remain in line with environmental, social and governance impacts and help to meet the SDGs (specifically SDG 6 - safe and clean water and sanitation; SDG 7 – affordable and clean energy; SDG 12 – responsible consumption and production; and SDG 13 – combat climate change).

The WPF will support the blended finance approach through the employment of first loss/subordinated facilities, loan tenure extensions, or the employment of guarantees (which is an essential financial instrument to provide security and mobilize private sector funding). The WPF will also work in close collaboration together with the subsidized loans provided by IDA and other development/institutional financiers, which would create a more attractive and affordable project, which would make water reuse more attractive and affordable to water users.

A set of eligibility criteria has been developed and adopted to make sure that selected projects are technically sound, financially robust and bankable; and that the use of any concessional loan corresponds to the actual de-risking needs and not to disturb the market. The projects will be required to demonstrate a clear link with climate change, comply with technical standards (i.e., capacity, type of technology, environmental and social safeguards risk, chemical), and need to be financially viable with certainty around offtake

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

24 Overall, the proposal is well aligned with the six GCF investment criteria, particularly in its high paradigm shift potential, because the successful implementation of the project could change the water reuse sector of the country.

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 144**
---

29. The funding proposal has a medium-high impact potential with a solid and well-articulated climate narrative. South Africa is facing rising temperatures together with declining rainfall and a significant increase in spatial and temporal variability of rainfall. As a water scarce country, this will have severe consequences for the population. The WRF will benefit 3.42 million people in areas where the water supply is to be made more accessible and reliable. Another 10 million people are expected to benefit from the WRF once a successful demonstration of the water reuse concept that will lead to full scale up of the WRF.

30. There will also be mitigation or benefits from reduced greenhouse gas emissions by utilizing wastewater for irrigation, which is an important climate change adaptation measure. The cost-benefit analysis and financial viability assessment will be determined at a later stage when technical designs for each project are set during the implementation phase. The WRF will be evaluated as a second-tier mining criterion regarding such interventions in the projects. The WRF contributes to improving the water supply deficit and the chance of water restrictions in response to water shortages. The average annual avoided cost (hereafter referred to as ACC) per capita for South Africa is R1,052. The ACC in the absence of the WRF is over 3 million South African rand (ZAR) (approx. USD 81.1 million).

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

27. The establishment and successful implementation of this programme has a paradigm shift potential because it will pave the way for fully utilizing reused water in water-scarce municipalities, which is currently not happening due to lack of infrastructure and finance. This becomes a challenge driven by climate change, and where countries are seeking to address market barriers and financial constraints. The WRF model is designed as an innovative service curve, together with the dedicated technical assistance, serves as a one-stop-shop model that is designed to drive this transformational shift in this relatively new sector. This model is replicable across other sectors and can be used for implementation of the Paris climate goals, including treating water as a new asset class (i.e. it is an innovative mechanism to mobilize private sector investment in the most dominated of public invested sectors [i.e., the water sector]).

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

28. In addition to the considerable increased resilience of municipalities and populations to climate change, successful creation of this new asset class of sustainable water reuse projects will also have a significant impact on reducing pollution in rivers and groundwater because of the consequential reduction of discharge from wastewater treatment works as they will be redirected to water reuse plants. Improved quality water supply will also contribute towards improved food security for urban and rural farms. Incorporation of ecosystem-based approaches to sustain environmental flows would also be encouraged.

29. Enhanced water security would enable further expansion of economic activities in sectors with considerable water consumption, such as agriculture and industry, as well as other business opportunities that are currently constrained by the lack of reliable access to clean water. The proposal also has gender-benefit by engaging women, youth and marginalized groups in the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation phases of planning and implementation. Such engagement will ensure that the projects are sensitive to their needs and concerns. Improvements in water security will also benefit local economic activities that typically depend on access to clean water.

31. Finally, this project is supporting at least eight of the SDGs, as it that it involves access to clean water, renewable energy generation, economic growth, development of sustainable


---
**Page 145**

3.4 Needs of the recipient

The programme is expected to catalyse greater levels of private sector financing for water infrastructure projects in Jordan where the participation of the private sector has been very limited to date, and where the water sector is currently dominated by public finance. This is critical as the WRP aims to address climate change impacts on the Jordanian water sector. It is providing necessary financial capacity to meet social and economic needs related to climate-friendly and clean water infrastructure.

The interventions of this programme will also address the current inability of municipalities to technically conceptualise, prepare, structure and implement water projects. The introduction of a new approach for project procurement with pre-identification and pre-contract support by this project will help municipalities to develop the required capacity to drive paradigm shift in this sector and scale up the wastewater and water reuse projects.

3.5 Country ownership

The interventions proposed are well in line with the national climate policies and priorities of South Africa. Strengthening climate resilience of water security at the municipal level is one of the key objectives of the National Water Act (2018) which provides for a nationally determined contribution period. Water reuse is also consistent with South Africa’s National Development Plan 2030.

DBSA has significant experience with projects in South Africa as well as projects in climate finance. It has a strong track record in the management of programmatic risk and the effective implementation of large-scale infrastructure projects.

DBSA has engaged with a wide range of stakeholders in the preparation and development of this programme, including DWs, water utilities, private sector water developer and service providers, financial institutions (banks, insurance companies, project-based organizations), etc., and potential financiers. A comprehensive stakeholder plan is also proposed to ensure that all relevant parties are engaged throughout the life cycle of the WRP thereby creating the foundation for achieving stakeholder buy-in and effective implementation of the WRP. This will be critical in ensuring a high likelihood of success and climate impact by working through local stakeholders.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Without doubt, GCF’s concessionality and intervention in the WRP are crucial to the success of this WRP. GCF support will increase market efficiency through a leverage approach to scale up private investment to address the current municipal-level appetite and budget for a programme of this magnitude.

The GCF cost per beneficiary (public sector project) is USD 543 and the GCF cost per beneficiary (private sector project) is USD 1,086. The cost per beneficiary in Jordan is USD 413.00. The efficiency of the WRP is quite high, and these costs indicate that the WRP supports adaptation impact with more efficient use of GCF capital than other similar projects. For example, the Water Reuse Project (WRP) in Jordan building climate resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector [BRCG]) represented a GCF cost per beneficiary of USD 117.70. In addition,


---
**Page 146**
---

The economic internal rate of return for the WWP as a whole is 28 per cent, while the financial internal rate of return on the programme as a whole is around 26 per cent. The net present value for the programme as a whole, factoring in economic co-benefits, ranges from EUR 13 billion to EUR 40 billion (USD 9.817 billion to USD 21.2 billion), justifying the investment from an economic perspective.

GCF will provide concessional loans to both public sector and private sector project promoters (public or private) and will consider a range of loan terms, including different interest rates, whether the borrower is public or private). The project will be classified as either publicly or privately owned depending on which entity is the primary beneficiary. The financing project legal form (such as a PPP special purpose vehicle), in the case of privately owned projects, will be determined by the GCF and the project promoter.

The total amount of concessional loans provided under this facility will not exceed 10 per cent of the total cost of the project. The loan terms for concessional loans will be subject to two distinctly priced tranches of GCF concessional loans will be required, comprising 65 per cent public and 35 per cent private out of the total GCF concessional loan amount.

The programme is expected to be effective in overcoming key market barriers such as (1) limited access to finance for water reuse projects; (2) lack of awareness among stakeholders about the benefits of water security initiatives; (3) inability of municipalities to technically conceptualize and prepare project proposals that are financially viable, technically feasible, and commercially attractive; (4) limited access to funding for adequate project preparation and implementation; and (5) limited engagement in the financing, development, and ownership of climate resilience interventions.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Programme background: The programme will finance the development and implementation of a pipeline of wastewater reuse projects at an accelerated level as part of the WWP and scale up a pipeline of wastewater reuse projects to enhance water security and climate resilience at the local and regional scale in South Africa. The programme will also support notification, registration, and certification of wastewater reuse projects under the GEF environmental co-benefit of the programme is the improvement in the quality of rivers and groundwater resources through the reduction of pollution from industrial and municipal wastewater discharges from wastewater treatment plants. With improved water security and climate resilience, there are expected to be a range of positive social and economic benefits for local economic activities and job creation as the main economic and social co-benefits.

Environmental and social risk category: The TSP has assigned the programme medium environmental and social risks in accordance with the GEF environmental and social risk categorization and within the MFS SRS accreditation level. The proposed activities are expected to have potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts that are few in number.


---

generally site-specific, largely reversible, and can readily be addressed through good management practices and adaptive strategies during implementation. Focusing on water reuse infrastructure development, the programme will include financing the AES's category I (high and robust) and category II (moderate) projects. Projects in category I investments, and no dam or impoundment infrastructure will be considered eligible.

### Safeguard instruments

The AE has provided an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) for the project portfolio that includes a summary of the AES's ERS risk assessment; an analysis on the potential ERS risks and impacts likely to be associated with the programme; and a description of the safeguard instruments that will be applied. The ESMP also provides information on mitigation measures to address such risks and impacts with specifications of roles and responsibilities for implementation, monitoring, and reporting. This is followed by a due diligence process that requires all potential investments to be screened on the basis of a detailed environmental and social eligibility criteria and in compliance with national laws and regulations. The ESMP will also include an overview of the project portfolio and its components. All subprojects will undertake site-specific environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) that informs the development of a project-specific environmental and social management plan (ESSP), along with an environmental and social scoring report and a safeguard report. Subprojects will be required to develop detailed work plans to address identified challenges including labour conditions during construction, operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects. Subprojects will be required to develop detailed work plans to address identified challenges including labour conditions during construction, operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects.

### Compliance with GCF's environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards

The primary guiding document for ESS compliance is the ESMP. The following ESS standards will be applied:

#### ESS1: Assessment and management of environmental risks and impacts
The programme is categorised as medium risk against the AES’s ESS standards. Further to the environmental assessment, the ESIA will provide a detailed description of how the project portfolio mitigates the potential environmental and social impacts per programme component and respectively in terms of the work plan for each subproject. The ESMP provides an overview of the project portfolio including an initial ERS risk screening of the first ten subprojects in the current pipeline and establishes the AES’s ESS standards for the project portfolio. The ESMP will also include a description of how to perform subproject screening and site selection to be applied to the entire portfolio. All subprojects will be required to comply with the AES’s ESS standards, including the application of the AES’s ESS standards. The ESMP requires through ESIA and site-specific ESMP for each subproject, additional information on the ERS risks and impacts of associated facilities and third parties. This is because some of these facilities may have a significant environmental or social impact in their own right works/plants, and may be dependent on other infrastructural requirements, such as networks for drainage, water supply, power, etc., which are not part of the project portfolio. The ESMP details the ERS risk assessment related to the construction and operational phase for each archetype of subproject. The ESIA will provide a detailed description of how the project portfolio complies with the AES’s ESIA and ESSP and structure and content guidance regarding safeguarding instruments to be applied across all subprojects. Subprojects may have different levels of risk, which could be limited to supporting only water reuse, but may include other types of subprojects, such as waste reclamation, sludge management, biogas to energy, upgrading wastewater treatment processes, the Services and Infrastructure for Water Reuse (SIWR) project, etc. The ESMP will provide specific impacts in the ERS risk assessment on a case-by-case basis on all activities to be financed by the programme that may have an environmental or social impact.

#### ESS2: Labour and working conditions
The programme has identified risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects. Subprojects will be required to develop detailed work plans to address identified challenges including labour conditions during construction, operational phases of subprojects and has triggered risks regarding labour conditions during the construction and operational phases of subprojects. Recognizing the importance of employment creation and income generation in pursuit of poverty reduction and inclusive economic growth, this ESMP will


---
**Page 148**
---

exclude projects that involve harmful or exploitative forms of forced and/or child labour. All subprojects will be required to source their labour force locally and to include specific requirements in the procurement and contracting processes to promote fair treatment, non-discrimination, and equal opportunities for all workers, including vulnerable workers such as women, people with disabilities, migrant and contract workers; promote decent work principles and practices; ensure that no child is exposed to hazardous or child labour; and provide workers with accessible means to raise workplace concerns. The ESMF also requires the programme to comply with the national Occupational Health and Safety Act to protect workers from exposure to occupational health hazards. It further requires appropriate measures to those who may be exposed to the hazards arising out of or in connection with the activities.

### 3. Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The programme is expected to improve water use efficiency and contribute to the long-term water security in South Africa through the implementation of a range of projects that will reduce demand for water. The ESMF is triggered because potential waste/effluent streams are planned for treatment that will allow reuse and/or recycling, or where there is no suitable alternative. In all cases, this stage requires robust environmental management to prevent and control possible pollution incidents. Environmental management plans must be developed for the associated facilities which may contain complex chemicals that are of environmental and human health concern. The ESMF has noted that the standard for environmental management systems (EMS) applied in South Africa is ISO 14001:2015. This standard is applied to safeguard environmental integrity and human health, and this applies to emissions to air; discharges to water; greenhouse gas emissions; soil contamination; subproject-associated waste streams; and the disposal of hazardous materials. The ESMF has also noted that international best practices and standards. As part of the subject due diligence, the programme must ensure that all projects are in compliance with relevant legislation and to put in place specific mitigation measures. The ESMF also requires all subprojects to consider the social and environmental impacts associated with their activities and to comply with relevant policies and standards of environmental management. As part of the eligibility criteria, subproject activities which have a high potential for causing significant harm or damage that could harm the communities or impair the quality of the receiving environment, will be categorized as high risk and considered ineligible for the programme. The ESMF has noted management of localized pollution hotspots and/or areas of environmental concern as an area where knowledge benefit can be explored for each subproject. As part of the minimum requirements for decommissioning and remediation, the ESMF has noted that in order to ensure that as a result of the programme activities, the owner of the facility must comply with all relevant legislation and standards relating to the management of waste streams, including recycling and reuse, both for the infrastructure directly invested into by the programme and the facilities and processes of third parties associated with it. It is suggested that the Al provide specific guidance on this matter. The ESMF has also noted that in order to ensure proper environmental management of the wastewater discharged related to the assets invested

such as youth, ethnic groups and women, in terms of their basic needs (e.g. reducing income and food supply) or strategy needs (e.g. limiting agency and decision-making power). The ESF further requires due diligence to be conducted at the subproject level in order to: anticipate and avoid adverse impacts on human health and safety; ensure that all relevant environmental aspects are considered throughout the subproject lifecycle; promote quality and safety in the infrastructure design and construction; avoid or mitigate risks associated with climate change, natural disasters, floods, droughts, diseases and hazardous materials; put in place effective measures to address emergency events and avoid disasters; and ensure the safety of the programme personnel and property. ### 5.5 Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The programme expects to develop a safeguard policy on land acquisition and involuntary resettlement with the municipalities. Once an acquisition of land or the resettlement of communities is expected. Land acquisition and resettlement will only be considered as last resort measures. The ESF has stipulated in the ESMP include a stipulation that subprojects involving displacement or relocation of more than 50% of households must have a resettlement plan approved by the ESF before financing by the programme. The ESF also requires that the site selection of subprojects will not negatively impact on existing communities and their livelihoods.

The programme will also raise awareness and ensure transparency of land rights in consultations and engagements with communities. The AE has provisionally triggered its own safeguard on land acquisition and involuntary resettlement, which is currently under development. Further assessment will be conducted for every subproject, and detailed action plans are required where it is necessary to address the risks associated with land acquisition and/or a resettlement action plan to be developed at subproject level under such circumstances.

### 5.6 Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The programme will seek to apply ecosystem-based approaches in the design of water reuse subprojects. The programme will also require that all subprojects demonstrate positive impacts on biodiversity and ecosystem services over and above their direct project benefits, and that they contribute towards achieving the subproject co-benefit objectives with clear targets and indicators in order to align with energy-water-food nexus principles.

The programme will avoid any sites that have been set aside for conservation purposes, and the AE's related safeguard will be applied for sites selected for subprojects that may fall within critical biodiversity areas. The ESF has also stipulated a safeguard requirement on biodiversity where no reasonable alternative exists in terms of project design or location. The safeguard requirement is to demonstrate that there is no viable alternative within the region; no mitigation measures can be implemented, and the programme will not proceed with the subproject processes for which critical habitat was designated; no reduction in the global and/or national population of any species listed as endangered or vulnerable on a list maintained by a reasonable period of time and then a robust, appropriately designed, and long-term biodiversity monitoring plan is put in place. The ESF also requires that the programme will develop an action programme. Upon meeting all these requirements, the programme may support such a subproject with the relevant mitigation strategy encapsulated in a biodiversity action plan as required by the AE's safeguard policy on biodiversity.

The ESF has also stipulated a safeguard requirement where subprojects are likely to require rehabilitation or restoration of the site to be included in the subproject design. Where biodiversity is affected, the ESF will require that all adverse impacts associated with this activity be avoided or mitigated in any other way, the ESF will require these effects to be used as a last resort and develop action plans building the necessary management and mitigation measures needed

## GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESF 7: Indigenous Peoples

The AE has noted that impacts on Indigenous Peoples are our expected in the implementation of the projects and to that effect has included project exclusions on: projects that contravene the traditional rights of Indigenous Peoples; projects that have a high likelihood of causing significant harm that undermine Indigenous Peoples' community rights to land, natural resources, language and indigenous cultural heritage. Where there is a reasonable expectation that Indigenous Peoples may be present, the AE has included screening guidance in the ESMF and included provisions to guide the development of Indigenous Peoples places where required, including the need for free, prior and informed consent (FPIC). The Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group (IPAG) and Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group are available to provide advice to the AE and executing entities (EEs).

### 1. Cultural heritage

The programme will not support projects that involve displacing, uprooting or rendering inaccessible cultural heritage sites or features by applying appropriate safeguards in subproject screening. However, the associated pipelines of assets invested by the programme may have an indirect impact on tangible cultural heritage.

The AE has therefore provisionally triggered its relevant safeguard, which will be applied when a subproject is identified as having a high likelihood of causing significant harm due to changes in the physical environment; is located within a legally protected area or a legally defined buffer zone; is located near a site with significant cultural heritage value; and/or may impact on intangible cultural heritage. The AE has also provisionally triggered its relevant safeguard for commercial purposes: or may impact or depend on cultural heritage including human capital, natural capital or institutional capital. In such a case, the programme will deploy a heritage specialist to provide advice on how to address these issues in accordance with the relevant requirements in the subproject ESF. The ESF provides an indicative outline for a cultural heritage assessment at the project level and a detailed framework at the subproject level.

### 2. Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harm (SEAH) safeguarding

The ESMF has provisionally flagged SEAH risks at the programme level relating to the proposed archetypes, primarily during the construction and operation of subprojects. Local community members and vulnerable groups such as women, children, and people with disabilities, as well as workers (the male) construction workers are vulnerable to SEAH exacerbated by the traditionally male-dominated nature of the construction industry, which can lead to a lack of awareness in the workplace, as well as community health and safety. The ESF has incorporated SEAH-related questions into its screening process at both project and subproject levels. The ESF will also address gender-based violence and incidents of SEAH. As part of the subproject due diligence, the ESMF will require SEAH risks and impacts to be identified following a four-step approach: identification, assessment, mitigation, and monitoring and reporting in accordance with SEAH. The process will result in an SEAH management plan to be implemented and monitored by the EE. The AE has also provisionally flagged SEAH at the programme level as part of its risk management plan to guide development at the subproject level. The AE will also address SEAH risks through a gender-sensitive approach, including the incorporation of a prospective social assessment required at the subproject appraisal stage which incorporates SEAH safeguarding measures and guidance on how to address these issues. The ESF will include commitment, mitigation measures, training and awareness-raising, and mechanisms for SEAH grievance redress in a gender-sensitive and survivor-centred approach.

### 3. Indigenous Peoples' rights

The AE has also provisionally flagged Indigenous Peoples' rights at the programme level and responsibilities among the AE, programme clients, interested and affected parties, national authority, and regional and local authorities. The ESF will provide guidance on how to address these issues by providing a framework and process within which site-specific ESIA and ESMPs will be prepared at the subproject level; the programme will establish a WPO to support targeted municipalities that are proposed as Indigenous Peoples places where required.

## Grievance redress mechanism (GEM)

Having triggered the AF’s own standard on grievance redress, the programme will establish an independent GEM to resolve all complaints arising from the implementation of the project and/or any other related issues or complaints/grievances from aggrieved parties. The GEM specifies criteria for handling complaints/grievances and provides a process for their resolution. The complexity and scale of the complaint/grievance will determine the nature of the redress mechanism required to be proportionate to the nature and scale of the potential subproject risks and impacts. The GEM will be independent from the project management, incorporating the independent GEM and to work with other redress mechanisms, such as public participation committees and grievance resolution mechanisms (e.g., a dedicated Help Desk and a suggestion box located at central offices). The subprojects GEMs will be required to follow the same approach as the independent GEM incorporating procedures to ensure SEAM incidents are reported and resolved in an effective manner. The programme will encourage the use of local or subproject level GEM, recognizing that the local GEM can provide a more efficient and cost-effective mechanism for resolving grievances while also making sure that stakeholders are informed about the GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism (IGRM) and how to access it. In addition, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy will be available as part of the Indigenous Peoples Policy. The GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### Gender Policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore met the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment submitted by the AE provided an analysis of the status of gender equality and the empowerment of women in South Africa, and as it relates to the various themes in the environmental sector - water and sanitation, climate change adaptation, and disaster risk reduction and recovery programme. The gender assessment involved secondary review of data including internal and external sources, a desk review of relevant policies, strategies, guidelines, action plans, approaches and guidelines, good practices on gender mainstreaming, and the national gender policy document. A survey was also conducted to gather information from stakeholders who were also contacted to inform the gender assessment, albeit the coronavirus 2019 pandemic contributed to some delays in this process.

The 1994 Constitution of South Africa guarantees equal rights and opportunities for women and men, and the right to sanitation is enshrined in Section 24 of the Constitution, guaranteeing access to water and sanitation as a basic human right. This provision also supports well-being, as well as a right to have the environment protected. Section 9 of the same Constitution provides that everyone has the right to freedom and security of person without arbitrary arrest or detention. The country has a national strategy on gender mainstreaming in the environmental sector which is aligned with the GCF Gender Policy. The National Water Act, 1998 (Act No.36 of 1998) provides for the creation of inclusive policies, and overall provides strategic guidance in the environmental sector to ensure that all people have access to water and sanitation services. This provision prohibits direct or indirect discrimination based on several grounds, including gender and/or sex. The country has a national strategy on gender mainstreaming in the environmental sector which is aligned with the GCF Gender Policy.

The country has been demonstrating progress towards achieving gender equality and the empowerment of women, such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol) and the Southern African Development Community, have been documented. However, there are still significant gender disparities in access to water services, particularly for women and girls because water insecurity is rapidly growing in South Africa especially in urban areas (women, girls and poor communities). The country has a high population growth rate which increases demand increases. There are significant gender and social inclusion dimensions of water access, use and control. Climate change worsens existing vulnerabilities and adds to the pressures on the environment and natural resources. Gender equality is also affected by climate change as women in South Africa have less decision-making power that affects their ability to respond to the effects of climate change.

Meeting WASH priorities and needs requires recognition of gender barriers and development of strategies, policies and programmes that address these barriers and socioeconomic co-benefits and ensures adequate empowerment of marginalized individuals and communities. The gender assessment provided an analysis of the status of gender equality and the empowerment of women in South Africa, as it relates to the various themes in the environmental sector - water and sanitation, climate change adaptation, and disaster risk reduction and recovery programme projects and more specifically in water reuse initiatives and programmes. The gender assessment also identified gaps and opportunities for action at national level and across sectors to drive progress on gender equality and empowerment of women, collaboration between infrastructure service delivery and public works programmes, water and gender agenda integration into the broader development agenda, and gender mainstreaming within urban development.

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

This is an adaptation programme investing in water reuse infrastructure in South Africa.

The project will adopt a hybrid finance structure that enables investors to participate in individual water reuse projects.

## GCF requested to provide a subordinated loan of USD 200 million and a grant of USD 35 million. The DBSA and/or other development finance institution (DFI) partners will co-finance a total of USD 150 million in subordinate debt, and participating municipalities and the country’s financial institutions will contribute equity.

### 6.3.2 Accredited entity/existing entity capable to execute the current programme (low risk)

* DBSA (rated Baa by Moody’s) is the AfD and FIs for the programme.
It will work closely with DWS, which is a national entity and fully owned by the Government of South Africa. DBSA has a track record in investing in public and private sector projects in the country.

### 6.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (high risk)

* Credit risk: GCF will assume the credit risk of the borrowers under the blended financing structure (underwriting). The GCF loan will be subordinated to other senior debts and rank pari passu with other debt instruments issued by public sector entities (municipalities, utilities, water user associations) and private sector borrowers that benefit from the project will be subject to similar credit risk. Given the high level of exposure of DBSA and its subsidiaries to the financial industry’s low exposure and a high risk perception of the water reuse sector, the creditworthiness of the GCF loan is considered to be at a higher risk than other loans. It is unknown or they have limited track records. The comfort can be derived from the fact that the GCF commitment will be in the form of a grant and will not exceed USD 30 million per subproject, which is lower.
The AfD’s due diligence on the borrowers and its capacity to identify and finance pipeline projects will be critical to the success of the WRP.

* Grant risk: The impact risks are the AfD need to mobilize and facilitate the following co-financing amounts:
    * Senior debt of USD 650 million;
    * Equity of USD 200 million;
    * Subordinated debt of USD 150 million;
    * Grant/budget allocation of USD 150 million; and
    * Guarantee of USD 50 million.

* The GCF loan takes a junior position along with other DFIs and/or DBSA which would help to mitigate the risk in terms of senior position. The guarantee will also provide additional comfort for commercial investors. A low appetite for financing such projects from other private sector entities is expected, given their limited experience in water reuse projects, which will lead to the impact risk; the term sheet includes: (1) the co-financing ratio of subordinate loans being at least 50% and not exceeding 75%; (2) a minimum equity ratio at the programme level; (3) confirmation from a co-finance to finance the guarantee; (4) confirmation by municipalities for their budget allocation or confirmation of grant financing; and (5) a minimum equity ratio as part of the eligibility criteria of the subloans.

71. Foreign exchange risk: The AE will convert the GCF loans in United States dollars into South African rand and provide that to the sub-borrowers. The AE will enter into an appropriate hedging mechanism to cover the tenor of the loan. Depreciation of the local currency may adversely affect the ability of the borrower to repay its debt. To mitigate this risk, the AE may manage this risk during the first 9 years; the GCF loan amount includes a reserve of up to 5 per cent of the total loan amount. If the reserve is insufficient to cover a resulting foreign exchange shortfall (i.e., a shortfall in USD which occurs from a payment default not prepaid by the borrowers). After year 10, if the available reserve amount is more than zero, the remaining balance will be managed by the AE. ## Conclusion

Although DBSA has acknowledged the importance of due diligence on co-financers, DBSA is unable to produce such assessments at this stage of the project. DBSA has, however, indicated that due diligence will be undertaken and provided to GCF in a timely manner as per the GCF policy.

Therefore, as a prerequisite to the second disbursement, DBSA has agreed to a condition precedent that the co-financer must provide an updated due diligence report in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, confirming that AML/CFT due diligence has been undertaken on the co-financiers, and that no such co-financier is subject to financial sanctions or restrictions by UNODC.

### 4.3.5 Risk rating

The Secretary has conducted a review of the project in accordance with relevant GCF Board-approved policies and does not find any material issue or deviation with respect to compliance with the required due diligence information for this funding proposal, the Secretariat has determined a risk rating of "medium".

#### 4.3.6 GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area and single proposal is not material.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

DBSA will play the dual role of AE and EE, as DBSA will ensure monitoring and reporting as required by GCF and will offer programme management support and provide backroom services.

DBSA will coordinate the work of third-party auditors engaged in the programme to monitor and evaluate results. During programme implementation DBSA will ensure that any individual or entity involved in the project is aware of, and complies with, the terms and conditions of the funded activity agreement and/or the terms and conditions of the funding agreement as applicable.

DWS will contract with DBSA, in its capacity as AE/GF and EE, to establish and support implementation of the governance arrangements to give effect to the National Water Resource Strategy. The governance arrangements are based on those established by DWS and DBSA and at an operational level, DWS and DBSA will establish a programme management arrangement.

The proposed blended finance solution is designed to finance water reuse projects in a way that optimizes the use of climate finance, and enhances the use of private capital, thereby


---
**Page 159**
---

taking historical infrastructure to market. Applying a developed and agreed upon suite of blended capital principles for the WRP, an independent Blended Capital Facilitator will act as a specialist service provider in the WPO, working alongside the Water Reuse Unit and the Project Preparation Facility (PPF) to provide technical assistance and financial support for water reuse infrastructure projects as approved by the Water Reuse Unit and WPO.

The financial management between the AE and EE of the WRP will be governed by the FRS framework. The GCF will provide a grant to cover the costs associated with this arrangement. DBSA will participate in the oversight committee as the implementing agent of the National Water Fund (NWF) until such time that the NWF is legally institutionalized. DBSA will provide all administrative services such as procurement of goods and services through DBSA supply chain management processes, procurement of professional services, payment of contractors, suppliers, consultants, advisors, and employees. It will also arrange a ring-fenced bank account for the WPO function and make payments to it.

The WPO will adopt DBSA's accounting systems and will be audited independently on a yearly basis.

Given that financing is expected to be in the local currency while GCF extends the facility in USD, an AIF will arrange a hedge. In the event that the AE would need to unwind a hedge (typically after 5 years), it may require up to USD 10 million as seed for the first 9 years to meet residual foreign exchange shortfall.

### Results monitoring and reporting

This is an adaptation project focusing on strengthening socioeconomic resilience at the community, regional and national level through improved water security by implementing water reuse projects through innovative public-private Mixed financing arrangements. The project will be implemented in four phases over a period of five years (2018-2023). 35,42 million are direct beneficiaries and 3,07 million are indirect beneficiaries. The economic co-benefits include the creation of 69,000 jobs and the reduction of pollution into local water resources, as reported under the corresponding co-benefit indicators in the financial planning document.

The theory of change adequately illustrates how the results chain will cascade from the goal statement to the project activities and clearly articulates the relevant interlinkages between the inputs, outputs, outcomes and impacts. The logic model has been formulated, and the proposed output and outcomes are defined in a manner that adequately supports moving towards the desired impact.

The logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including the inclusion of solid means of verification, and selecting the appropriate core and supplementary indicators for the chosen logic model. The indicators have been validated by the secretariat.

The secretariat has identified the two-fold high-level risks for this programme, which are:

- The WRP being an actual vehicle to process projects and finance, has not yet been established, and GCF funds will be dedicated for setting it up; and
- There are projects ready in the pipeline, and pipeline generation depends on the WRP being established. Therefore, there is a risk that no projects will be processed.

Therefore, the secretariat has proposed the following two conditions and agreed with the AI in the term sheet accordingly.:
